## D: D & B–DIFM

### D & B
 
 <i>abbr.</i> ditch and bank.

>  <b>J.G.WILSON</b> I believe this would be the cheapest fence of all ...; a Ditch and Bank in a mile covers exactlyan acre of ground, but the D. & B. is an excellent drain ... when the ground ... is apt to be wet ... D. and B. is the best fence ... <i>(May 2, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May 2 Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 187]

### D & D
 
 <i>abbr.</i> drunk and disorderly

>  <b>CRANNA</b> `Drunk and disorderly, eh, Jim?' ... Tom scowled. `Haven't been D and D for years, smart-arse.' <i>(1989,Visitors,18</i>)

### D Block
 
 <i>n.</i> security block in Paremoremo prison

>  Nor was D Block reserved for men convicted of extremely violent, shocking offences. Because every block had total security status, this measure was unnecessary. Instead, D Block housed Paremoremo's most disruptive or revolutionary elements. <i>(May 12, 1985,NZ Times,4</i>)

>  The longest-serving survivor ofParemoremo's D Block, the only man toever briefly escape from this electronicfortress. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### D-Day Dodger
 
 <i>n.</i> [(WW2)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He is really geting on my nerves as he natters on and on, a lot of nonsense about getting out of B Ech and back into the sharp end with the boys. Plenty of room at the other end of the paddock. A bunch of D-Day Dodgers and if they're going to hang around in their own half ... they might as well ... have a brew-up. <i>(1970,On the Ball,136</i>) [NOTES: Transf. to rugby union.]

### D-up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  We had to D up and get our hands on the ball. <i>(Apr 13, 1997,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  You have to 'D' up for 80 minutes and we didn't do that. <i>(Sep 3, 2001,Dominion,26</i>)

### D.C. Day
 
 <i>n.</i> Decimal Currency Day

>  Monday the 10th of July 1967 is a significant date in New Zealand history, as this was the day New Zealand officially changed from GBP. s. d., to a decimal currency. [42] In the build-up to 'D.C.Day' as it was called, the Government ran an extensive publicity campaign to prepare, inform, educate and reassure the public. <i>(Jun 2003,NZ Memories,No. 42/41</i>)

### dab
 
 <i>n.</i> sand flounder (formerly any flounder)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> All the coves of this bay teem with fish of various kinds, such as cuttle-fish, large breams, ... small grey breams, small and large barracootas, flying gurnards, horse-mackarel, dog-fish, soles, dabs, mullets, drums, scorpenas or rock-fish, cole-fish, the beautiful fish called chimera, and shags. <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,114</i>)

>  `The position is scandalous,' said Mr.Coltman, J.P., at the Auckland Magistrate's Court a few days ago, when a fish inspector candidly confessed that he did not know the difference between a dab and a flounder, adding that he did not pretend to know anything about fish, ... <i>(Jul 30, 1910,NZ Truth,266/7/2</i>)

>  <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i>: Dab or `sand flounder.' <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  NEW ZEALAND SAND-FLOUNDER (PATIKI) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (Richardson) ... The common Sand-flounder is known from one end of New Zealand to the other, sometimes by other names in different localities. In Auckland it is known as the Dab. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,191,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER ... Other names: <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i>; diamond, tinplate, dab, square; patiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,216</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER [<i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i>] ... Other names are dab, diamond, tinplate, and square. The Maori name patiki, originally generic, is now loosely used for most flounders. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> FLOUNDER (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia(ii) Rhombosolea retiaria (iii) Rhombosolea tapirina (iv) Rhombosolea leporina.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southernwaters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLOUNDER, SAND ... Other names are `dab', tinplate, square flounder. The Maori name `patiki' is used for all flounder except the sole which is the `patiki rori'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,71</i>)

>  Many people claim that the flesh of the sand flounder is the best and sweetest of all flatfish ... Otherwise known as the diamond or dab flounder, it is easily distinguished by its diamond shaped body ... <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> SAND FLOUNDER (Diamond, dab, patiki) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> ... The sand flounder ... can easily be distinguished from [the yellow-belly] and all other flounders by its distinctive diamond shaped body. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,309,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> A dairy [at Thames] was selling hot pies and fish bait, a fishshop was selling dabs (baby flounder) and crab sticks ... and pots of ex-Chatham Islands kina roe. <i>(1985,G'day Country,56</i>)

>  [species name] sand flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> .. [other name(s)] dab, diamond back, square, tin-plate, three corners <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Sand flounder Rhombosolea plebeia</i> ... Often called dab, sometimes diamond or square. Restricted to New Zealand. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,143</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to daub with clay (See [wattle and daub](../W#wattle-and-daub))

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> And our cookhouse was finished and `dabbed' to completion before the mansion was finished, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/5/279</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a short darting run. [(rugby)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> An audacious dab by Mill surprised Southland and Stringfellow cut past ... to score halfway out. <i>(1970,On the Ball,110</i>)

>  From the ruck which followed, G.L.Colling worked the blind side and T.Toleafua made a dab. He passed the ball inside to A.J.Clark and he brushed off two attempted tackles before scoring in the third ... <i>(Aug 19, 1971,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  The three meanings given for `dab' do not include the short darting run made by a rugby player; ... <i>(Mar 28, 1979,Otago Daily Times,21</i>) [NOTES: (G. Griffiths)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a try, attempt

### dabchick
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Podiceps</i>, a small grebe found mainly in the NI; see also diver, grebe, totokipio, weweia. totokipio.

>  A small dab-chick (<i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>) is common in our freshwater lagoons; and a large crested grebe, hitherto undescribed, inhabits the lakes of the South. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/19</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS, Gray. Totokipio. Dab-chick, Little Grebe. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/73</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS. Gray. Dab-chick. Totokipio. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,39</i>)

>  <i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>. - Totokipio, Dabchick. Both Islands. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS, <i>Gray</i>. - Dab-chick. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/127</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  .. the divers are represented by the almost cosmopolitan crested grebe and the dabchick. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,197</i>)

>  Wild duck and dab-chicks swum about the lake [south of Kaikohe]. <i>(Jun 10, 1920,Quick March,39</i>)

>  The little grebe (or dabchick) was not uncommon in the quiet reaches of the river or deeper lagoons. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/279</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <i>Poliocephalus rufopectus</i> Gray. Little Grebe; Dabchick. This species probably eludes observation in many localities where it is not uncommon. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,VI/81</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> DABCHICK <i>Podiceps rufopectus Local names</i>: Diver, Weweia. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Australian Little Grebe Tachybaptus novaehollandiae</i> (STEPHENS, 1826). NATIVE OTHER NAMES: <i>Black-throated grebe, Australasian little grebe, Australian dabchick. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,56</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Hoary-headed Grebe Podiceps poliocephalus</i> JARDINE & SELBY, 1827 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Hoary-headed dabchick, Tom Pudding</i>. ... Breeds mainly in southern Australia and Tasmania. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,56</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>

>  Almost certainly, no breeding populations of southern crested grebes remain in the north, although New Zealand's two other grebe species are found there: the New Zealand dabchick and the Australasian little grebe. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 New Zealand's only endemic species of grebe, the New Zealand dabchick or weweia.., a small and threatened species found in the North Island, shows the same peculiar structure possessed by all grebes: webbing is absent and the toes are expanded into broad lobes. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/82</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS. (NEW-ZEALAND DABCHICK.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Weweia, Totokipio [, Taihoropi (Hokianga), and Taratimoho (Waikato)]. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,350,[1888 2nd edn II/280] ed.</i>)

>  <i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>, Gray. (The New Zealand Dabchick.) <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/83</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <i>Podiceps rufipectus</i> Gray, 1843 <i>New Zealand Dabchick (Weweia) </i>North Island <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,18,1980 ed.</i>)

>  New Zealand Dabchick <i>Podiceps rufipectus</i> GRAY, 1843 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Weweia.</i> ... Once the New Zealand dabchick bred in the South Island; now the species breeds only in the North Island. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,54</i>)

### dack
 
 <i>n.</i> a silly person. [1984 16 M E91 Pakuranga Coll. 1 DACK {a silly person] [M2]]

### dactylanthus
 
 <i>n.</i> wood rose

>  Pureora's endangerd <i>dactylanthus</i> population has a rare claim to fame - it is one of the few sites where another endangered endemic species, the short tailed bat, is known to assist with pollination of the plant. .. <i>Dactylanthus</i> is New Zealand's only completely parasitic plant and grows at several sites in Pureora Forest Park. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,p24</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> It was a small twig, of reddish wood, with three delicately shaped wooden flowers clustered towards the end. They were hard, beautifully fashioned, securely fastened to the stem. `No, they won't come off,' said Les. `That's dactylanthus; Dactylanthus Taylori. It's a parasite that gets into the roots of various native trees, usually five finger or lancewood.' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,57</i>)

>  &#65279;Ecroyd recently succeeded in germinatingdactylanthus seeds. <i>(Jun 1992,North & South,18</i>)

>  &#65279;Richard Taylor, early missionary and thefirst European to describe dactylanthusand send samples to England, reportedhearing about blue flowers seen inTaranaki, but Ecroyd suspects these mayhave been fungi. <i>(Jun 1992,North & South,18</i>)

>  I notice Mr H. Hill has been collecting a rare plant discovered by him a few years ago, etc. The plant would be Dactylanthus Taylori, order Balanophoreae. <i>(Jan 17, 1908,Colonist (Nelson),2</i>)

>  The method by which Dactylanthus Taylori (as this plant is known scientifically) obtains food from its host, is most curious and interesting. . . <i>(Nov 13, 1926,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <i>"Wooden flowers," which are the roots of a host plant moulded into these pansy-like shapes by the absorbent action of the Dactylanthus rhyzome as it grows adherent to the host root.</i> <i>(Apr 20, 1937,Evening Post,16</i>) [NOTES: italics correct]

>  .. and Mr Hamilton drew attention to a specimen of an extrememly rare plant - the <i>Dactylanthus taylori</i> - found in the North Island. <i>(Jun 10, 1891,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

>  We have just seen a collection of plants which is perhaps the rarest ever gather in New Zealand. They are known botanically as <i>Dactylanthus Taylori</i>. The native name is pua reinga. <i>(Jul 5, 1895,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Excellent specimens of a beautiful and rather rare parasite, known to the botanical world as dactylanthus Taylorii [sic] and colloquially as woodflowers, were found by Mr. H. J. Frost when excavating at a house site in New Plymouth. <i>(Jun 27, 1933,Evening Post,6</i>)

### dad-pop
 
 <i>n.</i> popular music of a recently past era

>  Once [Cliff] Richard hit number one with the saccharine "Livin' Doll" he changed course and began producing the decent, somewhat dull dad-pop we associate with him today. <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,8</i>)

### daffodil
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the Air Force [(WW2 army sl.) ca1941 (Professor R. Clarke) `beautiful but yellow'.]

### Daffodil Hill
 
 <i>n.</i> = Bludgers' Hill. [(WW2)]

>  <b>HELM</b> The hill that has since become famous in the 2NZEF, Bludgers' Hill, or Daffodil Hill (beautiful but yellow) as it is now known. <i>(1944,Fights and Furloughs,8</i>)

### dag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTCHINGS</b> And then the dag bags had to be manhandled to the dag crusher down by the pond. Bill Schmidt was in charge of the dag crusher. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,80</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>MUSGRAVE</b> He laughed contemptuously at the curious expletive `Dag!' <i>(1917,Myola,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[Baker Australian Language 1942 suggests derivation (by confusion) with dab, be a dab at; see W3 dags; EDD reference at n1. might apply here; also OED2 sb&sup5..], to be extremely good at.]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> dag at, be a. To be extremely good at: from the middle 1890's: Australians'; hence, by 1920, New Zealanders'. Ex. [dag `hard case']. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,204</i>)

>  `To be a dag at,' `to put across a beaut,' `to jerry to,' ... need no explanation. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  ... with reference to cleaning or dagging crossbred sheep. <i>(1878,NZ Country Journal,(1877) II/400</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Old Pete's a real scrounger, a bit of a dag. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  Old Pete's a real scrounger, a bit of a dag. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> I heard a lady up the road ... saying that they all thought Dad was a pommy holy roller and it [the fire] was all a bit of a dag, a way of getting rid of him.' <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,95,1988 ed.</i>)

>  `She's [i.e.co-host Kerry Smith] a bit of a dag, whereas I'm a pretty straight person,' says Dougal Stevenson. <i>(Aug 24, 1988,Press, The,18</i>)

>  `By the time I'd been there a year or two I was counted all right and a bit of a dag. ...' <i>(1948,Canta</i>) [NOTES: 1948 July Canta in 1990 McKay James K. Baxter 58 In Canta, July 1948, he wrote of King's:]

>  <b>HALL</b> I said `What did you do that for?' and he said `Just wanted to see what it was like to score a goal. Bit of a dag, eh Sir?' <i>(1977,Middle-Age Spread,44</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Someone considered "a dag", "a bit of a dag", "a rough diamond" could get away with a lot. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,148</i>)

>  Land Rovers inside shops, meals being cooked on shovels over a fire, rustic barbecues on the street and among some of the finest delicacies to tempt your tastebuds during this "bit of a dag day" will be cowpat burgers, hot dag dishes, and real rustic-sized meals. <i>(Apr 5, 1999,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Williams as-near-as-dammit qualifies for that great New Zealand accolade "a bit of a dag". <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to remove dags (from a sheep's hindquarters). [(farmers')]

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Dagging the hoggets, or drafting the rams. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  Daging and doseing 530 hoggets. <i>(Jun 8, 1894,Chudleigh Diary,386,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Mount Watkins Remit No. 3: `That an allowance in the case of daggy sheep be arranged for,' was dealt with; ... it was decided: `That all members refuse to dagg [sic] sheep during shearing except the dagging rate be added to the shearing rate, as we are of the opinion that dagging is not shearing. <i>(1912,NZ Shearers (Report Third Annual Conference),15</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Three or four weeks before lambing time the ewes should be dagged, and if crutching is followed, any wool ... may be clipped in a careful manner. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,68</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The ewes should also be dagged and the tails trimmed before turning the rams in. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 It is advisable to dag all dirty sheep before shearing, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,73</i>)

>  Here most farmers content themselves with dagging their sheep before sending them to the shearing shed; but there are not a few who neglect even this elementary practice. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,707</i>)

>  Dagging sheep before they enter the shed is sound practice. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,310</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The next point, ... [12], relates to `daggy' sheep. Sheep should be dagged before they enter the woolshed, ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,11</i>)

>  They are brought to the sheep yards, where any dirty animals are `dagged' (any stained wool and pieces of dung hanging around the tail are clipped off). They are then `penned' overnight in the woolshed to protect them from rain or heavy dew. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,66</i>)

>  dag NZ & Austral. 1 n. (usu. in pl.) lock of wool clotted with dung on hinder parts of sheep; sl. funny person, a character (he's a bit of a dag). 2 v.t (-gg-) remove dags from (sheep). 3 dagger 2 person who removes dags from sheep. [Eng. dial.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,185</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 280, DAG & DAGLOCK; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 120]]

>  Other types of fleece which may be dealt with in the course of a rouseabout's day are: Dags (dirt-clogged wool) if the farmer hasn't bothered to dag before shearing; shed stain, which is good body wool spoilt when one sheep empties bowels on another while penned up closely(avoided by keeping sheep off grass for at least six hours prior); ... <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> In this particular week the men had cut firewood, dagged rams.. and repaired the orchard fence. <i>(1986,Haliburtons of Glenfarg,1</i>)

>  We're told to get our stock ready for a Sunday so we dag them, yard them. <i>(Jan 2, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),00</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

>  ".. But also, I had a balance to my life; after advising people about money I could go home and dag sheep." <i>(Apr 7, 2005,Dominion Post,C8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A clot of matted wool and excrement hanging from (or cut from) the hindquarters of a sheep. Such daggy wool removed from a sheep; see also sheep-dag.

>  Land Rovers inside shops, meals being cooked on shovels over a fire, rustic barbecues on the street and among some of the finest delicacies to tempt your tastebuds during this "bit of a dag day" will be cowpat burgers, hot dag dishes, and real rustic-sized meals. <i>(Apr 5, 1999,Dominion,10</i>)

>  The employer was certainly standing in his own light, as dirt on the board was bad for the fleece, and sheep did not do well with daggs [sic] on. <i>(1912,NZ Shearers (Report Third Annual Conference)</i>)

>  Dag: Hard or soft dung hanging from the breech of a sheep. Cutting this off the sheep with an old pair of shears is called dagging. The ds. are afterwards gone through by ad.-picker or d.-boy, who cuts out any wool worth saving. (C) [c1890-1910].) <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Tramping on hard dags with moccasins can give a shearer a bad stone bruise. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,12</i>)

>  DAGS: Soiled wool about a sheep's tail. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  dag n. excreta-soiled wool; v. cut such wool from a sheep; n. unconventional person. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  Dags: Soiled wool about a sheep's tail. <i>(Feb 24, 1977,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Dags: Wool contaminated with faeces which is either adhering to, or has been clipped from, the posterior of sheep. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Dag: literally, this refers to a clot of FECAL MATTER sticking to the tail end of a sheep. It has, however, become part of the language as in `What a dag!' - an admiring statement directed at someone who has done something slightly risque. Some joker threw a pie in the Prime Minister's face, what a dag! <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,31</i>)

>  Dags sheep excreta attached to wool. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Old Wool Terms)]

>  <b>HUTCHINGS</b> We used to roam the district ... picking up wool bales, crutchings and dags <i>Ibid.</i>86 Bill Schmidt, the dag crusher ... remained faithful to his fibrolite bach, and ... one suspects, true to Elvis Presley. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,79</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Dag. Dried dung hanging from the wool on the sheep's hind legs, under the tail. Cutting this off with an old pair of shears is called dagging. The dried dags on a sheep make a dry, rattling noise as the sheep runs, hence the term `rattle your dags'. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)

>  Other types of fleece which may be dealt with in the course of a rouseabout's day are: Dags (dirt-clogged wool) if the farmer hasn't bothered to dag before shearing; shed stain, which is good body wool spoilt when one sheep empties bowels on another while penned up closely(avoided by keeping sheep off grass for at least six hours prior); ... <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)

>  'Real dags' they were; there was one fellow, like, who cut off a leg of hisengine-driver's overalls to wrap a billy in, and another who had a beard like agoat! <i>Ibid.</i> 35 He was a tough old dag, and no mistake. <i>(Apr 1945,NZ Geographer,I/21</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Getting the skin off with its usual attachment of dags was hard work. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,193</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Here even the dags know their place. <i>(1986,Haliburtons of Glenfarg,15</i>)

>  'What a dag - living in a log cabin!' <i>(1975,Islands,4(2) : 118</i>)

>  If we did not have Bill in Porirua, these five kids would be on the streets... Bill's got them there because they like to be there - he's trendy, he's a dag ... he's just brilliant. <i>(Apr 5, 2000,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The Beehive was a blur of rattling dags and rustling bustles all year. <i>(Dec 23, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Wallace was a fullback, sometimes a three-quarter, but it's not just the backs who could be dags. <i>(Feb 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B4</i>)

>  I remember stoned bores talking about lacing the prime minister's milk bottles with LSD as their idea of wit - and the people who actually were given acid without warning. What a dag. Kind of. Wasn't it? <i>(Apr 15, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  Rags, dags and dreadlocks abound, mottled body suits, straw ponytails, group howls - all that's missing are the fire pois and the hacky sacks. <i>(Jan 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

>  This is a dag from several angles. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  I married a real dag, didn't I? <i>(1973,Islands,6/347</i>)

>  On Sundays, he leaves the dogs at the hut, graunches into the city in a clapped out Morris Oxford smelling of oily rags and sheep dags.. and takes his wife and his eldest brother down to his jazz club by the Avon. <i>(1973,Islands,6/360</i>)

>  The T wing of the shed, where the wool press and bins are located.. has a large cellar underneath, into which the daggs and such of the wool as requires handpicking is passed through the hatchway in the floor above. <i>(Sep 16, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  The old joke about growing trees for pulp being akin to growing sheep for the sake of their dags, dates back to the 1960s. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Woolly dags especially have a downside. They blow about in gales and lodge tenaciously in rose bushes where the highly visible white bits attract attention to the dark bits, shocking anyone with a sense of aesthetics and especially our farming friends. They say : "Do you know what dags are <i>worth?</i> <i>(Mar 26, 2000,NZ Catholic,14</i>)

>  I am thinking of washing my own locks andpieces and dags in future. <i>(1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,VII/569</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> By the end of February, he [sc. the station manager] considered that the Doctor was now competent to undertake a job without supervision and entrusted him to sorting the dags., <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,151</i>)

>  Interest rates are dropping like dags on shearing day. <i>(Sep 26, 1991,TV3,Nightline</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1938 Havelock]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Had a dag of a French period this week - teacher was reading ... and when she translated `quite naked' she blushed all over. <i>(1965,Power of Joy,252</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `Saying `tock-tick' too,' Bertie loved to say. `A dag of a watch.' <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an extraordinary person, a character; a `hard case'. Compare doer @n. [1953 15 M A24 Thames DHS 1 DAGS [a silly person] -->1953 16 M A34 Thames DHS 24 DAGS [names for authorities] 1984 16-17 F E3 Pakuranga Coll. 34 [something amusing] (A) DAG [F4M2] -->1984 14 F E138Wgton Girls C. 34 DAG [F6]]

>  `Anzac Types 2 - The Dag' ... Yes, 'Ennessy was a dag if ever there was one! <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,47</i>)

>  <b>17</b> I do remember though, ... three Diggers, real `dags', the three of them, who sang once on a route march as recently as a year ago. <i>(Sep 10, 1919,Quick March</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Dag. A `hard case'; a wag; a `character': Australia, thence New Zealand: from ca, 1890. Prob. ex dagen, q.v. [cant for an artful criminal or near criminal, itself ex cant dagen or degen, a sword] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,204</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... in constant use by our youngsters ... dag. [See also verb, where the implication is that Baker is not familiar with the noun in the sense of `character'.] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  In the rural districts [of New Zealand] a formidable body of idiomatic uses is developing and a new language is being coined. This isno more true than it is of the `remote' back-country of Canterbury, where changing vocabulary is already a noticable geographical characteristic. The local idiom is naturally often linked with sheep, wool, and station practice. `Dag', for example, is a word used first to describe a tuft of wool fouled by excrement. The removal of dags from the hind-quarters of sheep is a seasonal operation usually called `crutching.' Used personally, `dag' was earlier a term of some approbation. Its meaning has widened, however, and now signifies no more than `hard case', or `tough guy', or `card.' <i>(1945,NZ Geographer,19</i>) [NOTES: (Cumberland's note to Pascoe's `High Country')]

>  He was a tough old dag, and no mistake. <i>(1945,NZ Geographer,35</i>) [NOTES: Pascoe `High Country']

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The following New Zealand expressions derive from British dialects, ... dag: tough guy <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Struth, he was a dag. <i>(1946,That Summer,31</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `For once in ya life ya right. But you're a dag, you are. What's biting ya?' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,45</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> dag: 'character', wag. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,299</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He was a bit of a dag on that job, although he got quite a few skins. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,69</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Perfidious males,' she said, smiling. `Oh, you're a real dag.' <i>(1973,NZ Jack,162</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> I'm dying to see what she's like drunk. ... She's a hell of a dag.' <i>(1978,World Is An Orange, The,78</i>)

>  We both started giggling, because Aunty Jude was what she called a dag, but she was good value too ... <i>(Oct 6, 1979,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: (Eldred-Grigg `Puss in a Poke') in 1984 NZ Short Stories 68]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> But curiously enough, someone considered [at Nelson College] `a dag', `a bit of a one', `a rough diamond', could get away with a lot, even a lowly new bug; ... <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,148</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Joff, I can't wait,' Martha said. `It'll be a real dag.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,44</i>)

>  Two handsome middle-aged women boarded ... They looked a couple of dags. The driver asked if their husbands had let them out on the town. They laughed. <i>(1985,G'day Country,160</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> This country club ... had its characters, commonly termed `proper dags'. <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,155</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dag, daggy amusing person or thing, ... - what a dag of a person or thing. - a bit of a dag of a person. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,34</i>)

>  He knows he is good and he will walk around.. looking to see who is taking photos of him, he is a real dag. <i>(Oct 1, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to cut encrusted dirty wool from around a sheep's rear end.

>  <b>MONEY</b> Dagging and paring sheep, tailing lambs etc ... were the other employments ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Dagging the hoggets or drafting the ewes. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,9</i>)

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Rasmussen dagged a few hoggets. <i>(Sep 12, 1893,Brabazon Diaries,12</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The ewes should be dagged .. before turning the rams in. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He reckoned he put in over twenty years dagging sheep at Wakarua for Reg Collier - mostly with the tongs. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,180</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> When the pressure's really on we'll dag after tea. I think it's an important part of these boys' education that they dag, so they get an understanding of what the shearer's doing. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,80</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a funny person, a comedian; one who plays the wag, is amusingly mischievous.

>  <b>FRASER & GIBBONS</b> DAG, A: A funny fellow. A wag. and these comments and uses: <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,70</i>)

>  Cliff was a hard case. He knew lots of yarns too. He'd always start by saying `Did you hear theone about ...?' He didn't seem to like telling them when Norman was there, but he told me some corkers after. He was a real wag. It was good fun going round with him. <i>Ibid</i>. 80 `He's a wag,' said Cliff, `that Johnny Nelson. He's like Hoot Gibson in the pictures.' <i>(1984,Some Other Country,78</i>) [NOTES: 1950 Gaskell `All Part of the Game' in 1984 Some Other Country 78]

>  She was always told that dag was Southland rhyming slang for wag, which latter seemed to her mostly used in the North Island at any rate. But the early use (now obs) of Dag! as an expletive (related to Doggon!), from 1917 and Dag at `a dab at' may have some bearing on reinforcement. The probable origin is in EDD n2., v2. 11. a daring feat among boys Ches w Mid w Som: e.g. Som `set a dag: perform some feat in such a way as to challenge imitation ... saying: `There's a dag for you ... do it if you can.'' 12. have or make a dag at `have a shot at'; OED2 sb&sup5. esp. doing dags, 1879. The earliest non-extant usage may have referred to an event, rather than a person, out of the ordinary, rather than vice-versa as the present evidence might suggest. <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Dental Nurse from Southland]

>  Lambie appears, to my homosexual gaze, as a mumsy sort of woman, with the tang of Taranaki about her (I mean here the odour of 'a good sport', 'a dag', with something well-meaning and ingenuous in her manner). <i>(Jul 20, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),24</i>) [NOTES: Original in Landfall, 205 (Wells "Cosmetic..")]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Their stage attire consists of freezing-works overalls, gumboots and a generous sprinkling of straw or hay consistent with their "dags from the Catlins" image. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/46</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] a hard-case character.

>  <b>LEE</b> At any rate, the dag or hard-case character was always much appreciated wherever he appeared. <i>(1977,Roughnecks, Rolling stones & Rouseabouts,58</i>)

>  Any body who's a dag is a hard case. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> to get moving, to hurry along. [ca1979 July 10 (Doug Edwards) ... heard a young girl using it early 1979. 1986 a U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c. Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987) If someone tells you to get cracking or rattle your dags, they want you to hurry up.]

>  Blessedly, Riuchard's ex-wife's body is found in its concrete tomb, and the police rattle dags. <i>(Aug 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Told me to rattle my dags out of there - All right with a sugar bag over her head - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,161</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> JAMESON: It's about Matt! You have to see me first! At the double! Rattle your dags! <i>(1969,Awatea,27,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SANDYS</b> Come on, come on. Rattle your dags. ... <i>(1982,Love and War,271</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Dag. Dried dung hanging from the wool on the sheep's hind legs, under the tail. Cutting this offwith an old pair of shears is called dagging. The dried dags on a sheep make a dry, rattling noise as the sheep runs, hence the term `rattle your dags'. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)

>  But we really do need to know exactly how National proposes to rattle Maoridom's dags. <i>(Nov 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (transf.) To shorten trousers (etc.) roughly by cutting off excess length.

>  `Let's Go Native.' The [Maori boy] is wearing the [cast off] `pant' well up under the armpits ... They had been dagged at a convenient length round the ankles also. <i>(Feb 16, 1940,National Education,16</i>) [NOTES: (D. Leggett)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Baker refers to the above cit.and comments:] ... its application to the hacked hem of a pair of cut-down trousers is an interesting contribution to New Zealand colloquial speech. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,54</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an extraordinary situation or occurrence; esp. what a dag!. [1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 8 DAG, WHAT A DAG (=HOW FUNNY)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> Dag: literally, this refers to a clot of FECAL MATTER sticking to the tail end of a sheep. It has, however, become part of the language as in `What a dag!' - an admiring statement directed at someone who has done something slightly risque. Some joker threw a pie in the Prime Minister's face, what a dag! <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,31</i>)

>  what a dag <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  what a dag: odd situation, character <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>ROCHE</b> [New Zealanders c1939] larded their conversation with strange, uncouth expressions. `What a dag!' they said. (She was horrified when she found out what a dag was.) <i>(1979,Foreigner,187</i>)

>  I remember stoned bores talking about lacing the prime minister's milk bottles with LSD as their idea of wit - and the people who actually were given acid without warning. What a dag. Kind of. Wasn't it? <i>(Apr 15, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Lange came back from Australia after the debacle of two MPs who had supported him in his drive last year for the leadership fulminating that in Australia `the dags did not wag the sheep' in the Labour Party. The inescapable inference from that is that here the sheep is being wagged by the dags (a choice of phrase hardly likely to enthuse those accused of doing the wagging). <i>(Sep 26, 1983,National Business Review,9</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  And sadly, as most news editors grew up in the era when Budgets left no dag unrattled, the media will continue to treat them as mega-occasions. <i>(May 24, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### dag cutting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Dag Cutting. - The wool rollers take off the dirty portion of the fleece and throw it into a bin, the dag cutter ... with a pair of shears cuts off all the wool he can, which he throws into another bin. It is then called `second pieces'. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)

### dag one's trousers
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to remove the cuffs from a pair of musterer's new work trousers [so that they do not become tangled in small prickly plants and other impediments.]

>  <b>GORDON</b> The well-dressed hillman always dagged his trousers. No - the men who worked with sheep didn't imitate their woolly work's sanitary habits. Dagging trousers is cutting off the cuffs to give a nice tatty trouser leg which would get even tattier on the job thanks to spaniards and wild irishmen - spear grass and Matagouri. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

### dag pickers' ball
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sorting of dags from cleaner wool [usually carried out under the woolshed catching pens in wet weather.]

>  If he is unlucky he might have to go pearl-diving at the dag-pickers' ball - a wet-weather job sorting wool from dags under the woolshed. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> If the weather was too wet for outside work, they joined the shepherds in the woolshed at the 'dag picker's ball,' sorting out the dags from the cleaner wool, and helping to bale the crutchings. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,48</i>)

>  The dag pickers' ball is held under the woolshed and needs to take place in company. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### dag picking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sorting of wool from dags.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I was very thankfull [sic] for your reply to my application for the job Dag picking ... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,87</i>)

>  sorting dags - dag-picking. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### dag room
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area beneath a woolshed where dags consolidate.

>  <b>THORNTON</b> There is an eighteen-metre-long dag room - an innovative provision with a tramline down the middle to take a trolley. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,171</i>)

### dag score
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) make an assessment of encrusted faeces on a sheep rear end.

>  At tailing, in early November, the ewes were weighed, faecal egg scores taken and dag scored on 0-2 system. <i>(Jul 2004,Country-Wide,39</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the extent to which a sheep produces dags or dungy wool in relation to weight gain.

>  He charted the amount of dags against weight gain, finding a very even correlation - more dags, less weight gain, and a high correlation between dag score and Wormfree blood tests run by MAF, Invermay. <i>(Aug 31, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

>  Analysis showed that bareness [of a sheep's rear end] related to total average dag score. Only 3% of lambs with no dags at all got flystruck compared with 49% with a dag score of four and 82% with a dag score of five. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

>  The dag scores, although the scoring system was slightly modified between tailing and pre-wean, strongly favoured the treated ewes. <i>(Jul 2004,Country-Wide,39</i>)

 <i>n.</i> A young dag-picker q.v.

>  <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: See cit. dag-picker.(Acland Gloss.)]

### dag-classing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sorting of wool from dags. (See [dag-picking](../D#dag-picking))

>  One man will find himself kept fairly busy keeping the sheep up to two good crutchers, and 'dag-classing' at the same time. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,705</i>)

### dag-crusher
 
 <i>n.</i> a machine to crush dags to enable the removal of wool.

>  Dag-crusher machine to remove wool from dags. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHINGS</b> And then the dag bags had to be manhandled to the dag crusher down by the pond. Bill Schmidt was in charge of the dag crusher. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,80</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a person who operates a dag-crusher, or dag-crushing plant.

>  Bill Schmidt, the dag crusher ... remained faithful to his fibrolite bach, and ... one suspects, true to Elvis Presley. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,86</i>)

### dag-crushing
 
 <i>n.</i> the crushing of dags preparatory to removing the wool. [1988 Feb 14 On a shed in Central Otago (past Lawrence)]

>  As its operations have grown, they have at various times included a wool scour, a trucking business, a dag-crushing operation and the installation of one of the first grab-sampling and core-testing machines. <i>(Oct 11, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### dag-cutter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Dag Cutting. - The wool rollers take off the dirty portion of the fleece and throw it into a bin, the dag cutter ... with a pair of shears cuts off all the wool he can, which he throws into another bin. It is then called `second pieces'. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)

### dag-picker
 
 <i>n.</i> a shedhand who removes wool from dags; see also dag-boy.

>  Dag: ... Cutting this off the sheep with an old pair of shears is called dagging. The ds. are afterwards gone through by a d.-picker or d.-boy, who cuts out any wool worth saving. (C [c1890-1910].) <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>DUFF</b> Dag: ... Cutting this off the sheep with an old pair of shears is called dagging. The ds. are afterwards gone through by a d.-picker or d.-boy, who cuts out any wool worth saving. (C [c1890-1910].) <i>(Jul 22, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,40,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> <i>(1913,Country Life</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a best example, a molre sensational event

### dag-rattling
 
 <i>adj.</i> [(identical citations from two different sources with the same date and page number; one must be an error)]

>  The package, despite being described by most commentators as slender and likely to do little to preserve this country's economic dignity, was actually quite ... er ... dag-rattling. <i>(Oct 10, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> [They met] in the dust and dag-stink of a woolstore. New to the city, a refugee ... from a depressed backblocks farm, that first one had been shy and timid. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Paua Gatherers)]

### dagger
 
 <i>n.</i> one who dags sheep.

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> He would take what came, nor shirk or fret,/ Be `brander', `rouse-about' or `dagger',/ Yet work he never seemed to get. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,28</i>)

>  What with the dogs the roussies and penners-up and daggers brought to give them a hand in the shed and yards ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,127,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> What with the dogs, the roussies [sic] and penners-up and daggers brought in to give them a hand in the shed ... <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),27</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> In just the same way as the classer enjoys the top of the woolshed's social scale, so the dagger is the most lowly. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,25</i>)

>  The couple is now employing a casual dagger ... <i>(Feb 8, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

### dagginess
 
 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the carrying of encrusted faeces in the rear end wool of lambs and sheep.

>  Another drawback to paddock sheepfarming .. is scouring or dagginess in spring. <i>(Oct 1885,NZ Farmer,293</i>)

>  Mr Turner also culls for dagginess. <i>(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  This [production of pelleted faeces] leads to a reduction in dagginess of animals and helps to reduce the likelihood of flystrike..the potential benefits that can be gained through the grazing of Lotus are: .. reduced dagginess ... <i>(May 7, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

>  .. the toxins they [endophytes] produce could cause a range of problems such as ryegrass staggers, dagginess in lambs, increased risk of flystrike and heat stress. <i>(Nov 8, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### dagging
 
 <i>n.</i> removal of wool coated with faeces from the posterior of sheep.

>  For three years in summer Waddell would drive the 90-minute road from Hamilton to Piopio, sweat the day out dagging on the farm, working for his parents. <i>(May 2000,North & South,63</i>)

>  Those rural breakfasts - plates of steaming porridge.. followed by chops, sausages, bacon and eggs, washed down by pints of tea! All to fuel the male mechanism for the daily round of dagging, dipping and fencing. <i>(Jan 28, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Dagging and paring sheep, tailing lambs, etc, felling timber, ... were the other employments... at Mr. McBeth's farm ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> When the docking, and dagging and cutting come he is worked as hard as any man <i>(1898,Maori Maid,147</i>)

>  LAMB DAGGING. - ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,319</i>)

>  Proper dipping, crutching, dagging, &c., are necessary; and if scouring be present care should especially be taken to keep the posterior parts clear of dung-clogged wool ... <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,577</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> So, after three days of mustering about the paddocks, with a dagging thrown in, we were glad to get away up Yoe Creek again, to straggle the ewe country. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

>  Dagging: Removal of wool from the posterior of sheep coated with faeces. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,50</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> I found on this heavy grass country carrying a flock of Romney sheep that there was continual dagging and crutching to keep the wool clean ... <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,79</i>)

>  His pater paid to the runholder.. for him dagging" sheep - "dagging" is the technical term for clipping the dirty wool from around the jumbuckposteriors. <i>(Sep 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

>  When witness had finished "dagging," hedrafted the sheep. <i>(Oct 24, 1914,Stratford Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Dagging was becoming necessary with the increase of long-woolled sheep and of young grass. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `That an allowance in the case of daggy sheep be arranged for,' was dealt with; ... it was decided: `That all members refuse to dagg [sic] sheep during shearing except the dagging rate be addedto the shearing rate, as we are of the opinion that dagging is not shearing. <i>(1912,NZ Shearers (Report Third Annual Conference)</i>) [NOTES: 1912 NZ Shearers ... Report Third Annual Conf. 15 Mount Watkins Remit No. 3:]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the removing of encrusted faeces from hindquarters of sheep.

>  A little wool taken off when dagging is all that is required. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,364</i>)

>  Butchers in the freezing works in Canterbury have several times drawn attention to the faulty and non-dagging of sheep and lambs. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,705</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Cleanliness, by way of proper dipping, dagging and crutching are good preventive aids. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,120</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> So after three days mustering about the paddocks, with a dagging thrown in, we were glad to get away up Yoe Creek again to straggle the ewe country. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

>  Dagging was needed less often. Wool was not shed-stained. <i>(May 8, 1969,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  His back wasn't standing up to a day's dagging as well as it used to ... <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  Mr Turner also culls for dagginess. 'I don't like dagging,' he says. <i>(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

### dagging bay
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area in a woolshed away from the main shearing board where sheep are dagged.

>  8 stand woolshed and covered yards (2,500) 3 stand dagging bay ... <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

### dagging gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of workers who dag sheep prior to shearing.

>  The dagging gang then gets to work while the lambs are being docked in the other pen. <i>(Aug 1919,NZ Farmer,975</i>)

### dagging pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a penned space in a woolshed where sheep are dagged prior to shearing.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The drafting yards and dagging pens were concreted and all under cover, so they could also be used as night pens. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,130</i>)

>  No matter how clean the dagging pen is kept, it will be found that the fleece is more or less stained with urine and dung. <i>(Oct 1915,NZ Farmer,1258</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> It is an advantage, if possible, to provide one or two small dagging pens to hold about 12 sheep each. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,353</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Sheep-holding space beneath the wool-room floor leads up to a ramp to the wet-weather entrance through the trap door, which is situated in a dagging pen that is really an extension of the sheep entry race. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,357</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Of course, to spend much time in the dagging-pen is not a shepherd's life-long wish, but it has to be done. [dagging-pen] <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,105</i>)

### dagging plant
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a portable machine for removing dags from the rear end of sheep.

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> We were to see the introduction of portable dagging plants which in time has all replaced those blister making dagging shears that somebody once said needed a strong wrist but a weak head to operate. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,186</i>)

>  On site - electric dagging plant. <i>(Mar 23, 1996,Napier Daily Telegraph,24</i>)

>  Wolsley single phase dagging plant $450. <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,34</i>)

>  Farm clearing sale: .. portable dagging plant, docking gates ... <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

### dagging race
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The three-stand woolshed has a 500 night-pen capacity, a dagging race and a cradle. <i>(Mar 28, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### dagging shears
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Many accidents occur on farms and stations through men being careless when carrying dagging shears. <i>(Oct 26, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,84</i>)

>  dagging shears: Handshears that are too worn for shearing but still suitable for cutting dags or for gardening. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/23</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### dagging shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small shed that is set up for dagging or crutching, but inadequate for shearing.

>  114 ha of good grazing land with hay shed, sheepyards and dagging shed. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,5</i>)

### daggings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Advt] Daggings, Daggings, Daggings. Wehave a buyer for good dried woolliedDaggings. <i>(Jan 29, 1917,Feilding Star,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the product of the dagging process.

>  Daggings - we are buyers of good dags. <i>(Nov 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  Wanted to buy: wool, sheepskins, daggings. <i>(Aug 24, 1972,NZ Farmer,136</i>)

### daggish
 
 <i>adj.</i> of the nature of a dag n1.; humorously eccentric. [ca1940 Havelock]

>  There, he shook hands with the obviously brilliant female journalist's parents, donned a daggish hat and assisted lambs into their docking chairs. <i>(Oct 2, 2003,Dominion,B9</i>)

### daggy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep or wool] encrusted with dried excrement.

>  Young people mainly,.. mostly farming or shearing folk by the way they were dressed; rough old jeans, muddy boots and in some cases, singlets mightily stained as if their owners had spent the day, if not the week, working on the daggy end of a sheep. <i>(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),92</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is not advisable to carry out this work [sc. crutching] [75] too early or else the ewes will be very daggy by the lambing season, <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> ... a truck of daggy wool. <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,153</i>) [NOTES: 1940 Meek `Wool Commander' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 153]

>  One daggy sheep can stain quite a few clean sheep when penning up is in progress. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,310</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The next point, ... [12], relates to `daggy' sheep. Sheep should be dagged before they enter the woolshed, ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,11</i>)

>  [Freezing Companys' Advt] Notice to Fat Stock Producers Daggy and Dirty Stock. ... All stock for slaughter must be free of dags and dirt. <i>(Feb 12, 1960,Dominion,11</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> The sheep all had to be crutched ... clearing the wool from under the tail ... to stop them getting daggy. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,77</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Then it's grab a daggy ewe and drag her to the board,/ Pck up your hand-piece and pull on the cord./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,72</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> As I was cleaning the legs on a daggy lamb it suddenly kicked and I accidentally cut the hamstring. <i>(1986,Black Billy Tea,98</i>)

>  Aspinall says the New Zealand shearing shed image of daggy sheep is a million miles away from the reality of cheesemaking, where bad milk equals an unsaleable product. <i>(Apr 4, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),10</i>)

>  Sheep allowed to get daggy soon fall off. <i>(Oct 1885,NZ Farmer,293</i>)

>  Daggy fleeces in the shed at shearing time cause a lot of extra and unpleasant work. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,705</i>)

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> Don't put daggy sheep into the night pen before shearing: let them stand in the yard before shedding and save discomfort for the shearer. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,104</i>)

>  One daggy sheep can stain quite a few clean sheep when penning up is in progress. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,310</i>)

>  You're increasing costs - when you send daggy, dirty stock to the works. <i>(Jan 28, 1965,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  And the hurrying hoggets haunt me and their daggy backsides daunt me. <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60</i>)

>  .. cutters will need a re-sharpen after every five to ten sheep, depending on the coarseness of their wool, how daggy their backsides are ... <i>(May 2002,Growing Today,65</i>)

>  <b>BALL</b> You are a daggy sheep. <i>(1970,Passionate Pen, The,129</i>)

>  I hit up the boss at the finish for expenses and payment for the daggy sheep. <i>(Jan 16, 1920,Maoriland Worker,8</i>)

>  Don't forget to skirt your fleeces. Even the smallest lot should be carefully skirted, so as to relieve the fleeces of all stained and daggy portions. <i>(Dec 3, 1885,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Owing to the long wools of the North Island being so daggy and having to be shorn so closely, it paid shearers as well to shear in this island for 16s 8d, where the cut is slightly rougher. <i>(Aug 12, 1887,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  Any ewes of whatever age should go out of the flock if they are scouring, or daggy, or bad doers. <i>(Feb 13, 1907,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  Sheep allowed to get daggy soon fall off [in condition]. <i>(Sep 29, 1885,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  In trimming this sheep the machine took off some slightly daggy locks in the breach in a way that gave much satisfaction. <i>(Mar 25, 1887,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  . . but every particle of wool is taken off, the trimming is perfect, and even the daggy locks that are occasionally found in the breech are taken off with the utmost ease, and by the time the sheep leaves the operator's hands it looks as though it had been shaved rather than shorn. <i>(Oct 8, 1890,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  The great thing is to be very careful to keep all heavy, daggy, discolored pieces and locks quite separate from the light clean portions. <i>(Oct 31, 1889,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  By doing so now the wool from those which are clean can be saved and sold, whereas if it is not done till they get dirty and daggy, the wool is spoiled. <i>(Feb 22, 1900,Otago Witness,6</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of persons or events] known to stand out from a crowd or to be amusing.

>  Two young men with bad hair, daggy clothes and odd expressions spent their days happily juggling teacups, teaspoons and sugar cubes and tenderly tending their tea-making paraphenalia... <i>(Sep 23, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  Nor were there daggy caravan vacations driving us to the end of our golden tethers, but which we'd all agree later were worth the hassle. <i>(Dec 5, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Cropdusting, as seen by Stephen Woodman and Andrew Niccol in their daggy Paying Through the Nose (Sphere, $4.95). Wry cartoons give ingenious literal interpretations of English expressions ... <i>(Aug 3, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dag, daggy amusing person or thing, ... - what a dag of a person or thing. - a bit of a dag of a person. - 'dag' an excrement-coated wool clot around sheep's anus, is the origin of another popular antipodean phr. rattle your dags! hurry up! <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,34</i>)

>  She mutters to herself on the tube, and her compulsive need to play the recorder in times of distress is so daggy as to ring brilliantly true. <i>(Nov 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  Randerson, who is wearing daggy striped stockings - the only colour in her all-black outfit - will be in St Petersburg for the city's 300-year anniversary... <i>(May 9, 2003,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  [Hdg] Daggy boys relieve the grey... Te Nana and his great friend and sevens comrade Eric Rush are living proof that even in a game that can get as grim and grey as rugby, there's still room for the daggy boy... How daggy is that? To be fair, though, there's no records of him ever playing in a black singlet and gumboots... Wilson, a true daggy boy, is a man who often jokes about his own playing abilities but his team mates didn't like him just because he was funny. <i>(Feb 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B4</i>)

>  But the highlight of the evening has to be the genuinely daggy Aotearoa platter, brought to the table with the shrimp cocktail riding high in the retro martini glass, with battered mini-hotdogs on sticks with authentic dipping sauce of Wattie's tomato, as well as Marmite and cheese club sandwiches, good roast lamb and mint, kumara shavings and, for sweets, a thick pancake rolled up with lemon and sugar just like Mum used to make. <i>(Jul 19, 2001,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  For a circa 6ft, middle-aged woman to dress in this way would be described in England as "eccentric." Here, we have out own word - "daggy", which carries the connotation of warm acceptance .. While employees in the Foreign Affairs Ministry might be alarmed if she was their boss, in the case of Winz, I imagine that having a daggy CEO could be a plus. <i>(Jul 14, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Daggy inventions from school students - hanging basket liners made from sheep 'dags'and bench-top hydroponic systems are just two of the innovative projects ... <i>(2001,Internet - see Notes,News:</i>) [NOTES: agriculture.massey.ac.nz]

>  <b>Faith Oxenbridge</b> She...is even nice to the daggy teachers with bad breath and dandruff who pretty much everyone else despises. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,16</i>)

>  Don't you worry about daggy boy. <i>(Sep 9, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)

>  Journalists don't get paid a million a year to wave their daggy little personalities around to a sighing and adoring throng. <i>(Oct 5, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C8.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> . [pop group; aka Daggy and his Taihape Dickheads]

>  daggy a. NZ & Austral. (of sheep) clotted with dags. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,185</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

Two of Bruiser's boys and their cousins, the McCartins, would do it from Taihape with their band Daggy and the Dickheads. <i>(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

### daggy boy
 
 [aka daggy-boy; used variously: comedian Fred Dagg, rugby as well as rugby player Israel Dagg, race horse Daggy Boy, and etc.]

### dago
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) a Maori [(offensive, ?obs)]

>  At a recent Otaki meeting an intoxicated Dago assured me that ... he was the `pest plurry chockey in New Zealand.' <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  ... Parata Pene is a smart wowseristic dago, who proudly declared himself to be the President of the church, presumably a Mormon temple. [Pene is (obviously) a Maori] <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  Heavens above, we filch the nigger's land and then we make an alien of him. Why the day will come when the Dago won't be at home here. <i>(Jan 17, 1911,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  Among the many things for which New Zealand is noted is the manner in which the native population, i.e. the Maoris, commonly and contemptuously called by the pakeha, `the dago,' has been cared for by a paternal - like Government. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,344/7/4</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Skin colour and the mixture of blood which is responsible for it [sc. high visibility in a white world] are often commented on by one Maori to another. The terms `mongrel', `black mongrel', `dago', or `black nigger', for instance, are often used by Maoris either in referring to themselves or to other Maoris in the neighbourhood. Thus one informant, in introducing herself, remarked: `I'm only a mongrel' - meaning only a mixed-blood Maori. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,299</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a hat (?of what kind)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> It was not merely that Tony ... dashingly wore a round black hat of the variety known as dago, and was further very elegant in an expensive winter overcoat; ... <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,186</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a day-boy; cf day-goes etc. [(boarding school) ca1950s St Patrick's Silverstream (Warwick Howe) -->1951 16-18 M 32 St Bede's, Chch 25 DAGOS, DAY-GOES [M16]]



 
 <i>adj.</i> (offensive)

>  "Dago" oyster saloons are springing up anywhere in Auckland's principal streets. <i>(Jul 23, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Make the whole place smell like a dago fish shop with decayed scrapings. <i>(Jul 19, 1913,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  <b>McGEE</b> Tipper sounds like a dago waiter bending and scraping like a catholic warming up for confession. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,73</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) [c1930s early p.c. Dr Harold Miller, of one of the last European itinerant bottleos in Adelaide Road, Wellington, competing with the majority Indians in the trade with a street-cry (and sign painted on his cart): `GIVE ME YOUR BOTTLES BEFORE THE DAY GOES'.]

>  His rival in trade was a Dago - that is,he was either an Italian, a Spaniard, aPortuguese, a Greek or a Hun. <i>(Dec 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 33</i>)

>  [Caption] Mother told me, "Never let a Dago by without doing something." <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 5</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Its a quiet dump. All Dagoes. <i>(1946,That Summer,34</i>) [NOTES: White Man's Burden) [?DALLY, ?MAORI]]

>  An oily voiced Italiano or Greek or some class of Dago knocked and walked into my office. <i>(Mar 13, 1926,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> "You calla me Dago? I calla you Square-'ead." <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,35</i>)

>  All foreigners aboard ship are either "Dutchmen" or "Dagos". <i>(Feb 8, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  "Our bloody housekeeping's gone up since you met that dago," said Pop most weekends. <i>(May 7, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  [Hdg] Was it Vitriol that the [Greek] Fish Shop Dago Threw? <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  Up in Gisborne.. where the chief products are greasy dagos, long beers and Tucker's tenants, a bright infant has been unearthed. <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> So, you're mucking around with dagoes now. Mum's going to be interested. <i>(1951,Through South Westland,83</i>)

### dags
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [rarely used in sing.] encrusted faeces that hang on to the wool at a sheep's rear end.

>  Dag, dags. Hard or soft dung hanging from the breech of a sheep. <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  Woolly dags are of value. We will pay highest market prices according to quality for dry wooly dags. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Dags: Dung, hard or soft, soiling the wool round the sheep's tail. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,24</i>)

>  If fed correctly, goats don't produce dags. <i>(1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar,9</i>) [NOTES: 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

### dagwool
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wool from the rear end of a sheep.

>  <b>BREMNER</b> .. locks, pieces, crutchings and dagwool were bought at auctions or from dealers ... <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,115</i>)

### dahn line
 
 <i>n.</i> type of set line with multiple hooks used for fishing groper and sharks [also dan line]

>  I still catch groper on a dahn line. It's a line with ten baited hooks, an anchor on the bottom and a float on top. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,24</i>)

>  <b>MUNRO, D.J. & P.J.</b> Later the "long-line" was introduced, with 100 to 300 hooks, buoyed with floats at each end, and the "dan" line - 7 to 20 hooks, with a grapnel at bottom and buoy at top, left in place for 1 to 3 hours, when the hard work had to be repeated. <i>(1984,History of Port Chalmers Fishermen's Co-operative,9</i>)

### Daily Keno
 
 <i>n.</i> a daily lottery game

### dairy
 
 <i>n.</i> corner shop, convenience store (US); a small shop selling groceries, milk, etc. outside normal shopping hours [It is difficult to find "dairy" clearly used in the sense of a small mixed shop before 1943 ("cash grocer" seems to have been the usual term), the following sequence does seem to indicate the change in use from "dairy" as a supplier of milk (often with several depots around the town) to "dairy" meaning a mixed shop. The shop in Upland Road, Kelburn, was run in 1938 as a confectionary shop (cnfr); in 1939 it is listed in both Wise's and the Wellington telephone directory as "dairy" and is the only small shop listed under "Dairies" in the classified section which uses "dairy" in its trade name.]

>  Upland rd... 92 Skelton Mrs H cnfr <i>(1938,Wise's NZPO Directory,1071</i>)

>  Dairies... Upland Rd Dairy, 92 Upland RdKelburn <i>(1939,Wellington Telephone Directory,00</i>) [NOTES: In Trade Directory]

>  <i>Krishnan's Dairy</i>, a runaway hit two years ago, has sold out its Auckland season this month and is heading the same way in Wellington. <i>(Oct 7, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Then a good read, / a New Zealand novel about a dead / man found in the Tip-Top freezer / in a corner dairy <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  There is still a danger where dairy shops buy raw milk for use in milk shakes. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,306</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> He entered the dairy. ...Mrs Morpeth sold milk, cream, ice cream, bread, scones, all sorts of sweets ... and blocks of cake and tins of biscuits; ... <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,30,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUCKWORTH</b> It's raining like a bastard, and we didn't put any [milk] bottles out. I'll have to go to the dairy. <i>(1963,Barbarous Tongue, A,181</i>)

>  Outside the dairy Sue Appleton's poodle. <i>(1974,Islands,7/51</i>) [NOTES: Press (Man in Wardrobe)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> Dairy a NEIGHBOURHOOD mom and pop STORE <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,31</i>)

>  dairy ... 4. N.Z. a general shop, selling provisions, cigarettes, etc., and open seven days a week. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,281</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 186; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 120]]

>  dairy: <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Only the dairy and the take-away still open. Down the other end usual crowd round the fun parlour but where I was, nobody else. <i>(1983,Murder to Burn,94</i>)

>  There's a doctor's house, ... a TAB, and here's a dairy. Have I enough money for some chippies. <i>(1985,Women's Work,215</i>) [NOTES: (Coalhouse (The Mask)]

>  Sells bread as well as milk in London in 1860s. A small, mixed-grocery store (orig. licensed to sell milk and dairy products, and other perishables after normal trading hours); poss. earlier dairy shop: cf. <i>(1904,Our Mutual Friend,vol II p. 208,ed. Howell ed.</i>)

>  <i>how far was it?</i> / about / from here to the dairy <i>(May 17, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>) [NOTES: Janet Charman (when i was young)]

>  [If a milk-bar or a dairy establishment sells, say, tea or sugar, it must observe the award conditions applicable to grocers. ] <i>(1945,NZ Parliamentary Debates,245/263</i>)

>  In Auckland a large number of shops close on Saturday... The result is that all these little dairies have arisen . In a city in which the milk is delivered every morning, irrespective of the dairies, there are twenty-nine dairies in one road. It is not milk they are selling, but groceries. <i>(1945,NZ Parliamentary Debates,272/268</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> Then, on a corner, up against the footpath, a sagging dairy with its tiny window stuck about with torn and fly-spotted posters for ice-cream and cigarettes. <i>(1990,Bad Music,103</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> dairy farm, a farm producing milk or milk products; dairy cattle, any cattle bred or kept to produce milk rather than meat; dairy factory, a butter or cheese factory; dairy-beef, beef animals bred in a dairy herd. [(obs)]



 
 <i>n.</i> in earliest NZ use, as Brit., a dairy-farm; see also dairy station.

>  <b>REVANS</b> <i>(1841,Letters,170, 180</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Revans Letters (to Chapman) TS ATB 170,180]

>  <b>DILLON</b> He has very large herds and a large dairy where he makes a great deal of butter and cheese. His dairy is of about 40 cows. <i>(Apr 10, 1844,Letters,40,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> As it grew we reached the hospitable roof of Geo. McRae who has an extensive sheep run and a large dairy. <i>(Mar 11, 1853,Traveller under Concern,86,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> A dairy is ... a more interesting, and, generally, a more prettily situated homestead than asheep station, which ought not to be placed ... where there is low underwood to catch the fleeces of the sheep <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),237</i>)

>  `Herringbone' dairies, developed in New Zealand, are increasing rapidly in number. <i>Ibid./</i> 83 The backing gate is used to bring cows from the yard into the dairy. Its main purpose is to keep stock near the milking area. As each cow is taken in to be milked, another fills up the space it has left and there are no delays in milking. The gate is controlled by the milker, who can bring in the next cows without having to leave those already being milked. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,81</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> There were 7 or 8 of us lads and [c1840s] our chief business [15] was a dairy - but not such a dairy as we have now. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> The place on a farm where milk, cream etc, are kept and butter made [1844 See Dillon above.]

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The dairy, a cool building shaded by a pine-tree, stood near the house. On a bench which extended along the interior stood large shallow metal dishes in which the milk was placed. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,14</i>) [NOTES: c1880s Seddon The Seddons (1968) 14]

>  Definition. - A `dairy' is: (a) a milk-house, milk-shop, dairy factory, and any other place wheredairy produce is collected, deposited, treated, separated, prepared, or manufactured, or is sold or offered or exposed for sale; and includes (b) a farm, stockyard, milking-yard, paddock, stable,stall, and any other place where cows from which the milk-supply of a dairy is obtained are depastured or kept. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,148</i>) [NOTES: 1898 Dairy Industry Act in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 148]

>  (Appendix iii) `Dairy' means - (a) A milk-house, milk shop, dairy factory, and any other placewhere dairy produce is collected, deposited, treated, separated, prepared, or manufactured, or is sold or offered, or exposed for sale; and includes - (b) Any place where cows from which the milk supply of a dairy is obtained are depastured or kept. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide</i>) [NOTES: 1898 The Dairy Industry Act in 1902 Brett's Colonists' Guide]

>  <b>KEENE</b> A dairy made from packing cases was attached to the back of the house; and shelves to hold the milk pans were made from split rails. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,58</i>)

### Dairy Board
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> At the farmers' instigation, in 1922 the Government set up a Meat Board, and a year later, though there was in this case some opposition from farmers, a Dairy Board. These Boards were made up chiefly of producers' representatives, though with a few Government members, and were empowered to control the export trade. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,245</i>)

### dairy butter
 
 <i>n.</i> the usual name for farm butter, home-made butter as distinct from factory butter, or earlier shop butter

>  Dairy Butter. <i>Ibid./</i> 151 Stamps. - the following stamps are prescribed by the regulations:- In the case of a private dairy:- ... NEW ZEALAND/ Produce. Pure/ DAIRY BUTTER./ No. 0 Regd./ ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,108</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> The table groaned under a huge joint of cold meat, loaves of home-made bread and pounds of dairy butter. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,18</i>)

>  <b>BETHELL</b> `Discipline' The taste of the potatoes was satisfactory/ With a sprig of fresh mint, dairy butter, and very young green peas. <i>(1929,Garden in the Antipodes, A</i>)

### dairy co-op
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  What you have seen is the difficulties in the short term combining with the corporate culture from which most directors come with the particular culture that has been part of the history of dairy co-ops and the Dairy Board. <i>(Jan 23, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  They get elected to a dairy co-op by winning enough votes in their district or ward. <i>(Mar 28, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

### dairy conversion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Roadley was one of the pioneers of dairy conversion in Canterbury, after relocating from the North Island in 1980 to party of the existing Dromore farm, which was then run as a sheep farm. <i>(Nov 5, 2002,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  The vice-chairman of the dairy section of the North Canterbury Federated Farmers.. said the number of sharemilkers fluctuated, depending on whether there was a burst of dairy conversions. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,32</i>)

### dairy factory
 
 <i>n.</i> a factory where usu. cream is made into butter, or milk into cheese

>  <b>McALPINE</b> He would separate the milk - just two cows, but he sent the cream away to the dairy factory. <i>(1998,Passionate Pen, The,55</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> The dairy factory employs about 13 workers. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,23</i>)

>  Dairy and cheese factories are dotted over the plains, cream-cans stand at farm gates. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,25</i>) [NOTES: (from Korero, AEWS magazine)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Frank ... used to fly model aeroplanes of balsa [150] and tissue in the big paddock behind the dairy factory. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,149</i>)

>  ... milking cows and sending cream down river to the dairy factory, ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,200,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary</i>)

>  dairy factory NZ place where dairy products are processed. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 120]]

>  Last year, the gang tried to buy a dairy factory at Piriaka ... <i>(Jun 4, 1989,Sunday Star (Auckland),A6</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I have inspected a number of the dairy factories in the colony. <i>(1888,Report on Dairy Factories in NZ,5</i>) [NOTES: [OED2]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...he remembered Uncle Rangi laughing when he admitted to being scared riding his pushbike past an old church and gaveyard on his way from Ohaeawai to the late shift at the Moerewa dairy factory. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,65</i>)

>  ...the locality of Tokaora, now bereft of its hall, dairy factory and school... <i>(2009,NZ Memories,77/53</i>)

>  When horse and cart was the only mneans of transport, dairy factories were small and closely spaced, since it was impractical to take milk more than a few kilometres. Today's huge plants serve vast areas. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/39</i>)

>  "Dairy factory" means butter-factory orcheese-factory, and includes creameriesand skimming stations. <i>(1898,NZ Statutes,62 Vict. 22/73</i>)

>  The Moa Dairy Factory traces its origins back to 1884 and was a first for the Taranaki region. Butter was sold to local suppliers and storekeepers and later cheese. <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Memories,No. 31/52</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) dairy stock that is brought in (hired) to graze land.

Using only the best dairy salt, or in case of common salt carefully crushing the salt, mixing it well with the butter, and, after letting it stand for 24 hours, again working and mixing, will prevent it. <i>(Mar 1, 1873,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Fernridge School pupils have spent the past two years helping to establish insurance populations of <i>Muehlenbackia astonii, Coprosma wallii, Carex cirrhosa</i> and <i>Brachyglottis compactus</i> (Castlepoint daisy) in the school grounds. <i>(Oct 2003,Footnotes,No. 20/7</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?often wrongly applied to Cape weed.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CAPE WEED (<i>Cryptostemma calendulaceum</i> [1967] <i>Calendula</i>) or Cape daisy is abundant in pastures and waste places in the North Island and is also recorded from Greymouth. It is a soft herb, rarely a foot high. ... This weed comes from Cape Colony and so is properly called `Cape Weed'. The same name is given to cats-ear, a much more common weed. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,184,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Cape daisy [=] Cape weed: catsear: hawksbeard [s] </i> <i>Ibid./</i> 20 <i>daisy, Cape [=] Cape weed: catsear: hawksbeard [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,11</i>)

The very thick leathery leaves ... with lower surface entirely covered in soft, feltlike, buff to almost white tomentum.Notes Contributor Orsman Database / DEH : VUW <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,22</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> But ... the dog-daisy ... and the foxglove, to mention only a few British plants, are now wild in various places. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,135</i>)

>  It was thought that the Manhire scandal, which was responsible for the retirement of ex-Inspector Gillies from the police force, had been deeply and irrevocably buried, and that dogdasies [sic] six feet in height were growing on its grave. <i>(Sep 2, 1911,NZ Truth,323/4/4</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> OXEYE DAISY (<i>Chrysanthemum leucanthemum</i>), also called dog daisy, moon daisy and poached eggs, is common in pastures on somewhat heavy and damp land throughout the islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,176,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> SHASTA DAISY (<i>Chrysanthemum maximum</i>); WHITE BUSH MARGUERITE (<i>C. frutescens</i>) <i>(1980,Wild Flowers</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The smell of dog-daisies was unpleasant ... <i>(1965,Adaptable Man,The,113</i>)

It is s about as common as the former species [oxeye daisy], and grows in similar places. It is inodorous, and the flowers are about 2 inches across. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,176</i>)
