<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Celmisia</i> MOUNTAIN DAISIES <i>Celmisia</i> is a large genus of daisies with about 60 species in this country and the remaining few in Tasmania and Australia. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,194</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> [caption] Two mountain daisies are present, <i>C[elmisia] semicordata</i> below and <i>C. armstrongii </i>above. <i>Ibid.</i> 179 Mountain daisies or celmisias are probably the most frequently encountered of our alpine herbs. a. <i>Celmisia coriacea</i>, =cotton plant. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,177</i>)

>  <i>ibid.</i> 210 ... the great Mountain Daisies [fn Celmisias of various species, notably <i>Cel. coriacea</i>.] and other plants which fleck the upland meadows with purest whites and softest yellows ... [<i>Celmisia coriacea</i>] The great white buttercup of the Southern Alps, handsomest of its genus, grows in profusion, only rivalled in beauty by the marguerite-like flowers of the numerous different kinds of `cotton plant' or mountain daisy [fn Species of <i>Celmisia</i>.]. To this assemblage of plants also belong the New Zealand `Edelweiss' [fn <i>Helichrysum grandiceps</i>.] (no mean representative of the Swiss plant [190] from which it derives its name), and the exquisite white-flowered ourisias [fn <i>Ourisia macrocarpa</i> and <i>macrophylla</i>.], ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,188</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Most abundant are the giant alpine buttercup (Ranunculus Lyalli), popularly called `mountain lily', and the celmisia or mountain daisy, called by the Maoris <i> ti-kumu. </i> <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island)70</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> (And) <i>(1908,Kea, The: a NZ Problem</i>)

>  As adjective, often used as part of the name of a species found in the back country, mostly smaller than their down-country relatives, e.g. <i>m</i>. pine, totara, minnow, duck (blue duck), daisy, lily (Mount Cook lily). (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Celmisia coriacea (The Mountain Daisy) </i>... Both Islands, mountainous districts. The Mountain Daisy. ... There are many varieties. <i>Ibid./</i> 449 Most of the [<i>Celmisia</i>] species are found in sub-alpine regions, and are termed by the inhabitants Mountain Daisies or Cotton Plants. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,448</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> `We have the Edelweiss, just like Switzerland. It's a little, flannel-like flower, found on the steep crags of our Alps. And in the meadows [160] grow mountain lilies ... Here, also are mountain daisies called cotton plants. ...' <i>(1941,Valiant Love,159</i>)

>  <b>METTHEWS</b> <i>Celmisia </i>- New Zealand daisy-like flowers. The most popular are <i>C. coriacea </i> (Mountain Daisy)<i> C. Traversii</i> and <i>C. Lyallii. </i> <i>(1943,NZ Gardening Dictionary, The,36,(3ed) ed.</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Of these [sc. alpine flowers] the most common are the edelweiss, ... the white or yellow mountain daisy, of which there are no fewer than sixty varieties, and the mountain lily, which is native to the lower slopes of the Southern Alps. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),12</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Mountain Daisy - tikumu, <i>Celmisia coriacea </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,III/705-12</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> Tussock grass and beech forest had given way to monowai bushes and mountain daisies.... <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,91</i>)

>  <i>mountain daisy</i> perennial NZ herb. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,491</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Mountain daisies are very common in the mountains. ... Their scientific name is <i>Celmisia. </i> <i>(Jan 17, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  <b>PRINCE</b> The Saddle itself is ... studded with Ranunculus Lyalli (mountain lily), several forms of Celmesia (mountain daisy), and with Edelweiss plants ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,9</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Half way up the mountains some pretty flowers were gathered, amongst which were the mountain daisy deserved notice. <i>(1858,Reconnaissance Survey  S.Otago</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Thomson Reconnaissance Serv. Sthn Otago in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 335]

### Daisy Patch
 
 <i>n.</i> a field on Gallipoli

>  ... the brusquely-interrupted tea and the race over the paddock next to the `Daisy Patch' with one or two of us dropping in the machine-gun fire. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,9</i>) [NOTES: (Anzac: A Reverie)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> There [on 5 May 1915 in the front line] survivors were ordered to cross an exposed strip of ground, frosted with spring daisies, before [the Turk-held hill] Achi Baba - a place long to be cursed as `The daisy patch'. ... The daisies were soon less conspicuous than the blackening blood of dead and dying New Zealanders as more and more were urged across the open ground. ... After the daisy patch, demoralisation and anger was as rampant among New Zealand officers as among men. <i>(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,109</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> "You're not going to charge across the daisy patch, are you?" "Of course we are," the Aucklanders replied. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,125</i>)

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> Every battalion had its "Daisy Patch". <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,197</i>)

>  They went first over broken country, butafter a time came to a level plateaucovered with wild flowers. This was "TheDaisy Patch". There the men began tofall. <i>(Aug 18, 1915,Otago Witness,49</i>)

Here the vegetation, though dense, assumes a dwarf and stunted appearance, and consists largely of shrubby veronicas, white and yellow-flowered daisy trees [fn <i>Olearia</i> and <i>Senecio</i>.] <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Along the coastal fringes [of Stewart Island] it consists of a characteristic scrub known locally as `Mutton-bird scrub', the principal constituents ... being Puheretaiko (<i>Senecio Rotundifolius</i>), Tete-a-weka (<i>Olearia Angustifolia</i>) and Daisy Tree (<i>Olearia Colensoi</i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,xvii</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The whole [Waikaka] valley and the ... ridges were covered [in 1876] with luxurious white tussocks (<i>Poa caespitosa</i>) diversified with a few snow grass tussocks (<i>Danthonia raoulii</i>), trees and shrubs comprising Maukoko, or native broom (<i>Carmichaelia australia</i>[?lis]), Koromiko (<i>Veronica salicifo</i>), daisy tree (<i>Aleria [?Olearia] rirgata [?virgata]</i>), cottonwood (<i>Cassinia fulvida</i>), ... native thorns or matagouri and mingimingi (<i>Coprosma propinqua</i>). [check] <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,239</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fritz landed a daisy-cutter and the transport driver done his block and took his hook. <i>(1935,Slang Today</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Daisy-cutter. 4. A German shell that, on impact, burst instantaneously and scattered its fragments very close to the ground: New Zealanders': in G.W. Cf. grass-cutter, q.v. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,205</i>)

>  ... a `daisy-cutter,' [was] a shell bursting low. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang Press[Slang of the NZE.F.]]

>  Bunglebah must be a daisy-cutter if the time.. cabled over to the daily papers for the Epsom Handicap.. is correct. <i>(Sep 20, 1890,NZ Observer,15</i>)

### dak
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) marijuana [According to Chris Fowlie, a distinctly NZ term, more commonly heard in the provinces]

>  On the porhc dak curls away in a grey green highway / from Aotearoa to a nation on the spur of Africa. <i>(Jul 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  The women are passing a joint around - "dak" they call it these days. <i>(Aug 1991,Metro,68</i>)

>  If you want a cruisy, clear day, smoke some really good, locally grown dak. <i>(Jun 29, 1998,Dominion,13</i>)

>  NZ First MP Ron Mark remains Parliament's most vociferous opponent of decriminalisation, convinced that such move would see Parliament go to "dak and ruin". <i>(Mar 21, 2000,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Grow a bit of dak for the readies. <i>(Jun 18, 2000,NZ Catholic,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> marijuana

>  [Hdg] Dak and ruin amid mass inhalations.... New Zealand First's Ron Mark suggested that, since the Government was happy to back compulsory testing of police and defence personnel, it should set an example by introducing compulsory drug-testing of MPs and staff. "Or are we to allow Parliament to go to dak and ruin", he added., mystifying more innocent members with his pun on one of the many cool slang names for marijuana. <i>(Mar 16, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  So, when Ron Mark MP suggested drug testing our elected representatives in the wake of the latest joint-spotted-in-House brouhaha - memorably punning a threat of things going "to dak and ruin" in the process - it was unclear to an irony-challenged individual like myself whether he was serious. <i>(Apr 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  So, when Ron Mark MP suggested drug testing our elected representatives in the wake of the latest joint-spotted-in-House brouhaha - memorably punning a threat of things going "to dak and ruin" in the process - it was unclear to an irony-challenged individual like myself whether he was serious. <i>(Apr 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### daker
 
 <i>n.</i> a cheap fired-clay marble. [ca1930-35 West Coast children's (R.B. McLuskie) A cheap ceramic-clay marble - often when playing marbles children cry - `No dakes'.]

>  The bigger or more skilful boys won mostof my marbles off me, their glassies orsteelies (ball-bearings) blasting my claydakers ruthlessly out of the ring... The result was an ominous jungle, to be viewed through the peephole for a daker a time. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> daker marble; variant of Australian `dakes', marbles. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,24</i>)

### dakes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STEWART</b> Marbles.. was played with those beautiful and beautifully named taws such as agaties, cat's-eyes and glassies (glassies were to be found in the tops of lemonade bottles) and with the small clay cannon-fodder called dakes. You knocked your opponent's dakes out of the ring with your taw, and then you could keep them. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,100</i>) [NOTES: 1991 edition]

>  <b>PARK</b> With red faces and eyes bulging with an afternoon of stooping, they fired their taws, green and blue and brilliant yellow, at the squares richly lined with the weatherbeaten old clay soldiers which sold at twelve a penny and were known as dakes. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

### dakkies
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The dakkies of old were the sort of thing you could pull on over a pair of big thick socks to watch Sunday night television, not caring if you spoilt kebab sauce on your lap or Ribena down your front. <i>(Jun 8, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),10</i>)

### daks
 
 <i>n.</i> (trademark) pants; underpants or underdaks [(trousers in Australia; in NZ usually underpants)]

>  You just knew, likewise, there'd never be a moment when Wacko and Debbie would rip their daks off at the same time and in the same place. <i>(Oct 17, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  When she realised I wasn't putting my daks back on she turned her attention to Paul. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,12</i>)

### Dally
 
 <i>n.</i> A Dalmatian (naturalised or born in NZ); earlier Austrian (Northland and Auckland usage chiefly, as Dalmatians settled orig. on and around the gumfields, and were orig. termedAustrian), cf Scandy. [(occas. derog.)]

>  <b>McGEE</b> And dogs, madam, dings dallies dutchies dagoes boongs chinks gooks wogs commies coconuts coons kaffirs pommies polys woollys gollys spics spucs spastics the PYM queers and other long-haired fairies - yes! We love them all! <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,40</i>)

>  There was a time when our word was our bond, when you would help your fellow Dally. Why? Because he was <i>Nash</i>... When we visited our relative or friend in hospital we brought them good Dalmatian food, after all, who of the <i>Dallies</i> had a stomach for plain English food? [49] W e all knew who was a <i>Dally</i> - by their faces, real Slavonic and broad, with high cheekbones - by their special walk or gait which went with their breed. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/48</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> One of the two young Dallies who ran an orchard up at the back of Mrs Crump's place. ... had come out from Dalmatia. <i>(1946,That Summer,48</i>) [NOTES: (`Making of a New Zealander')]

>  The father's a Dally, but the mother is Irish, as far as I know. <i>(Jun 1955,Numbers,3/13</i>) [NOTES: (Duggan)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> You fullows guess I am bloody Dally from Dalmatia, eh? <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,190</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Dally: Dalmatian, Yugo-slav. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> New Zealanders whose forebears came from Dalmatia in what is now Yugoslavia. The Dallies are concentrated in the North of the North Island and tend to be farmers and winemakers. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,31</i>)

>  <b>ROSIER-JONES</b> Emma stiffens at the word `hori', but surely he's not referring to . . .[sic] `Emma has got Dally in in her y'know, Fred,' the manager says. `Coulda fooled me. Looks like the old tar-brush t'me.' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,129</i>)

>  We all roared at Billy T. James' Irish joke. Could we have more please - about coconuts, dallies, poms and horis? Chows too, if possible. <i>(Jun 15, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A8</i>)

>  Dally NZ colloq. 1 a. of Dalmatian origin. 2 n. Dalmatian immigrant. [abbr.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,186</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 120]]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> 'The Dallies don't need a compass,' they'd say. 'All they need is to be able to recognise Rangitoto. They never go out of sight of it.' <i>(2004,Hooked,204</i>)

>  And there was "a Dally from Dargaville" with a brand-new Commodore who wasn't that keen on being towed by it [sc. 1 1928 Chevrolet truck]. <i>(Apr 2002,AA Directions,No. 51 (Autumn) : 18</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He was young, tall, strong, handsome, with cut of features besides dark eyes and colouring which suggested Croat descent [43] what an earlier generation of New Zealanders would have called Austrian before the latter-day settling for Dally or Dalmatian. <i>(1975,More Than Enough,42</i>)

>  On local wine's academy awards night, cpomplete with statuettes known as "Dallies" for the biggest winners, the inevitable controversy over who won and who didn't is normally accepted as an inherent feature of the festivities. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> 'The Dallies made them,' said one of the workmen, 'way back in the 1930s.' <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,54</i>)

>  Northlanders were gumdiggers, Maori and 'Dallies', and none of these epithets was a compliment. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,42</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Two young dallies who ran an orchard ... had come out from Dalmatia. <i>Ibid.</i> 00 Apples do best on land like that, so it was apple-trees the Dallies had mainly gone in for. <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,10</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) Dalmatian [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Broke off his engagement ... and got this dog off an old Dally scrub-cutter. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,77</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> `She knows I like my vino,' he explained. `Where did you get it, Corrie? Is it dally stuff?' `Henderson special,' ... `Not dally though. ...' <i>(1965,Special Flower,38</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> Dally Day is a stayers day, a test of endurance not just for horses and riders but for the pounters as well. The Dallies arrive before the first race and are still there after the 10th. <i>(2000,First Past the Post,181</i>) [NOTES: Martin Tolich "Dally Day" from Metro Arpil 1966]

>  I was.. planning to take off on my big OE, maybe even check out the homeland, see where those tough old Dallie winemakers had come from... <i>(Dec 28, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D12</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> wishing to catch poems he could salt / and peg out on a line in the wind / to dry like a Dally fish <i>(1987,Kevin Ireland : Selected Poems,54</i>)

>  There was social card-playing and Dally bowls with a round bowl - not off-centre - and grape wines, and dances like the kolo, done in a circle. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,40</i>)

### Dally Alley
 
 <i>n.</i> Lincoln Road in Henderson

>  There was a time when the orchards and vineyards of Henderson and Oratia were ours, when Lincoln Rd was commonly known as <i>Dally Alley</i>. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/50</i>)

### Dally cabbage
 
 <i>n.</i> [1997 Maureen (?) Babich on TV 1 : The Way we Were 30 Dec. spoke of everyone in her Dally area growing Dally Cabbages which she didn't like and still doesn't.]

### Dally dog
 
 <i>n.</i> Dalmatian immigrant

>  Then there was their treatment of the Croatian migrants who arrived at the turn of the last century to dig gum in the Far North. Being not quite white enough, they were called, among other things, white Chinamen and Dally dogs. <i>(Jul 17, 2002,NZ Herald,A15</i>)

### Dally fleet
 
 <i>n.</i> a group of fishing vessels owned by Dalmatians

>  'The Dallies don't need a compass,' they'd say. 'All they need is to be able to recognise Rangitoto. They never go out of sight of it.' While there may have been a small amount of truth in the jibe in the 1930s, when the 'Dally fleet' was inclined to be the butt of jokes by other members of the fishing community, it was not the case in the 1940s. <i>(2004,Hooked,204</i>)

### Dally mafia
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive)

>  Tim's [Shadbolt] Team sank the Dally mafia without trace in yesterday's local body election. Mayor Tim recorded a massive 30,488 votes, compared with nearest rival Tony Covic, whopolled a paltry 7481. <i>(Oct 12, 1986,Sunday News,1</i>)

>  Our society, which represents a very large percentage of Yugoslav Dalmatians, is concerned that any paper can slander our community by calling them Dally mafia ... <i>(Nov 2, 1986,Sunday News,19</i>)

### Dally plonk
 
 <i>n.</i> wine made by Dalmatian growers in Henderson, Auckland, formerly sold cheaply at vineyards

>  <b>STEAD</b> In Mike's childhood New Zealand was still a beer-and-whisky cutlrue, and the local wine was popularly known as Dally plonk. <i>(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,19</i>)

>  You can buy an awful lot of Dally plonk for four pounds. <i>(1950,Landfall,14/127</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> And I make Dally plonk, all right. My plonk here is not best, soil too sour, but is all right. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,190</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> On Sunday we ... had a barbeque on the beach with the fowls and a bottle of cheap Dally plonk. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,111</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I do recall one evening, drunk/ In Devonport on Dally plonk, <i>(1968,Collected Poems,429,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Dally - Dalmatian, made by Yugoslav immigrants; e.g. `Dally plonk' - Dalmatian wine <i>(1980,History of New Zealand,332</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Dallie plonk the often rough and usually red wine sold here mid-century, when most Kiwis were beer drinkers, before the Dalmatians got their wine act together and Kiwis atarted drinking the grape, c.1960s. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,34</i>)

>  <b>RENNIE</b> His only experience of wine was the rough, home-made red Northlanders called `Dally plonk'. see cits at Dally <i>(1988,Super Man,167</i>)

>  <i>(Dally) plonk </i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

### Dalmatian
 
 <i>n.</i> an immigrant settler in North Auckland from Dalmatia; one descended from a Dalmatian settler.

>  We told everyone that we were Dalmatians and later Yugoslavs and we all stated this with pride - after all, this respect had been earned with much sweat and just as many tears, in the gold fields of the South, on the wild gum fields of the North and in the mountains of Coromandel and in the swamps and drains of the Hauraki Plains. There was a time when the distinction in our community, between a Yugoslav and a Dalmatian was alomost impossible to discern. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/48</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> I chose vineyards where the Dalmatians didn't use shotguns. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,44</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Coates: We have difficulty in the north in connection with the Dalmatians. <i>(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,148</i>) [NOTES: 1916 Gordon Coates speech in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 148]

>  Twelve people at work [in Waikato]. English French Swiss Dalmatian. <i>(Mar 22, 1919,Chudleigh Diary,465,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Sweat rolled down the Dalmatian's matted chest. Martinovitch came from the gum fields, and worked as if God were in him. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,128,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Then one little Chinese moved close . . .[sic] not that he'd have spoken to her, it was the Dalmatian quarter [of Auckland] where all the thick voices said whiningly: `Good night girlie' ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,332</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> The evening before, a kindly Dalmatian had cut up a sheep's carcase for me ... <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,187</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> A Dalmatian once told a friend of mine why he left Europe. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,9</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> Besides the [gum-]diggers, more especially the Austrians - or Dalmatians as they later insisted on being called - paid well for the service; ... <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,59,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> ... an excursion to the vineyards that the swarthy Dalmatian stock have sown ... and brought to fruition in the ways of their forebears on the hilly slopes that carry the tang of the Adriatic Sea. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,15</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Towards the close of the nineteenth century some Yugoslavs, or Dalmatians, discovered that, by hard work, good wages could be made on the gumfields. <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff,31</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> ... compounded on the Dalmation[sic] coast before he migrated ... the dialect of his Dalmation[sic] village birthplace ... <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,8</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> [Dalmation] `There were gumfields all around here, weren't there?' `That's right. Crawling with Dalmations. Only they were called Austrians then. ...' <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,156</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> ...the [kauri gum] industry was largely in the hands of the Dalmatians who came to dig and remained to settle as excellent colonists. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,85</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> ... the Austrians soon established themselves as fine, hard-working and law-abiding people. ... When the map of Europe was changed after the First World War they changed with it and became known as Dalmatians. <i>(1970,Way Up North,35</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> ... I remember two [stories] about Maoris and one about a Yugoslav or [45] Dalmatian, as they were then termed, told by a foreman who had come from a North Island job. <i>(1975,Milford Road,44</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He was young, tall, strong, handsome, with cut of features besides dark eyes and colouring which suggested Croat descent [43] what an earlier generation of New Zealanders would have called Austrian before the latter-day settling for Dally or Dalmatian. <i>(1975,More Than Enough,42</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Actually Joe, the Dalmatian proprietor, who hardly remembers his real name himself anymore, never leaves his home and business entirely unattended even during national emergencies. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,3</i>)

>  Our society, which represents a very large percentage of Yugoslav Dalmatians, is concerned that any paper can slander our community by calling them Dally mafia ... <i>(Nov 2, 1986,Sunday News,19</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> Austrian was our first name here and it stuck. But it wasn't the name of a country the New Zealanders invoked when they said it; it was the name of a fear. ... With every shipload ofDalmatians coming to work on the gumfields that fear grew. ... But to my child's mind it was all a confussion - all those names. Austrian. Dalmatian. Some said Croatian. And now Yugoslavs. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,24</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> For ours was a two-way world, and I'm still not sure what I am, New Zealander or Dalmatian first. But I know this, the scent of the tea-tree will always say Home to me, calling me back to dusty North Auckland roads. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,138</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Nick was saying he was a New Zealander, but he knew he wasn't a New Zealander. And he knew he wasn't a Dalmatian any more. <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,13</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> No use for Mr Mitchell to boast that he'd been one of our marchers in Auckland's Queen Street that day in 1914; the Dalmatians had carried an Austrian flag all right, lifted it high and burnt it for [24] all of New Zealand to know what we thought of being called Austrian. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,23</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> of a Dalmatian.

>  The country lifestyle reflected in many of the stories has gone now, and the characters - the swaggers and hawkers and Dalmatian gumdiggers and stern Presvbyterian fathers - have gone with it. <i>(Jul 10, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),86</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Late in the 1800s a lot of Dalmatian men left Europe, looking for a new life away from chronic unpleasantness in the Balkans. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,45</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> When we declined their beer they triumphantly produced some sherry from a Dalmatian vine-yard ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,32,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> The little Dalmatian manager had sauntered up through the tables. <i>(1960,Tangahano,96</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The Jar [title] ... I'd bought a peter of wine/ From the owner of a Dalmatian vineyard,/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,392,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Why? I said, there are plenty of Dalmatian girls out there. I bet you could get plenty of New Zealand girls too. <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,12</i>)

### Dalmatian bean
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Her pride and joy is the Dalmatian bean. She says just about everyone in the north grows this bean, which has been here for at least 100 years... It is a tall-growing runner bean. <i>(Apr 29, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

### dalo
 
 <i>n.</i> taro, a root vegetable

>  It's easy to get lost in the lush dalo fields of Muaniweni, a farming settlement.. about an hour out of Suva. <i>(Nov 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

### dam
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ (as in Aust. and Brit. dial.- Sc Yks Derb eAng) the water held back by the barrier, as well as the barrier itself. R Mason notes that one swims in a dam rather than walks on it; and that this has been Auckland usage from c1922.

>  dam n. an embankment for restraining the flow of water; the water so collected. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,3</i>)

>  A question on the Evans Bay dam in which a six-year-old boy was drowned ... was raised ... at a meeting of the Wellington City Council last night. <i>(Oct 17, 1963,Dominion,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> whalers' [(whalers')]

>  <b>GRADY</b> Dam - Mother whale in lactose state, with calf in company. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>) [NOTES: (Cook Strait Whalers' glossary)]

### dam dropping
 
 <i>n.</i> an extreme sport (see 2000 citation)

>  The Waingongoro River near Hawera is home to the successful dam-dropping and white water sledging adventure. <i>(Mar 19, 2001,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Hawera has its own high adventure treat for tourists in the form of "dam dropping" on the swift Waingongoro River. I politely declined this white-water sledging invitation... Fact is, I didn't much like the thought of leaping off a dam six metres high. <i>(2000,Directions,Summer 2000 No. 46 : 29</i>)

### dama
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  By the early 1900s dama were believed to be extinct on the Australian mainland, but DNA tests proved that the dama that Governor Grey released on Kawau in 1870 were originally taken from South Australia, and therefore a pure strain of the lost species. Kawau dama (Aussies say "tammar") are now an international star for forensic genetics, but it won't save them. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### dama wallaby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Aussies heard of the 2005 eradication target and immediately ordered 30 dama wallabies from Kawau. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### damper
 
 <i>n.</i> a control on a stove

>  <b>McALPINE</b> I tried to cook for weeks on that wretched thing till I found out the damper had cracked and fallen into the soot at the bottom. <i>(1998,Passionate Pen, The,157</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> food



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a scone or unleavened bread cooked in fat over an open fire or in the coals [ a staple food for early farm and station personnel.]

>  <b>WELD</b> As occasion might offer, he should not think it a degradation, gentleman born though he might be, to heap fresh logs on the fire, put on the soup pot, or to bake a damper in the ashes. <i>(1853,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,139</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> What the host always furnishes is tea (at every meal), damper, i.e. a sort of dough cake baked in the ashes, very good if well made and fried pork. <i>(1847,Letters to His Father, 1843-1851,00</i>) [NOTES: Quoted in Miller; Early Victorian NZ, p. 140]

>  Bread in the shape of a very palatable kind of damper, or unleavened cake, is now an essential part of native food. <i>(Jun 9, 1849,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  Shew that the constant use of "damper" amongst colonial shepherds (being a perpetual feast of unleavened bread) renders them just as likely to be the lost tribe of Israel as the Maoris. <i>(Mar 27, 1860,Taranaki Punch,2</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> As they sat and ate their simple fare - "damper" baked on the red-hot embers of their fire.. their conversation was of their "claim". <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,83</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> DAMPER. This requires the same scone mix. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,48</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> `Damper' is a dough made from wheat-flour and water without yeast, which is simply pressed flat, and baked in the ashes; according to civilized notions, rather hard of digestion ... <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),284fn</i>)

>  I'd flood it and have boats and cook damper and do a sort of Huck Finn thing. <i>(Feb 1997,North & South,19</i>)

>  Good damper keeps for a couple of days and makes excellent toast. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  And this, too, to colonials who had known most of them what it was to hunger and thirst after "damper" or "johnnycake" warranted to stay by a man for three days at a stretch! - to long vehemently for Maori hen only three removes from a watertight boot soaked in hair oil! - who knew most of them what it had been to partake of '"Sunday duff " boiled in a kerosine tin. <i>(Oct 16, 1890,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> That is what is called fat damper, otherwise scones, a Bushman's delicacy. <i>(Jan 1856,Journal,214</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980) Sewell Journal 1853-1856]

>  <b>PAUL</b> .. night after night on the damp cold ground, mutton, damper and tea ... <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,24</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> These and other employments, such as baking my dampers ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,31</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Boundary-keepers' food consisted of the ubiquitous damper, boiled and fried mutton and tea. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,141</i>)

>  And weka, uncared for in the settlements! Catch it, as Mrs Glass would say, at Rotoiti or Cape Foulwind; stuff it with sage and onion (for even these condiments accompany the epicurean explorer), roast it on a stick, watch it for half an hour at daybreak, spattering and hissing between you and the fire as you make the damper or pancake, while your companions are snoring under their blankets around you, and then serve it upon the saucepan lid. <i>(Oct 3, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,123</i>)

>  Being regularly knocked up, I meant camping for the night, so I laid down, and took a feed of his damper, mutton, tea and sugar. <i>(Mar 17, 1855,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  Mr. Colenso having shown so much sympathy with the sufferings of the clerk of the roads, might expend, without appearing in the least ostentatious a little of that same milk of human kindness with which he is so completely saturated, upon the unfortunate pork and damper fed surveyors. <i>(Apr 7, 1860,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

As their name suggests the adults of both sexes are a uniform black in colour with a prominent white ear spot that can be turned [239] off and on like a signal light ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,238,1984 ed.</i>)

### Dan Doolin spawn
 
 <i>n.</i> [also Dan Doolin 'bait]

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Fishermen on some West Coast rivers speak of "Dan-Doolin spawn" or "whalefeed". <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,21</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Whitebait in pristine condition are beautiful... There are local names for them, too - elephant ears and Dan Doolin 'bait (which isn't true whitebait at all), and cucumbers (which are actually smelt, Retropinna retropinna, or paraki). <i>(1989,Homeplaces,104</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Tiny, largely transparent bullies are also amongst the migrants ... common bully (<i>Gobiomorphus cotidianus</i>), redfinned bully (<i>G. huttoni</i>), and bluegilled bully (<i>G. hubbsi</i>). These are sometimes caught in large quantities by whitebaiters. The fishermen call them `Dan Doolin Spawn' or `Whalefeed'. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,189</i>)

### dance a haka

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... dance a haka: to celebrate <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Dance a haka. To exhibit joy, `dance with pleasure': New Zealand coll.: since ca. 1890. B., 1941. The haka is a Maori ceremonial dance, wild and impressive. Cf. the Australian corroboree, n. and v. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang,1027</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>dandelion [=] beaked hawksbeard: catsear: hawkbit: hawksbeard: hawkweed [s] ...dandelion [s] Taraxacum officinale</i> Weber ex Wiggers <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,20</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Taraxacum magellanicum</i> NATIVE DANDELION ... Hairless rosettes like [the dandelion] but usually much smaller ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,214</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Taraxacum magellanicum</i>

### dandy
 
 <i>n.</i> (a vehicle) [as SOED dandy-cart?]

>  <b>ASPDEN</b> The joys of helping Dad to deliver the milk.. with the assistance of the dandy, were shared by all the older children. <i>(1981,New Life, A,15</i>) [NOTES: From 1865]

### Dansker
 
 <i>n.</i> a Danish settler

>  Many of the original "Danskers" have sold out. <i>(Oct 15, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### danthonia
 
 <i>adj.</i> . [loose comb. danthonia country, turf, tussock.]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Many an aspersion is cast at a danthonia knoll waving white with rank growth, and the grass is condemned as being unpalatable, whereas the person casting the aspersion is probably standing on the very same grass eaten down so well that it is to him `English grass'. ... the better English grasses are seldom seen to much advantage when growing within a danthonia or a browntop sward. I consider the stunted, ill-natured herbage of the better English grasses is less palatablethan the young, fresh herbage of the second rate species. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,134,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> She sat faced out to the paddock of dry danthonia grass, <i>(1955,Collected Stories,100,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Soils of poor quality both upland and lowland are commonly known as <i>Danthonia</i> country, by which is inferred their poor quality. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,133</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> With the onset of autumn rains, the bared ground, which was clothed originally by the once-dominant danthonia turf, is replaced by <i>annuals</i>. <i>Ibid.</i> 88 [Table 1] Danthonia tussock <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,31,1970 ed.</i>)

>  The property when taken over consisted of 28 acres of sandy gumland hills in weak browntop and danthonia pasture, reverting back to manuka. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,17</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> We climbed the boundary fence and our hobnails bit into the smooth-stalked danthonia grass of the lower slopes of the Kitchener Flat. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,144</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A native group of tussock-grasses; genus to which many spp. of tussock-grasses belong

>  Snow-grass, carpet grass, hassock grass. <i>(1876,TrNZI</i>)

>  <i>danthonia</i> A large class of Australian and New Zealand grasses of which one is valued as a pasture grass; the largest is the `snow-grass' of the New Zealand mountains. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),109,8th ed. ed.</i>)

>  <i>danthonia</i> n. tufted perennial NZ and Australian pasture-grass. [<i>E. Danthoine</i>, botanist] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,187</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 121]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a large genus of tufted perennial grasses, common to New Zealand and Australia.

>  <i>Danthonia semi-annularis</i>, a variable grass found in nearly all soils and situations throughout the colony. <i>(1871,TrNZI,2/104</i>) [NOTES: [OED2]]

>  ... coarse tussock-grasses. (Danthonia) <i>(1872,TrNZI,3/416</i>)

>  a native grass, strictly <i>Danthonia semiannularis </i> <i>(1910,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,vii</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> This must not lead the farmer to suppose that `danthonia', as all these different forms are called in the papers and by seed-merchants, will ever replace ryegrass, cocksfoot, or red-clover in the better land. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,87</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> During the past few years the use of danthonia, the best of the native grasses, has considerably increased. <i>Danthonia pilosa</i> is the best type, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,103</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> DANTHONIA (<i>Danthonia semiannularis</i> and <i>D.pilosa</i>) [(<i>Notadanthonia</i> spp. syn. <i>Danthonia</i> sp.)] is a native grass, of more than one species, often occurring as a weed in permanent pastures in dry country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,38,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> On hard hill-tops and narrow ridges and knobs, danthonia and microloena had grouped themselves in little companies. <i>(1921,Tutira,167</i>)

>  ... the land becoming gradually less productive owing to the replacement of good by poor pasture plants such as redtop (<i>Agrostis</i>), Danthonia and Chewings fescue. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,81</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Of the grass associations, danthonia may be regarded as an indigenous induced association and browntop an exotic induced one; ... <i>Ibid.</i> Appendix II 354 Danthonia - <i>Notodanthonia</i> spp. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,247,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> In time of drought/ The danthonia shines like a flame. <i>(1952,Three Poems,59</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There were the scrublands of blackened second growth, the pastures of danthonia and browntop ... the marginal land of bracken, hebe and flax, the reproachful columns of burnt-over bush ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)

>  <i>danthonia [s] [12 Notodanthonia sp(p)] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,21</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> DANTHONIA (<i>Rytidosperma</i> sp(p)). ... Some species are native but several were probably introduced from Australia with early shipments of sheep. <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,50</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Unless you walk over this country [Central Otago] you'll miss the native grasses, like danthonia and the blue tussock. To a Merino, one mouthful of this hard feed [13] is worth 10 of soft greenfeed. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I started a fire when I could scarcely walk, the danthonia dry as a county chairman's speech. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,116</i>)

>  On poor tussock country .. the use of danthonia invariably increases the carrying-capacity. <i>(Mar 20, 1918,NZ Journal of Agriculture,135</i>)

>  .. the land carries danthonia, mainly, with manuka and matagouri on the lower levels. <i>(Oct 6, 1938,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

>  The following applications for patents were gazetted last week:- .. an invention for manufacturing brushes and brooms out of New Zealand grass, named danthonia raoulia, more commonly known as "snow grass," by Henry James Shaw, of Christchurch. <i>(Dec 15, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The herbage was principally the large coarse snow grass (Danthonia Raoulii); to this we set fire in several places, which rapidly spread over the rough hill side. <i>(Jun 22, 1894,North Otago Times,1</i>)

>  Some of the land is is [sic] poor, but danthonia is making its useful way through scrub and fern. <i>(Apr 2, 1904,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The open mountain top is covered with a growth of grass, but it is of a very coarse character, being no other than the <i>Danthonia Cunninghamii</i> and <i>Danthonia Raoulia</i>, or snow grasses. <i>(Feb 8, 1873,Otago Witness,3</i>)

### danthonia country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hill country that grows danthonia as its principal pasture grass.

>  This flag is neglected by sheep; they prefer the shorter green grass. In danthonia country, therefore, as in rough bush country, it is profitable to run a limited number of cattle ... <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,15</i>)

>  .. if we are going to dilly-dally around with one ewe or less to the acre on danthonia country, we will never get a good sward. <i>(Aug 19, 1948,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

### danthonia-dominant
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> One direct outcome, however, of this increased carrying capacity was to overcrowd the stock camps on the knolls and ridges and to submit the former danthonia-dominant turf to such intensity of wear by treading, scratching, and rubbing, together with rank fertilising of the camps by dung and urine, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,31,1970 ed.</i>)

### DAP
 
<b>1</b> <i>abbr.</i> (Rural) di-ammonium phosphate, a widely used fertiliser.

>  I've put some DAP on my best paddocks around the house. <i>(Jul 14, 1983,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  Local advice on recommended rates of DAP should be sought. <i>(1983,NZ Farmer,16</i>)

### daphne
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DUNCAN & DAVIES</b> (And) <i>Pimelia longifolia </i> <i>(1917,Descriptive Catalogue</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN & DAVIES</b> ... (And) <i>Pimelia longifolia </i> <i>(1925,NZ Native Trees</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pimelia</i> spp.; usu. with distinguishing epithet, freq. Maori, native, New Zealand; also Maori, native daphne. [[Spec. use of daphne--> for daphne-like shrubs.]]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Another very floriferous shrub to be seen on the [204] drier hillsides of Nelson and Marlborough and of North Island hills is known as the NATIVE DAPHNE or TARANGA (<i>Pimelia longifolia </i>). [chk pimelia] <i>(1961,Flora NZ,200,1929, 204 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> We returned from North Canterbury with a spread of native daphne donated from a garden ... a few miles from Rangiora. <i>(1986,Sea of Words,190</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> New Zealand Daphne - taranga, <i>Pimelia longifolia </i> <i>(1933,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,III/705-12</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Similar in general appearance to the hebes with spreading four-ranked leaves are a number of species of <i>Pimelea</i> or New Zealand `daphnes'. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,168</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Pimelea lyallii </i>SAND DAPHNE ... Low, sprawling sub-shrub ... Flowers white, strongly scented, attractively clustered at branchlet tips. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,114</i>)

### darg
 
 <i>n.</i> [EDD. DARG sb. & v. Sc. Ire. Nhb. Dur. Cum. Win. Yks. Lan. 1. sb. A day's work; the amount of work done in a day. Also used fig... 2. a certain quantity of work, whether more or less than can be done in a day; a set task.]

>  [Hdg] "The Darg"... The darg is a means used by coal miners and men employed in other occupations, to provide work for three men where the employer would require only two... A similar system is being adopted by the Amalgamated Society of Engineers. <i>(Apr 15, 1919,King Country Chronicle,7</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> For a good many years I have run a daily column in the Southland Daily News called "The Day's Darg," a mixture of fact, comment, verse and humour. "Darg" is a coalmining term meaning a norm of work.Theoretically, when you have finished your darg, you go home, but somehow that has never worked for me. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,165</i>)

>  The perusal of Hansard is not part of my daily darg, thanks be! <i>(Jul 29, 1924,Otago Witness,1</i>)

### dark
 
 <i>adj.</i> shady (See [face](../F#face))

### dark chocolate
 
 <i>n.</i> used to distinguish the darker-coloured (often termed `energy') from `milk' chocolate; = Brit. plain chocolate. [[Cf. 1987 May p.c. Dr L. Bauer `Milk' and `plain' chocolate used on the London soap-opera `Eastenders'.] ca1940 at least: Havelock.]

>  <b>FRAME</b> And on my last day in London, Ben, the poet, paid me a visit and after we had our tea and Peak Frean's dark chocolate digestive biscuits we began to talk about chess ... <i>(1985,Envoy from Mirror City,34</i>)

>  Rich and firm and brooding, its loaded with currants and plums with a lick of liquorice, leather, and Marching chocolate, if that is what they still call dark chocolate. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  While velvety milk chocolate has received most attention in dairy-festooned New Zealand.. dark chocolate is the silkier sweet. <i>(Dec 1989,Cuisine,50</i>)

### dark face
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a south-facing hillside. (See [shady face](../S#shady-face))

>  From a departmental trial of inoculated [sic] clover seed sown with and without fertiliser on a sunny face and a dark face, it has been found that fertiliser is necessary to enable a quick establishment of clover plants, particularly on the sunny face, though a reasonable strike on the unfertilised dark face was obtained. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

>  The hogget block on Tara Hills consists of four faces, two sunny and two dark. <i>(Nov 21, 1963,NZ Farmer,54</i>)

### dark ghost shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka ghost shark; <i>Hydrolagus novaezealandiae</i>]

>  [species name] ghost shark .. [scientific name] <i>Hydrolagus novaezelandiae </i>.. [other name(s)] dark ghost shark, pearl fillets, N.Z. ghost shark, chimaera <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>DARK GHOST SHARK Hydrolagus novaezelandiae</i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,81,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] ghost shark .. [scientific name] <i>Hydrolagus novaezelandiae</i> .. [other name(s)] dark ghost shark, pearl fillets, N.Z. ghost shark, chimaera <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Dark ghost shark Hydrolagus novaezelandiae </i>... The commonest New Zealand member of this family (Chimaeridae), although only occasionally seen by coastal fisherman. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,39</i>)

### dark horses
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of biscuit

### dark rock shell
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. white rock shell]

>  <b>POWELL</b> DARK ROCK SHELL (<i>Lepsia haustrum</i>). Smaller and thinner shelled than <i>scalaris</i>, and is easily recognised by the large open mouth with a conspicuous brown patch, ... The Maori name is Kaeo and Ngaeo. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,28</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rock shell, dark .. .. kaeo, ngaeo .. .. <i>Haustrum haustorium </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

### dark shed
 
 <i>n.</i> a darkened shed or area for trapping or handling deer.

>  <b>YEREX</b> On one occasion I was mustering some newly-captured deer into the race and dark shed of one of our traps and by necessity. [99] In building your trap include the wing and race leading to the dark shed, and situate the dark shed so that a vehicle can be driven up to the outside. Deer will go down a race into a dark hole of natural appearance ... we built races and sheds. <i>(1982,Farming of Deer,98</i>)

### dark side
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the south-facing aspect of a paddock or hillside. (See [dark face or shady face](../D#dark-face-or-shady-face))

>  <b>HALL</b> It's September and still pretty cold, so the sheep tend to stay on the warm northern slopes. These shepherds are approaching from the south - the dark side, we call it. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,18</i>)

### dark skin
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori (See [also darkie](../A#also-darkie))

>  <b>POWELL</b> This concluded my first commercial transaction with a dark skin, and each party was well satisfied. <i>(1966,Chatham Islands,28</i>)

### darky
 
 <i>n.</i> an offensive application of the sense `(American) negro' (recorded in NZ from 1872 for a black sea-cook) to Polynesians, occas. as a term of address, or as a nickname.

>  Newly graduated police officers talking about "darkies" and "coons". <i>(Mar 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> `Halloa', cried Harry, as we pulled up our horses about a mile from the pah, `there go a string of darkies a-head, ...' <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,31</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> In those days [c1939] racism was more blatant than it is now. ... I had become accustomed to being addressed as `snow', `darky', and even `Hori', often by adults who would have been surprised had they known they were giving offence. And at high school Stuart was known as Sam (short for Sambo). But `nigger' was the ultimate, the unforgivable epithet. <i>(1984,Island to Island,93</i>)

### darl
 
 <i>n.</i> darling

>  The kisses stopped. Carol whispered urgently, furtively. `Tahuri, darl, relax. Don't worry, it's only me ... <i>(1989,Awekotuku Tahuri,48</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> Here my Billy, she says, here my darl. [To a parrot.] <i>(1990,Snow in Spain,11</i>)

### darnel
 
 <i>n.</i> sometimes called drake

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> DARNEL (<i>Lolium temulentum</i>), sometimes called drake, is an annual a good deal like Italian rye in appearance, not uncommon [occasionally found] in cultivated fields and waste places in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,47,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>darnel [s] Lolium temulentum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,21</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [Old Dart](../O#old-dart))

 <i>n.</i> in early perceptions, a Maori weapon

 <i>n.</i> just my dart, just the thing for me; just my choice, ideal; fancy, favourite. [(obs or hist.)]

 <i>n.</i> daughter

 <i>n.</i>

### Darth Vader's pencil box
 
 <i>n.</i> the BNZ building in Wellington

>  It's Friday, 7.15am. The sun streams across Wellington harbour directly into the 21st floor dealing room of the BNZ. The building forex dealers term Darth Vadar's pencil box. <i>(May 19, 1985,NZ Times,18</i>)

>  I am truly appalled by the effrontery of that foreigner from Southland, Mr Trevor Agnew. Especially with his reference to the BNZ building as being "Darth Vader's pencil box". <i>(May 5, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### Darwin's barberry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The council will eradicate boneseed and Darwin's barberry, evergreen buckthorn, hornwort, nodding thistle, mist flower and climbing asparagus (snakefeather) without charge in specified areas, while property owners are responsible for eradicating cathedral bells, old man's beard, banana passionfruit and ginger. <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E10</i>)

>  Chilean Flame Creeper, / pink flowereed Silk Tree, / Darwin's Barberry, / Buddleia, butterfly haunted. <i>(Aug 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>) [NOTES: Strangers, Elizabeth Isichei]

>  While there are several annual campaigns for pest plants such as Darwin's barberry and Old Man's Beard, there is nothing for similar noxious weeds. <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a spreading Berberis shrub, also known as American barberry.

>  <b>ALLEN</b> Darwin's barberry is an aggressive shrub weed of forest edges and low secondary forest in eastern Otago and Southland. <i>(1989,Fruiting in Darwin's barberry,1</i>)

>  <b>BEAVEN</b> Darwin's barberry (<i>Berberis darwinii</i>) is a spiny, evergreen shrub that has bright yellow-orange flowers and dark purple berries, native to southern Chile. <i>(2002,Darwin's barberry eradication,3</i>)

>  Darwin's barberry is spreading in several areas of Taranaki. Also known as American barberry, it is native to the Patagonia region of South America. Darwin's barberry is very invasive and can form dense colonies ... <i>(Jun 12, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,11</i>)

### dash
 
 <i>n.</i> of lemonade (added to a glass of beer):constr. a dash.

>  ... beer and a dash. <i>(Nov 11, 1905,NZ Truth,21/1/6</i>)

>  `Three straights and a dash?' she [sc. the barmaid] said to the Maoris, and gathered the empty handles in her fingers. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,88,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wilson `It was Easter')]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> dash: Mild expletive uttered when lemonade is found in beer. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

### dashing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> Dashing seems to be a shortened form of pan dashing, extracting gold with a pan about thirteen or fourteen inches in diameter. It is tempting to wonder whether the expression `he's done his dash' ... is connected with the gold-miner's dashing. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,152</i>)

### date
 
 <i>n.</i> Anus, arse (in pl. occas. balls, testicles, poss. f. n2.). -->ca1935 (esp. schoolboys') Up your date for 2/8

>  <b>McGILL</b> date anus; `get off your date' was common army advice WWII. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble-players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### date mussel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> BIVALVES ... 9. Date mussel, <i>Zelithophaga. op. cit</i> (?) <i>Ibid.</i> 80 A third borer, the date mussel <i>Zelithophaga</i> ..., excavates hard rocks by means of an acid secretion. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,78 Fig 20</i>)

### daw-dawn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MONEY</b> By the time we had boiled our billy, at the daw-dawn the next morning ... we were surrounded by an eager mob ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,98</i>)

### dawk
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [dork](../D#dork))

>  <b>STEWART</b> `You cheating lot of Ag dawks, cut it out and play properly.' To which the 5Ag [team] fullback ... called back. `Up you too ...' <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,55</i>)

### dawn address
 
 <i>n.</i> (on Anzac Day)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> ... each year on Anzac Day he emerged to lead the R.S.A. parade and make a dawn address at the lawn cemetery in town. <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,68</i>)

### Dawn Parade
 
 <i>n.</i> Anzac Day dawn ceremony

>  <b>BRUCE</b> Hearts remembering their ever-living dead. Dawn Parade [title] <i>(1964,Home Is the Warrior</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Nancy Bruce Home Is the Warrior in 1979 The Iron Hand 7]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Anzac Day, 25 April, brings a dawn parade for returned soldiers and memorial church services. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,155</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... the vicar would drone on about this Anzacer Day, this day of remembrancer. Years ago and now the rum and milk after Dawn Parade. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,176</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> That year I went into the Anzac service myself. Mr Meechin had no car. My father and I took him in for the dawn parade in the utility. When I was really toung I thought Dawn Parade was agirl, but as I grew up I came to realise the solemn implications of the Anzac Day ceremonial. <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,72</i>)

>  Although he attended Dawn Parades on Anzac day, Hart always felt uneasy about the R.S.A. As a Gallipoli veteran he found it difficult to relate to the majority of the R.S.A., who had joined up after 1915. <i>(1988,Great Adventure,130</i>)

>  It was gratifying to see the crowd had doubled at this year's Dawn Parade. <i>(May 8, 2002,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  This morning it was Anzac morning, kids home from school and no chance for a muse. It was the first time I'd planned on seeing the dawn parade instead of just reading about it. <i>(Dec 29, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  ...the past 30 years have found me tucked up warmly in bed while others were shivering at the Dawn Parade. <i>(Apr 19, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  The introduction of "dawn parades" similar to those held in Australia was advanced at a meeting of the Returned Soldiers' Association [in Dunedin] by Mr. C. L. Calvert... They were peculiarly Australian in origin, their particular association with that country being derived from the fact that the Australians went over the top at Anzac at dawn. <i>(May 12, 1938,Evening Post,15</i>)

### Dawn Parader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTCHINGS</b> It was the Dawn Paraders. The R.S.A. men marching. Marching in honour of their fallen comrades. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,34</i>)

### dawn raid
 
 <i>n.</i> an early-morning raid on a house suspected of harbouring Polynesian overstayers or others who had overstayed their immigration permits.

>  But then I remember the infamous dawn raids. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,71</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Besides there was the advantage that these [Pacific island] people were not admitted permanently, and if things got tough, we could ... round up, with dawn raids in the best totalitarian tradition, those who had overstayed and forcibly depart them. <i>(1981,Straight from the Shoulder,9</i>)

>  LIGHTNING and unexpected company takeovers - dawn raids - could become outlawed under proposed changes to existing takeover law. Securities Commission chairman Mr Colin Patersondescribed dawn raids as `entirely unacceptable'. <i>(Oct 6, 1983,Dominion,9/5</i>)

>  A stereotype of Pacific Islanders as overstayers, established during the `dawn raids' of a decade ago, remains an influence in the Labour Department's immigration division, the Race Relations Conciliator, Mr Wally Hirsh, said today. <i>(Aug 12, 1986,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Her confidence in her Pacific Island lineage was unsettled by the dawn raids in the 1970s when the Government decided to crack down on overstayers from the Islands. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 35</i>)

>  He [Dr Ian Prior] addressed a meeting at Newtown in 1976 called to protest against dawn raids, a mechanism sanctioned by the Muldoon government to extract overstayers from their homes and deport them. <i>(Feb 26, 2009,Dominion Post,B3</i>)

>  Kightley's plays include <i>Naked Samoans Talk About Their Knives</i> and <i>Dawn Raids.</i> <i>(May 8, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  [He]wrote and performed a series of plays - <i>Fresh Off The Boat</i> premiered in Wellington in 1993 and was followed by a host of others including <i>Sons, Tattau</i> and <i>Dawn Raids</i>. <i>(Oct 17, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),28</i>)

>  The programme.. had some hair-raising tales of individual and institutional racism in the prejudices directed .. against Pacific Islanders, with some vivid recall of the "dawn raids" of two decades back. <i>(Apr 9, 2000,NZ Catholic,19</i>)

>  Perhaps the difference is that an FPP Parliament does not change as the result of dawn raids. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  The haphazard targeting of the letters has angered Cook Islanders.. who say it harks back to the racism of former prime minister Robert Muldoon's dawn raids. <i>(Aug 3, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Her early years were spent in Porirua at the time of the dawn raids. <i>(Aug 31, 2000,City Voice,1</i>)

>  Her early years were spent in Porirua at the time of the dawn raids. <i>(Aug 31, 2000,City Voice,1</i>)

>  Was 70s New Zealand really that bad? "God, it was grim. it was Muldoon. It was dawn raids and overstayers." <i>(Sep 3, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  In the case of Sir Graham Latimer, it wasn't quite a dawn raid, but still quite uncharitably early in the day (8.00am) for a 72-year-old who hasn't been well... "You've now got a situation where you've got some commissioners sicking police, dawn-raids-style, onto other commissioners." <i>(Oct 31, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### dawn service
 
 <i>n.</i> (on Anzac Day)

>  Lest we forget, Saturday is Anzac Day.National Radio carries a dawn service at5.40am. <i>(Apr 25, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),129</i>)

>  <b>ALLAN</b> Dawn Service on Anzac Day was a memorable occasion. <i>(1981,See Our Island,73</i>)

### Dawson's catshark
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Halaelurus dawsoni</i> [Darwson's catshark]

>  Dawson's catshark .. [scientific name] <i>Halaelurus dawsoni .</i>. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Dawsons catshark Halaelurus dawsoni </i>Named after its discoverer, a New Zealand oceanographer, and known only from the deeper waters ... of our continental slope ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,28</i>)

### Dawsonia
 
 <i>n.</i> king moss, giant moss.

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Of all the ground dwellers in the forest none equals in stature the DAWSONIA or KING MOSS (<i>Dawsonia superba</i>), which though native also to Australia and Tasmania, nevertheless reaches its largest dimensions in the forests north of Westland, more particularly the [66] kauri forests. ... The normal height ranges from eight inches to a foot, but many specimens attain twice that height ... Truly it deserves the alternative name of GIANT MOSS. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,66,1961 (64) ed.</i>)

### day
 
 <i>exclam.</i> short for gidday

### Day and Martins
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> In Poverty Bay the bulk of the [shearing] work is still done by the `Day and Martins', as the white shearers frequently term their coloured brethren. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

### day card
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELTON</b> Many [bookmakers] have cards printed with the names of horses in each race. These are termed 'day cards', and in addition the bookmakers have 'doubles charts'. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,14</i>)

### day for the queen/king
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a day when musterers elect not to work or are unable to work but remain on full pay.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A foggy day is usually a cause for rejoicing by a musterer, for as it is impossible for him to see to do his work, he and his dogs have a day off - in musterers' parlance, 'a day for a king.' <i>(1947,Straggle Muster,119</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. heavy fog hung low on the tops. It was 'a day for the king.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,164</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> When you wake up in the morning in a mustering hut and hear the rain beating on the roof someone is sure to poke his head out of the door and look around and say: "It's a day for the King boys!" <i>(1951,NZ High Country,43</i>)

>  On such a morning I would lie in bed, congratulating myself on the prospect of 'a day for the King.' <i>(Feb 22, 1968,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> .. a foggy day meant a day off on pay (known as a day for a King). <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,66</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> We decided we'd have a day for the queen - no work, full pay - an old mustering convention. [day for a king] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,166</i>)

### day for/on the king/queen
 
 <i>phr.</i> a day off. [(musterers' et al.)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... he and his dogs have a day off [on a foggy day] - in musterers' parlance - `a day for a king.' <i>(1947,Wayleggo,119</i>)

>  When you wake up in the morning in a mustering hut and hear the rain beating on the roof someone is sure to poke his head out of the door and look around and say: `Its a day for the Kingboys!' I don't know how this saying originated but it means a day of enforced idleness; often very welcome after a run of fine weather. <i>(1951,High Country,43</i>)

>  <b>PRESTON</b> The next day being wet, all [mustering] hands stayed in camp - a `Day for the King', but not for the poor cook trying to prepare their meals over an open fire in the rain. <i>(1978,Woolgatherers,96</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> As well as superstitions, the whalers of Tory Channel had a few time-honoured habits. One of these was known as `a-day-for-the-Queen'. Its main application seems to have been when a whaler had had a heavy day on the grog and had butterflies in his stomach and head the nextday in the form of a hangover. If, for example, a harpoon-gunner like Trevor Norton was to have`a-day-for-the-Queen', he would be on a kind of half-alert. After a solid day on the booze he might find a nice, quiet bunk on board the Perano mother ship Tuatea, where he could nestle down rather than being on lookout duty up on the hill. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,140</i>)

>  <b>LILEY et al</b> ... although no objection is made to `having a day on the king or queen', nearly every day is worked in the bush regardless of the weather. <i>(1954,Health of Fieldworkers: NZ Forest Service, The</i>)

### dayfill
 
 <i>n.</i> the job of filling supermarket shelves during the day (See [nightfill](../N#nightfill))

>  Dayfill: We have a vacancy for a person to do dayfill, Monday to Friday, 8.00-4.00pm. <i>(Mar 20, 2004,Hawke's Bay Today,C3</i>)

### daylight
 
 <i>v.</i> [(railwaymen's)]

>  The Railways will have to knock the top off five tunnels ... The tunnel jobs - termed `daylighting' in rail jargon - are required because the tunnels are not wide enough to take the new electric locos plus overhead wires ... `We have to daylight five tunnels ...' <i>(Mar 12, 1982,Evening Post,1/9</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> `They can't daylight it [sc the tunnel],' said Bill discouragingly. There's a lake above. I never feel safe going through it.' <i>(1985,G'day Country,38</i>)

### Daylight Limited
 
 <i>n.</i> a North Island main trunk passenger train.

>  According to the Star the country will be bankrupt if the Daylight Limited is kept on. <i>(May 22, 1926,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  The introduction of the Limited train between Auckland and Wellington made an immediate appeal to the popular imagination... The introduction of a summer running train, which took advantage of the daylight, was also 'limited in its accommodation'... It brought about a desire for more specific titles, so "Daylight Limited" and "Night Limited" became the terms commonly used in referring to them. <i>(Jul 18, 1928,NZ Free Lance,35</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 9, 1974,Evening Post,144</i>) [NOTES: From a review by Doug Wilkinson of: Steel Roads of New Zealand by Gordon Trou]

### daylight robbery
 
 <i>n.</i> an excessive charge or demand for payment. [Davin 1949 is earliest OED2 citation]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `I can never afford it,' said his sister. `It's daylight robbery.' <i>(1949,Roads from Home,8</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> daylight robbery shameless overcharging; eg `Electricity charges these days are daylight robbery.' Mostly ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,35</i>)

### daylight training
 
 <i>n.</i> [(educationists' jargon)]

>  During recent years a new piece of educational jargon has become current in New Zealand - `daylight training' of apprentices. It is a phrase that is not known in England, where the words `daytime release' are used as a label to a process parallel, though not identical with, that now developing here. ... It became current in New Zealand during the hearings of the Commission of Inquiry into Apprenticeship and Related Matters, 1944, and has continued in use though the wording of the Apprentices' Act does not include it [but uses] `technical training during working hours.' <i>(Sep 1943,Education,2 (4): 23</i>) [NOTES: (H.C. McQueen, Commissioner of Apprenticeship)]

### daylighting
 
 <i>n.</i> see daylight v.

>  <i>(Mar 12, 1982,Evening Post,1/9</i>)

>  `Daylighting' of the Karori tunnel so that it could be widened was another possibility discussed. <i>(Mar 24, 1987,Karori and Western Suburbs News,1</i>)

### daypack
 
 <i>n.</i> a small pack suitable for one-day tramps

>  She took the backpack off, put the framepack on, staggered a little. The daypack she perched precariously on her shoulders, but she knew she'd need her hands free. <i>(Dec 28, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> He will put his daypack on the grass.. <i>(1992,Going West,43</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> I ate the half grapefruit and a piece of muesli bar remaining from my day pack. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [hunting and mustering] a small pack, holding provisions for a day's work. (See [hillbag](../H#hillbag))

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Much of the boned-out meat and prime cuts were carried in his pikau, an improvised daypack. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,188</i>)

### dayth
 
 <i>n.</i> date

>  <b>McGILL</b> `That's 1922,' said Kelvin. `See the dayth above the tunnel.' `Dayth' for `date', just like my brother Michael and Dad saying `fillum' for `film'. It is the Irish way, quite common in Kiwiland. <i>(1985,G'day Country,97</i>)

### db drag racing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A sport has even grown out of the super stereo craze. Called db drag racing, competitors park their cars side by side with a microphone mounted on the front seat. On the starter's orders, competitors crank up their stereos to full volume via remote control. The loudest one wins. <i>(Jan 19, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

### DC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Detention Centre. [(prisoners')]

>  I am writing you this from D.C. which is what we call it but it is really Detention Centre ... . Its[sic] good training here ... If you go to Borstal everyone has been in more trouble so Iwould not like to be there. <i>(Sep 1982,Landfall,143/261</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> There were lots of screws, not only from the prison but from the DC as well. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,214</i>)

>  <b>HARRISON</b> `A tattoo done in jail. Or borstal. Or D.C.' ... Detention centre. Or remand home. <i>(1981,Quiet Earth, The,153</i>)

### DCM
 
 <i>abbr.</i> get the D.C.M.,to get the sack; be fired (`Don't Come Monday'). [ca1972 Oct 25 I suppose I'll get the D.C.M. in the pay packet on Friday.]

>  `Hori', [the boss] says, `the D.C.M. means `Don't Come Monday' You're fired, got the bullet and sacked.' <i>(Jun 1963,Home Life,2</i>)

### de facto
 
 <i>n.</i> A common-law spouse, as opposed to a de jure, or legally married one; one who has a permanent, live-in relationship with a partner of the opposite sex outside marriage. [ca1950 Wellington]

>  <b>WATSON</b> Even if it was my de facto husband. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,108</i>)

>  Radicals are not race-conscious. To prove this, radical girls like to have a tame Polynesian boyfriend/de facto to show off to their friends. <i>(Jun 7, 1972,Salient,16</i>)

>  de facto: mistress <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>GLEN</b> Each one of the girls was quietly living with her own fears for husband, boyfriend, de-facto, children's and parents. <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,116</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(transf.).]

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> Dalton had previously captained the All Blacks against the Springboks in 1981 when ... Mourie had been unavailable. Dalton believed this question implied his role in 1981 had been that of ade facto and he judged this to be demeaning of both himself and his team. <i>(1986,Geriatrics, The,9</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> of the nature of a common-law marital relationship

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... Sarah filled/ a hedge-gap in Duffy's life,/ perhaps more as a burnt child/ than as a de facto wife: <i>(1966,Collected Poems,350,1979 ed.</i>)

>  The words `de facto' are Latin and mean `in fact'. ... In common parlance, a de facto relationship has come to mean a relationship where a man and woman live together as husband and wife buthave never been lawfully married. <i>(Aug 4, 1982,NZ Truth,16</i>)

>  included ... are births which arise from parents living in a defacto relationship. <i>(1982,NZ Almanac,173</i>)

>  de facto ... 2 n. NZ & Austral. (also de facto wife or husband) person living with another as if married. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,194</i>) [NOTES: [Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 291; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 125]]

>  A man was alleged ... to have used his former de facto wife's kitchen knife ... to stab to death her male friend ... <i>(Apr 7, 1987,Dominion,6</i>)

>  In evidence, Mr Hoey's de facto wife, Mrs Julia Hunt, said Vogel had seemed `wasted' at the party. She assumed he had `pilled up.' <i>(Jul 24, 1987,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Drummer stabs de facto wife. Sickness beneficiary dies of stab wounds. <i>(Jun 1987,Landfall,162/121</i>) [NOTES: (Johnston)]

>  <b>KOEA</b> His de facto wife has misbehaved atChristmas and has given him severalpieces of her mind and much else. <i>(1992,Staying Home & Being Rotten,20</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adv.</i>

>  <b>GLEN</b> We went on to discuss the common practice of trying out a relationship, living together de-facto as so many young people do today. <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,146</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the removal of a bottleneck

He added the de-bottlenecking was necessary given the group's expectation that demand for fuel will expand about 2 per cent a year. <i>(Apr 30, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> living in a non-marital relationship

 <i>v.</i> to delouse. See also kutu. [(nonce)]

### de-lethalise
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Long before the fancy renovations were made to the Wellington terminal, measures should have been taken to de-lethalise these areas. <i>(Jul 20, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

### de-rail
 
 <i>n.</i> illicit raw spirits.

>  <b>HYDE</b> It was Maori country, which meant no licensed hotels, but plenty of sly grog - the good stuff down to [129] de-rail, denatured alcohol and meths. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,128,1970 ed.</i>)

### de-wooling paint
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Generally there has been little change in fellmongering practice since 1892. Skins are still cold-water washed in `dollies' or paddles, deburring machines are no longer used, but the de-wooling paint is applied on a moving conveyor instead of on the piled skins. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)

### dead
 
 <i>adj.</i> in the sense `exact (likeness, etc.)' (See [cut](../C#cut),[ring](../R#ring),[rink](../R#rink))



 
 <i>adj.</i> applied to a horse which will be (or has been) scratched, pulled (i.e. not allowed to run its best), or otherwise interfered with, so as not to start in, or to lose a race; a horse which does not try to win.

>  <b>SCOTT</b> [If he backed it] ... the horse would be `dead' in that particular race, and would win the next time of trying. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,90</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> This big joker say to me, `Listen, mate, that horse has got no show. He is dead.' I say, `He is not dead. I can see him now and he looks very much alive.' He then say, `I don't mean he is dead that way, but he is not a jigger. He is going to be hooked, pulled and run into the ground.' <i>Ibid.</i> 32 He won't start. ... Dad backed him last week and he told Mum he was dead. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,24</i>)

### dead as a moa
 
 <i>phr.</i> long dead (See [moa](../M#moa))

### dead as Ned Kelly
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1920 p.c. Sep 1979 middle-aged woman, De Brett Hotel, Wellington. This was a saying of her father and an old aunt first heard c1920. `I wouldn't be dead for quids,' said by somebody enjoying himself.]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Dead! Dead as Ned Kelly. Just shows what the wrong kind of boose [sic] can do to a man. ... ' <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,172</i>)
