 <i>n.</i> [(boarding-schoolboys') ca1930 (R. Mason) Girls' Boarding-school, Auckland: chocolate tapioca. -->ca1941 N.C.C. Small Wanganui Collegiate: brown sinker with chocolate sauce. -->ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silversteam: brown blancmange.]

### dead bird
 
 <i>n.</i> 1898 MORRIS Austral Eng. 115/2 Dead-bird, in Australia, a recent slang term,meaning `a certainty'. The metaphor is from pigeon-shooting, where the bird being let loose in front of a good shot is as good as dead.

>  Aroncel, a nice filly.. was considered to be a 'dead bird'. <i>(Apr 12, 1890,NZ Observer,15</i>)

### dead block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hunting area no longer populated with wild deer or pigs.

>  <b>SWAN</b> Dead blocks are repopulated with pigs which he liberates. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,34</i>)

### dead country
 
 <i>n.</i> . [goldmining; cf kindly country]

>  I am informed that Copper was obtained in rising, if so they have gone through it diagonally as elsewhere and lost it, the back or roof shows nothing but dead country destitute of copper or lode formation. <i>(Jan 31, 1885,Nelson Evening Mail,3</i>)

>  In the Aroha Subdivision, as in other mining areas of the Hauraki Peninsula, a distinction between `kindly' and `dead' country is made by miners. ... `Kindly' country for each locality is country in which lodes of that locality may carry valuable ore, while `dead' country is country in which the lodes become barren and small, or pinch out altogether. <i>(1913,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.16 (NS) 98-9</i>) [NOTES: (Henderson and Bartrum)]

>  This morning it was 12ft. from wall to wall, but of this great width some 3ft. or 4 ft. was dead country not worth crushing. <i>(Oct 14, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [figurative, sports - mainly rugby]

>  Messenger, however, was near at hand and saved a score against his side by kicking out into dead country. <i>(Aug 26, 1907,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The Blacks were now making the game very interesting for the home team, and the ball was scarcely in play again before they were once more at their throats and were within an ace of scoring, Fihelly just saving by kicking into dead country. <i>(Aug 5, 1907,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  An interesting race between him and Bisley resulted in favour of the Nelson man, who kicked the oval to dead country. <i>(Sep 16, 1907,Marlborough Express,7</i>)

>  Manawhenua continued the pressure, and when Walden failed to kick the ball into the dead country Williams dived over to score. <i>(Sep 7, 1931,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Hicks secured and kicked to dead country. <i>(Aug 5, 1914,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

### dead fly biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i> a fly cemetery

>  <b>LELAND</b> Dead Fly Biscuit see FLY CEMETERY <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,41</i>)

### dead ground
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  Colors of gold have been seen right up to th eend, and Mr Cartwright has every hope that the next stope coming along will show a corresponding improvement, and there is every prospect of the reef making again over the piece of dead ground, to which I referred om an ealier report. <i>(Nov 1, 1897,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  Six or seven feet of dead ground separates the branches. <i>(Sep 30, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The most important feature to record for the time, is the sinking of a winze from the surface of the Homeward Bound claim, on a leader through a block of ground which was considered nothing better than "dead ground;" but from the commencement of the winze good payable stuff has been obtained . . . <i>(Dec 13, 1870,NZ Herald,2</i>)

>  No. 11 stope has been carried through the dead ground at the break, and the men at the time of my visit were stripping the reef on the seaward side. <i>(Nov 7, 1883,Thames Star,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Dead ground? Yeah, dead ground is the country in hollows and between ridges that you can't see when you're looking at something a long way off, like those mountains there. Distances are very deceptive in this sort of country. You can travel all day towards a landmark and never seem to get any closer. Then bang, you're suddenly there. Something to do with dead ground I suppose. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,76</i>)

### dead man's bread
 
 <i>n.</i> [The Mansfield use evokes a child's perception of `all berries are poisonous': `tall berry bushes' suggest tutu - red would barely fit that; `white' suggests ?? cotoneaster; ed. Murry 1923 dated 1916. [R. Hyde ?Tutu; ?Black (Deadly) Nightshade; ?Hinau; ?Tawa mistaken for karaka: cf the following where R.H. mistakes ?karaka and tawa:]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Karaka berries, purple, fell and rotted to nothing if the birds didn't find them. <i>Ibid.</i> 126 There was a dark trail, ... and a stream came with edgings of fine malachite ferns, ... Purple berries lay fallen, rich as damsons but not as big, and good only for the fantails ... <i>Ibid.</i> xx But ours [sc. leaves of history], darker, might cry, `Where is Selwyn? Where is Rutherford? Where is Katherine, with weeds on her grave at Fontainebleau, when what she really wanted was the dark berry along our creeks? (Don't you remember? We call them Dead Man's Bread.) <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,89,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We were at home again beside the stream/ Fringed with tall berry bushes, white and red./ `Don't touch them: they are poisonous,' I said./ ... And as you stooped I saw the berries gleam./ `Don't you remember? We called them Dead man's Bread!' <i>(1923,Poems (various authors),47</i>) [NOTES: 1916 Mansfield in 1923 Poems 47(To L.H.B. (1894-1915)]

### dead man's fingers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Consider the Stinkhorn. The Fairy Club or the the Shaggy Parasol Mushroom. The Slippery Lizard Tuft, King Arthur's Cakes, the brain [sic] Mushroom or the Dead Man'sFingers. <i>(Dec 10, 1988,Dominion,3</i>) [NOTES: (David Hill)]

### dead man's watch
 
 <i>n.</i> [(? naut. sl., WW1)]

>  <b>MILLER</b> As I was on Dead Man's Watch [on the troopship] I stayed in bunk till dinnertime. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,14</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Feb 5 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tracks and Trenches 14]

### dead marine
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) an empty pint (beer) bottle; very common in NZ for `empty liquor bottle' (esp. after a party) from the 1870s; see beer shell.

>  Complaints had been received by the DeadMarines Department. <i>(Dec 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 45</i>)

>  It is Town Talk... That the abnormal weather caused a lot of seasickness among usually tough yachtsmen... Perhaps this accounts for the large numbers of dead marines that have been seen in the harbour lately. <i>(Jan 6, 1912,NZ Free Lance,26</i>)

>  A huge burial mound for `dead marines', as it consisted of empty bottles. <i>(1902,NZ Illustrated Magazine,7/99</i>)

>  dead marine n. N.Z. & Austral. inf. an empty beer bottle. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,286</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  <b>WARD</b> The writer was told that some of the Government officials helped in the good work by throwing "dead marines," in the shape of beer and ink bottles, out of the windows into the sea. <i>(1928,Early Wellington,454</i>)

>  So what's a few dead marines between friends? <i>(May 14, 2002,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The dead marines rolled down the stairsor spun from the top windows to plop intothe salt sober harbour. <i>(1967,Man on the Horse, The,138</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A quart bottle of beer is a <i>rigger</i>, and a pint bottle is a <i>marine</i>: doubtless neither of these terms is peculiar to New Zealand, but they are rarely, if ever, heard in England. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,89</i>)

>  One back street especially was a little of smashed glass and discarded "dead marines." <i>(Dec 8, 1917,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  There was the "suicide," "dead to theworld," surrounded by defunct "marines,"and clasping in his hand a corkscrew. <i>(Feb 13, 1904,Free Lance,12</i>)

### dead meat ticket
 
 <i>n.</i> an identity disc, cf. meat ticket

>  <b>MILLER</b> Our Dead Meat Tickets (identity discs) were issued ... <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,15</i>)

### dead muzzler
 
 <i>n.</i> a wind opposed to a ship's course. [1933 ?Eaddy ?? -->1939]

>  <b>BAINES</b> Here we found three schooners wind-bound, for the breeze that had brought us along so famously was a `dead muzzler' for them. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,160</i>)

### dead needle
 
 <i>n.</i> get the dead needle from (someone): to get the cold shoulder from. [Compare get the pricker.]

>  I got the dead needle [from a woman]. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,COLE</i>) [NOTES: (`Blues in the Night')]

### dead out
 
 <i>adj.</i> dead drunk [cf naut. dead oh]. [1980 citation belongs elsewhere (deal to?)]

>  Magistrate: `How much whisky can a man drink?' Witness (a hotel proprietor): `Oh, it varies very much. Some men are `dead out' after three nips, while others can drink two bottles a dayeasy.' <i>(Apr 23, 1910,NZ Truth,252/1/6</i>)

>  <b>BERRY</b> Jim twenty-eight looking forty-eight, and Ben forty-eight pretending twenty-eight. Jim talking of bread, grass, acid, boob, screws and dealing to people, while Ben spoke of money, marijuana, LSD, prison officers, and good hidings. <i>(1980,First Offender,147</i>)

### dead possum syndrome
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Another common problem in emergencies is what he calls "dead possum syndrome"; people look at the thing they are trying to avoid hitting - so inevitably hitting it. <i>(Oct 1, 2001,Dominion,3</i>)

### dead ring
 
 <i>n.</i> the exact likeness of [(the dead ring of, also the dead spit of, the dead cut of) ca1938 Havelock schoolchildren]

>  "Aw, they were grand! One fellow was the dead ring of you. Man (reminiscently), they could pull some ugly faces." <i>(May 12, 1909,Otago Witness,73</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Bob said they were the dead ring of Gil, especially Gilbert. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,72,1976 ed.</i>)

>  You are the dead ring of that chap [Jiggs] in the skit in the back of the `Evening Post.' <i>(Nov 15, 1952,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dead ring exact likeness; eg David Ballantyne, The Cunninghams, 1948, writing of the sons: `They were the dead ring of Gil.' NZA. Aka `dead ringer', also US. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,35</i>)

>  The full-brother to Little Pointer is acting good. He is the dead ring of his relation. <i>(Sep 9, 1922,NZ Truth,9</i>)

### dead spit
 
 <i>n.</i> the exact likeness [(the dead spit of) the spitting image[OED2 spit sb?. 3 the very spit of 1825-1886; EDD gen. dial. and EDD. spit, esp. in phrs the very spit of in most Eng. dials except SW.]] (See [dead ring](../D#dead-ring))

>  He is well proportioned, and the dead spit of his daddy, with legs like bars of steel; a few black splodges through his golden coat indicate the Stockwell strain, as the Duke of Westminster's celebrated stud horse Bend Or is marked similarly. <i>(Aug 3, 1893,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  "Excuse me, sir, but, barring the whisker, you are the dead spit of a man I knew once, professor." <i>(Mar 15, 1898,Press, The,2</i>)

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> That horse ... is the `dead spit' of the steeplechaser Orangeman. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,91</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> `She's not like you - takes after her father?' ... `No, she don't; she's the dead spit of me. Any fool could see that.' <i>(1912,Stories,112,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... he belted my bare bottom and shouted you're like your parents no damn good you're the dead spit of your old man. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,231</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> look the dead spit: `Beaut baby! Looks the dead spit of his dad!' JUST ALIKE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,61</i>)

>  Everybody present fairly shook with laughter when this estimable person, having been asked how she could tell that the defendant was father of the child, replied: "I saw it two months afterwards; it had thriven wonderful, and it exactly resembles Mr------------ (the defendant). It's the dead spit of him-as if it had been cut off with a knife and fork. .." <i>(1885,Thames Star,2</i>)

>  It is a wonderful thing to be instantly recognised as a personage. For instance, there is an old bean at Home who is the dead spit of Lloyd-George. <i>(Dec 20, 1919,Observer (Auckland),10</i>)

### dead stick
 
 <i>n.</i> [(marbles)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> It is important to distinguish these `Action-Terms' of marbles from the innumerable merely descriptive marble terms which have no effect on the course of the action - terms such asRinger, In, Smug, Dead Stick, Skun, Skinned, Sported off, Scat, Cannons, Dubs, Kills, Funs, Keeps, Pink. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/770</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### dead sticks daisy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Downstream, where the Ashburton and Rangitata burst through their gorges onto the Canterbury plains, willow, lupins, and broom are already choking the riverbeds, usurping the silvery native cushion plants and the 'dead sticks' daisies. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/29</i>)

### dead throating
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  What self-respecting style queen would be seen dead throating a brown stubby? <i>(Nov 11, 1999,Dominion,13</i>)

### dead wool
 
 <i>n.</i> wool plucked from the skin of a sheep dead through natural causes.



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wool that has been sloughed off or plucked from a dead sheep [ of value when wool prices are high.]

>  All wool not taken from live sheep is called dead wool. <i>(Nov 21, 1868,Otago Witness,16</i>) [NOTES: useful descriptions in this article]

>  `Dead Wool,' removed from dead sheep, has little commercial value and is never an important item. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> When did we start buying dead wool? ... The smell of dead wool goes off when it is plucked. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,15</i>)

>  Dead Wool: Wool plucked from a dead sheep. May contain cellular debris from the skin known as scud. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>McKAY</b> Baxter's duties, as recorded in the station journal kept by the manager Mr Charlie Harris, were with the sheep, drafting, and picking over crutchings and dead wool in the paddocks. <i>(1990,James K. Baxter,86</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> To speak of selling three sacks of dead wool and buying a vacuum cleaner ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,11</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> When sloughed from a dead sheep, wool is not classed as cast but as dead wool. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,16</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL & TROTTER</b> It was a very bad winter and many sheep were lost. They retrieved the dead wool, scoured it, and sent some to the Dunedin Exhibition. They won first prize for the Scoured Dead Wool. <i>(1987,I, Duncan from Skippers,13</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> I told her I would be taking all the men and two pack horses next day to pick up some dead wool and would try to dig the horse out of the slip at the same time because I thought my saddle was still on it. <i>(1990,Wild Horses & Me,141</i>)

### dead work
 
 <i>n.</i> Preparatory work unproductive of ore or gold. [goldmining; used elsewhere - nb OED]

>  The weekly battery returns appears small, but the crashing leaves a very respectable margin of profit, which helps materially to keep down the burden of the cost of the dead work now going on in the mine. <i>(Aug 18, 1888,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  If it is satisfactory, as for instance when the supervisor is able to assure the executive that the miner is engaged on 'dead' work necessary to open up a promising claim, his subsidy is confirmed. <i>(Jun 11, 1935,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  ... while its close proximity to the vein would obviate the necessity of much `dead work' before the vein was reached. <i>(1910,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.11 (NS) 37</i>) [NOTES: (Webb, Mt Radiant)]

>  <b>MAY</b> DEAD WORK: all preparatory work on a claim - sinking shafts, driving tunnels, constructing a race - before gold-saving commences. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Most of the dead work is finished, and all hands will be chiefly engaged procuring quartz. <i>(Apr 6, 1881,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Like other engineers, he was doing some dead work, but hoped in a few months to get a return for the present outlay. <i>(Jul 22, 1897,Thames Star,4</i>)

>  The last time the tunnel closed 78 shores were necessary to reopen the works, and it would require more dead work if the tunnel is closed now. <i>(Feb 9, 1920,Northern Advocate,2</i>)

>  This tunnel is merely preliminary dead work which has no connection with the finished job; but all the same is absolutely essential to enable the job to be done. <i>(Jul 19, 1926,Auckland  Star,8</i>)

### dead zone
 
 <i>n.</i> area dubbed "low employment location"

>  For a "dead zone", Tinui seems remarkably alive and kicking. ... A year ago <i>Sunday Star Times</i> visited the tiny Wairarapa settlement not long after it was designated as one of 160 "low employment locations" (better known as dead zones or no-go areas) by Work and Income, meaning that people could not move there and receive a benefit. <i>(Mar 6, 2005,Sunday Times (NZ),A15</i>)

### dead-head
 
 <i>n.</i> a free-loader

>  It is not no uncommon experience.. tofind two-thirds of a railway carriagecomposed of "dead-heads". <i>(Dec 22, 1938,NZ Observer,9</i>)

### dead-house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COOPER</b> The body was then taken back to the dead-house. <i>(Dec 17, 1867,Digger's Diary,23,1978 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(?mainly Northland gumfields)]

>  ... when `the heroes of a hundred drunks' are helplessly prone on the floor [of a Kaipara bar] ... a couple of stretcher men speedily whip away the `dead drunk' off to an outbuilding in the rear ... known as `the dead house', there to sleep off the effects of the debauch. On one Sunday, 18 bushmen and gumdiggers were lying `heads and tails' in the deadhouse ... <i>(Jun 10, 1876,Weekend Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1876 June 10 Weekly Herald in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 52]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> [To publican] `Where is the guest?' `In the dead-house ... We have to put him in there at times; he won't keep out of the bar.' [? Main or additional meaning: where dead bodies are kept;backblocks morgue] <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,15</i>)

>  Dead House: A rough room with bunks round it, which was kept by country publicans to put the dead drunks in. (C.) <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> ... and the `deadhouse', at that time [c1900] a necessary abjunct to every country hotel, wherein the paralytic drunks were thrown, is now known only in history. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,121</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> [Northland] If he became drunk enough [in the gumfields hotel the White Pig] to be a danger to himself or to anyone else accommodation would be provided in the dead house. This institution ... was a small, strongly built shed with no windows. Auger holes provided light and ventilation. The door was locked outside by a strong hasp and padlock. <i>(1970,Way Up North,34</i>)

### dead-Kiwi bounce
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The only way out is up, and hopefully not via a dead-Kiwi bounce. <i>(Aug 26, 2000,Evening Post,20</i>)

### dead-sheep hole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was found near the farm's dead-sheep hole about 300 metres into the paddock. <i>(Jan 19, 1999,Dominion,1</i>)

### deadbeat
 
 <i>n.</i> a person down on his luck [aka dead-beat, dead beat]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> A deadbeat appealed to the owner for a job, and wouldn't take no for an answer. <i>(1875,West Coast Gold Rushes,156,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> This [gumfield] is the stamping-ground of the dead-beats of the World. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,18</i>)

>  <b>CRADDOCK</b> Rabbiters are not in the highest class of New Zealand workmen - in fact, as a rule, they are men who cannot keep steady at any other work, and are rather aptly called `dead-beats.' <i>(1904,Sport in NZ,10</i>)

>  If a hungry `dead beat' tried to strike one of Booth's hash foundries for a bit of tucker before he had earned about four times its value ... <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/1/7</i>)

>  dead a.- dead'beat n. a person down on his luck. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,3</i>)

>  dead beat n. man down on his luck, stone broke; completely exhausted. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  He had the appearance of being a deadbeat, owing to his rather shabby attire.&#8195; <i>(Aug 19, 1896,6</i>) [NOTES: not certain that this is NZ source - Jan]

### deader
 
 <i>n.</i> a dead fish.

>  That much-moustachioed officer sternly ordered Brown to take the deaders [the rotten 'couta] to the end of the jetty and hurl them back into their native element. <i>(Apr 1, 1911,NZ Truth,301/6/3</i>)

### deadheader
 
 <i>n.</i> someone removing dead flower heads from flowering plants

>  ...the only movement of any significance comes from the rose garden where teams of dead-headers, some from as far afield as Geraldine, move slowly like priests down the aisles of plants, bestowing the benediction of their secateurs on the dead and dying. <i>(May 10, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

### deadhouse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The "deadhouse" is a familiar adjunct toa bush pub. <i>(Sep 3, 1910,NZ Free Lance,13</i>)

### deadly
 
 <i>adj.</i> very good

>  see [Aviemore Visitors' Book] Fantabulous <i>(Oct 15, 1966,Dominion,3/1</i>)

### deadman
 
 <i>n.</i> a post

>  Where it is difficult to obtain ground hold for fence strainer posts, such as in swamps or rocky hill country, fences may be strained with a 'deadman', and the strainer post should be cast ... <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,61</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [fencing] a post placed underground to enhance anchorage of a strainer post.

>  Where it is difficult to obtain ground hold for fence strainer posts, such as in swamps or rocky hill country, fences may be strained with a 'deadman' ... <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,61</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> This method of anchoring is known as the "deadman." <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,418</i>)

>  .. a cheaper and less laborious alternative would be to use a treated timber post (or half a post) in place of the concrete slab, burying it to act as a 'dead-man.' <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  Posts: 400 No 1 2.7m round posts, 700 No 1 3.0m half-round posts, 200 deadmen. [dead-man] <i>(Jun 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,02</i>)

### deadman's arm
 
 <i>n.</i> a steamed pudding or currant roll.

>  Oh you [bush-felling] contractors in black singlets and bowyangs, fuelled onspuds and mutton and pumpkin and rice and spotted dog or deadman's arm and black billy tea: perfect toast sopping with butter and Cocky's Joy (treacle): come into our art galleries, onto our stamps, wrap within songs, ... <i>(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times,5</i>)

### deadset
 
 <i>adj.</i> definite

>  Right now, only one deadset certainty has been on top of his game from the start of Super 12 - Crusaders flanker Reuben Thorne. <i>(May 6, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### deal bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Police found three lsd tablets and 23 grams of cannabis in a "deal bag" during a search of the premises. <i>(Apr 6, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

### deal to
 
 <i>v.</i> give a hiding or bashing to; to beat up. [(mainly criminals')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> `Policeman' were plentiful, and already one inmate who had been seen talking to the screws about the matchstick affair had been dealt to. <i>Ibid.</i> 248 DEAL TO (v) Assault, beat up, damage, destroy. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,121</i>)

>  If you think you're gonna get your beans ... [sc. to get beaten up] well, you don't want to have it dealt to you. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

>  The week after the incident, she had overheard Carrington say he had `dealt to' somebody and had seen him move his fists and say `pow, pow'. <i>(Jun 21, 1983,Dominion,4</i>)

>  Britt had learned that a group of Rastafarians at the party had `dealt to' Miss Edwards and her friend Kim McFarlane. <i>(Mar 28, 1984,Dominion,7</i>)

>  `There were eight of them watching us getting dealt to and they did nothing,' Ms Stirling said. <i>(Sep 2, 1986,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Michael Morris of Wellington, says the term "deal to" sounds strange to him as a New Zealand citizen returning after 22 years away. He says he hears New Zealanders but never British people saying "deal to" and much to his surprise has also seen it in print here. He quotes a Lloyd Jones short story saying "... and the intervening coastline looked to have been dealt to with a meat cleaver".... That said, "deal to is accepted by Oxford's <i>Dictionary of NZ English</i> with plenty of citation. <i>(Sep 10, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The couple should drop the case. If they didn't they would be "dealt to". <i>(Jul 29, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(transf.)]

>  Rugby Commentator [on Canadian Team] Most of the team that dealt to Tonga are still with it. [On the Canadian rugby team which had defeated Tonga] <i>(May 30, 1987,TV1</i>)

### deal-fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In shape, [the family Trachypteridae] are usually long, deep, and very much compressed and flattened on the sides, so much so that their local appellations always embody some idea of these peculiarities - such as ribband-fish, lath or deal fish, band fish or blade fish, and also oar-fish. <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/196</i>) [NOTES: (Clarke)]

>  The Oar-fish, 12 to 20 feet long, with a high, flame-tipped head-crest (the elongated fin rays) is probably the sea-serpent of repute. Its pelvic fins are narrow, elongated processes, hence the name Oar-fish. The Deal-fish is much smaller, but of the same type, but we know very little of the habits of either of them. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,198</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 198]

>  <b>AYLING</b> RIBBONFISH (Dealfish) <i>Trachipterus trachypterus ...</i>The ribbonfish is similar to the oarfish but is much smaller.. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,189,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] dealfish 1069-81 DEALFISHES 1069-85 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  In May, two rare pygmy whales were stranded further north on Waitarere Beach, and in February, a two-metre-long deepsea dealfish with saucer-sized eyes and a retractable mouth spooked walkers on Waikanae Beach. <i>(Nov 18, 2002,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

### dealing to
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a beating

>  [the bouncer] descended, seized one of the chaps and hauled him away ... for a dealing to. <i>(Oct 1985,Metro,24</i>)

### deaner
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [deener](../D#deener))

### death cap mushroom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The death cap mushroom - which can resemble a puffball in its early stages - was small and brown with a green or brown cap and white gills. <i>(Jun 4, 2005,Dominion Post</i>)

### death lily
 
 <i>n.</i> descriptive for arum lily

>  You'll notice that grazing animals tend to leave these arums, callas, zantedeschias, call them what you will, alone because their poisonous leaves taste nasty. Death lily is one name Wellington Regional Council says has been applied to them. <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,9</i>)

### debt-dodger
 
 <i>n.</i> First Echelon volunteer; see also debt-evader, wife-beater. [usu. pl. (WW2)]

>  <b>BRUNO</b> (Foreword) Certain near-sober First Echelon wife-beaters and debt-dodgers <i>(1944,Desert Daze</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> <i>(1948,Maleesh George,84</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Debt Dodgers and Wife Beaters First Echelon, 2NZEF, WWII, used derisively even by the volunteers themselves; perhaps originating from media comment at the time, Jim Henderson speculates <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,35</i>)

>  <b>Sinclair & Harrex</b> The troops left home in three 'Echelons'. The first went to Egypt. As eager volunteers, they were labelled 'the wife beaters and debt dodgers'. <i>(1978,Looking Back,215</i>)

### debt-evader
 
 <i>n.</i> [(WW2) usu. pl.] (See [debt-dodger](../D#debt-dodger))

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> 'Truth' said last week ... that this First Echelon was made up of debt-evaders and wife-deserters and out-of-works. <i>(1946,We Walked Alone,62</i>)

### deburring
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also burr](../A#also-burr))

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Generally there has been little change in fellmongering practice since 1892. Skins are still cold-water washed in `dolllies' or paddles, deburring machines are no longer used, but the de-wooling paint is applied on a moving conveyor instead of on the piled skins. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)

### decile
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In scenario one, he has the top income deciles losing most government health, education and housing services and the middle deciles losing somewhat less, while those on lower incomes are left untouched. <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  My mother was a city person, didn't want to go on a farm, married a lower decile, so we have very little communication with the farmpeople. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Even before cutbacks, the government spends less on the 10th decile than any other - and this despite the fact that the average top-decile household has more people in it than any other decile. <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  I have worked in low and high decile, rural and urban, and single sex and co-educational schools. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,16</i>)

>  One of Don Brash's strongest areas of objection to "race-based funding" is decile funding, a way of helping the poorest schools that was introduced by National in the mid-1990s. <i>(Feb 8, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Many lower decile schools have all but emptied out as parents shift their children to 'better' (read higher decile) schools. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,NZ Education Review,7</i>)

>  However, Mr McCully said the taxi bill was "clearly the result of decile funding"... Under the Education Ministry's decile scheme, schools from the lowest-ranked income areas - ranked decile 1A - get about $330 a year per pupil of special funding aimed at increasing educational achievement. Schools in the richest areas - decile 10 - get nothing. <i>(Apr 3, 2002,Dominion,14</i>)

>  She acknowledged that the wealthy decile 10 school had better resources than many. <i>(May 16, 2001,Evening Post,3</i>)

### deck
 
 <i>n.</i> a wooden platform outside a house [The word deck is given in the 2-volume Oxford in what seems to be the NZ sense of a wooden platform outside a house. It seems totally alien to the English experience. How many English houses have decks? Patios, yes but decks, no. Is it from Oz, California, S. Africa?]

>  Back home in New Zealand, I now look with new eyes on the explosion of decks, patios and outdoor furniture, mostly made from rain-forest timber. New Zealanders recline and drink their beer on wooden chairs on timber decks - timber that was the orang-utan's home. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Dominion,8</i>)

>  A pregnant woman who fell two metres when a deck collapsed in Stokes Valley on Christmas Day believes an angel cushioned her fall. <i>(Dec 26, 1997,Dominion,3</i>)

>  [Advt]. Decking & Landscaping. 100x40 RAD H3 Decking. <i>(Dec 30, 1997,Norwester,7</i>)

### decker
 
 <i>n.</i> a double-decker tram. [(obs.) ca1930 Wellington Tramways (F. O'Brien)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a hat [(obs.)]

>  Hence it is that when a humble general ink-slinger meets the turf scribe, his `decker', `hard-hitter,' or `cap,' is raised in reverential awe. <i>(Jun 11, 1910,NZ Truth,259/4/4</i>)

### declamation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The work, part of the $18 million Waitangi park development, includes wharf piling, excavation and harbour "declamation" ... <i>(Mar 14, 2005,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

### Declaration of Independence
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The treaty which forms the base of all my proceedings was signed at Waitangi on the 6th February 1840, by 52 chiefs, 26 of whom were of the confederation, and formed a majority of those who signed the Declaration of Independence. This [sc copy of treaty] instrument I consider to be de facto the treaty, and all signatures that are subsequently obtained are merely testimonials of adherence to the original document. <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 17</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 25 Hobson to Bunbury in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 17]

### decol
 
 <i>n.</i> decolonisation

>  Julie and I have been running a theory course.. which is really a "decol" course too. Decolonisation. <i>(Dec 1998,Mana,38</i>)

### decommoditise
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Literally, Zespri has "decommoditised" its kiwifruit by differentiating them from other countries'. <i>(Jun 13, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

### decompensation
 
 <i>n.</i> a reversal of compensation procedures

>  "While he had been able to compensate for the effects of other personal issues and stresses, this additional stimulus had the effect of causing him suddenly to decompensate, to the extent that he was no longer fit to drive."... The driver had been appropriately treated by the company doctor and the risk of his "decompensation" could not have been predicted... <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### decoy
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to entice an animal into a yard or hazard.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The decoy pen was so situated that two or three sheep placed in it decoyed others on to the tip. <i>(1949,High Country Days,102</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> They took an old trotting hack, Berry, to decoy the foal to the yards and tied it up. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,124</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal used to attract others into yards or across hazards. (See [leader](../L#leader),[Judas sheep](../J#judas-sheep),[pet](../P#pet))

>  I would strongly advise making use of them [pets] as indicated as decoys. Thus when moving sheep, if the owner of the pet goes ahead with the decoy, it will be found there is little if any use for dogs or strong language. <i>(Nov 1917,NZ Farmer,1238</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> I then took up about twelve very quiet cattle and used them as decoys to drive with the wild ones into the paddock. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,45</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> As the occasion arises he will hold a few sheep in the race to act as decoys to those which are a bit tardy entering the crush pen. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,77</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> .. taking a small cut of about 50 sheep, I drove them partly up the hill and left them as decoys. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,107</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> To calm the nervy, a decoy would be tied up on the Bog Roy side, and a shepherd called 'the catcher' would run up, slip a lead over her and pull her across, or step over her, kick her bum and go maa-maa. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,117</i>)

### decoy horse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a horse used to attract wild horses into yards for breaking.

>  <b>CHINN</b> With every rider now concentrating on his duties, we drove the wild horses towards the station bullock yards where several decoy horses were quietly feeding <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,74</i>)

### decoy mob
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering of wild or bush cattle] a small mob of tame cattle to lead wild beasts.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> On a morning of brilliant sunshine I moved the decoy mob of young cattle slowly along the Big Bay beach. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,89</i>)

### decoy pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen beside a dip or loading race where decoy stock are kept to encourage others.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The tip, a small pen capable of holding 10 or 12 sheep, was situated a foot or so above and partly overhanging the dip. ... with the aid of a lever it could be tipped and the sheep sent slithering into the dip below. The decoy pen was so situated that two or three sheep placed in itdecoyed others onto the tip. <i>(1949,High Country Days,102</i>)

>  The decoy pen acts as an inducement for incoming sheep ... <i>(Jan 1, 1929,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

>  With side-delivery dips, often a decoy pen is used - on far side of dip. <i>(Sep 30, 1940,Taranaki Daily News,23</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The tip platform extends from the sheep entry gate to the decoy pen gate. The tip platform consists of three parts - the frame, the tipping portion and the decoy pen. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,365</i>)

### Decramastic
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of roof tile [Suggested as possibly NZ, but found in California, Australia, and Jamaica.]

### Dee
 
 <i>n.</i> Pl. often the Dees; a policeman, esp. now a detective. see also demon. [[In fairly gen. use, but first recorded in NZ.]]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> These ... are gentlemen who give their talents and time to the paternal Government for a certain modicium [sic] of payment, and are as known D's. [check cit. ?D.s??] <i>(1869,Comic Guide to Dunedin,27</i>)

>  I was informed that the D's were hanging around that vicinity. <i>(1895,Diary of Jonathan Roberts,18</i>)

>  Wellington Detective Department showed a mean sort of spirit over Detective Sergeant Cassell's farewell. As is well known the popular `demon' has been transferred to Napier, ... The uniformed branch of the police ... wished him all sorts of good wishes, but the other branch, the `Dees', was not represented, ... <i>(Jul 22, 1911,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  I asked a young man what was the matter, and got the reply, `Oh, it's nothing! Only the d's pinchin a little piece.' ... I found that d's were detectives ... <i>(May 1930,National Education,197</i>) [NOTES: (C.J. Hickson)]

>  <b>SAR GESON</b> [Barman speaks] That and not letting that bloody dee bulldoze me. <i>(1946,That Summer,74</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> The bloody Ds told me she was ... bloody lucky she didn't get clink. <i>(1960,Word for Word,252</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> `Bloody bastards, those dees are,' said Tabetha. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,90</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The second day he was beaten up by the cops/ For telling a dee his house was not in order. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,347,(1966) ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Sittin' there, broodin'. Waiting for the D's to catch up with him. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,39,(1981 TS) ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> `See that dee though. He was right pissed off; He's gonna be after you, my son.' <i>(1982,Other Halves,132</i>)

>  <b>McGILL & TILLEY</b> We left as soon as the Ds arrived. <i>(1980,In Praise of Older Buildings,18</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Once a dee, always on the outer. <i>(1967,Man on the Horse, The,152</i>)

>  <b>McGEE</b> He'll do well in the force, he's got the same look as all those C.I.B. Dees - hard, cold, dead, as if what those eyes have looked at, or for, in people, has killed the light in them. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a D-shaped iron clip or loop on a saddle serving to carry suspended articles.

>  <b>BARKER</b> We were well mounted, and all our luggage consisted of my little travelling-bag ... and what is termed a `swag' in front of F--'s saddle; that is, a long narrow bundle, in this instance enclosed in a neat waterproof case, and fastened with two straps to the `D's,' which are the steel loops let in in four places to all colonial saddles, for the purpose of carrying blankets, &c.; they derive their name apparently from their resemblance to the letter. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,82,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Dee (d&emacr.), n. [Written also D.] A steel loop attached to a saddle, for suspending packages; - so called from its shape, which resembles the letter D. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2021</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> He rolled his Erewhonian dress and small toilet necessaries inside a warm red blanket, andstrapped the roll on to the front part of his saddle. On to other D's, with which his saddle was amply provided, he strapped his Erewhonian boots, a tin pannikin, and a billy that would hold about a quart. <i>(1901,Erewhon Revisited,ch ii,(Everyman edn. 1932 (1960) 207) ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> When not in use, whips were carried coiled up and fastened to the back `D' on the off side of the saddle by a short strap that could be quickly undone when required. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,150,1954 ed.</i>)

>  dee, D. n. D-shaped metal loop or clip on a saddle. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [cits; see also deeing](../C#cits;-see-also-deeing))

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> A `ropey' will learn to snipe - which is a highly-skilled operation. The butt end of a clean-trimmed log is trimmed of angles. V's are cut at points opposite each other at the butt end (usually) and are connected by a Dee which runs round in a D from one to the other. In the Dee is laid a `strop', a short length of cable with an eye in each end. The eyes are connected with each other and to a short chain by a shackle; and the chain is attached to `the rope' with a hitch. <i>(1950,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: R. GILBERD Okaihau]

>  DEE: A groove in the front end of a log into which a strop is fitted for hauling. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> [The log] was hauled by wire rope or chain bridle. This was attached sometimes by cutting a `D' in the top half of the nose of the log, or more often by two heavy spiked `dogs' which were driven in each side of the log ... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,42</i>)

### deed of settlement
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ngai Tahu were granted statutory acknowledgement over the lagoon as part of their Deed of Settlement. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Ngai Tahu were granted statutory acknowledgement over the lagoon as part of their Deed of Settlement. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

### deeds land
 
 <i>n.</i> land subject to the deeds system

### deeds system
 
 <i>n.</i> a system introduced in 1842 by the Conveyancing Ordinance providing for the registration of documents affecting land ownership [Jim Cameron writes: Largely historical. This system has long been almost wholly replaced by the <i>land transfer system</i>.]

>  The land transfer system contrasted with the older deeds system, introduced in 1841 as a reformed version of traditional English real property law whereby rights to ownership depended on the existence and validity of a chain of documents. The term title deeds derives from this. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### deeing
 
 <i>n.</i> putting dees (n.2) into logs.

>  <b>CRUMP</b> During the next few weeks Jack learned about scarfing, backing, limbing, deeing, sniping, [etc.] <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,42</i>)

### deek
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "decoy"

>  We put out a couple of deeks [decoys] and just wait for them. <i>(Dec 20, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

### deeming
 
 <i>n.</i> having a narrow strip of private land to allow private access along waterways where public ownership does not exist

>  The Acland Report discussed various forms of "deeming", that is, having a narrow strip of private land to allow private access along waterways where public ownership does not exist. <i>(Dec 3, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### deener
 
 <i>n.</i> a shilling (piece); pl. usu. deener, when used with a numeral. [ca1950 (Wellington barber) used c1930-1935 ca1951 (P.S. Arden) `a shilling' used c1920 Auckland]

>  Following are other local money-names ... ls. - deener, bob, colonial robert or penny ... <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from Loafer, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  ... grasping that piece of current coin known to wicked bookies and others as a `deener', he banged gaily on the counter and demanded its equivalent in the diet which sends its genial whiff about 200 yards down the street against the strongest gale. <i>(Jul 20, 1912,NZ Truth,369/7/1</i>)

>  Mug is a humorous sort, and seen after the race in question, he remarked, `She has cost me about 60 googs, but I'll take my oath the squib will not cost me 61 (?) Not another `deener.'[punctuation sic] cf zwei-deener 'twopence' =2s. <i>(Feb 27, 1909,NZ Truth,193/2/8</i>)

>  I put the nips into one the other night 'n he came to light with a couple o' dener. <i>(Sep 19, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,63</i>)

>  We use midgic for a shilling, where the Australian habitually uses deener. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  We use midgic for a shilling, where the Australian habitually uses deener. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I'm on the beach myself, I said, but I can make it a couple of deener. <i>(1946,That Summer,77</i>) [NOTES: [reference to 1930-35]]

>  I couldn't even drink beer as I was down to my last deener. <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  deener ... n. (Sl.) shilling [Ital. denaro, money]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> Obol and diobol, the two in the one like a symbol of something. ... Two zacs to the deener, too. Nonsense. <i>(1970,O'Leary's Orchard,130</i>) [NOTES: (`Riley's Handbook')]

>  For the shilling bob was the most common nickname in use, but also recorded is deener. This latter name was used more widely in New Zealand and Australia than in Britain. The sixpence attracted many slang terms - tizzy, tanner, sprat, bender, and a kick. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> occas., and mistakenly, a sixpence [ca1940 Marlborough, -->ca1963 Auckland (V. O'Sullivan)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a penny. [[Is Mackenzie mistaken here; or is this a shift of meaning? I have heard deener mistakenly used c1940s and 1960s fo sixpence.]]

>  <b>MACKENZIE</b> deener a penny <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>) [NOTES: (Lag's Lexicon)]

### deep ground
 
 <i>n.</i> in goldmining spec. applied to places well below surface-levels; ground in which the gold-bearing material lay well beneath the ac surface.

>  <b>MAY</b> DEEP SINKING: putting down a shaft to tap a deep lead. No absolute figure can be given but `deep ground' usually required shafts of over sixty feet. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Large quantities of gold have been extracted from the ground at the rear of the township, and it is well known that several rich leads of gold run into the deep ground which the lessees are unable to work with the limited capital and appliances at their command. <i>(Jul 10, 1888,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Intelligent miners inform me that it would be an exception to get gold in this river drift, or on the reef underlying, also that it is an exception to get gold in the deep ground on the Arrow river. <i>(Sep 14, 1888,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

>  To miners who are working deep ground this is not of so much consequence, as they can stack their dirt, if it is worth it, until an opportunity of washing it occurs. <i>(Jan 18, 1869,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  This society was formed expressly to test the existence of gold in the deep ground, and their success has, therefore, a significance far greater than the mere value of what they themselves may obtain. <i>(Apr 5, 1862,Press, The,3</i>) [NOTES: The Deep Sinking Society!]

>  It has been an unusually fine winter, which gave the miners a good chance of working the deep ground, and the majority of the claims have paid well. <i>(Oct 13, 1877,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

### Deep Jay
 
 <i>n.</i> deep jungle

>  But controversy over Eder's account of New Zealand soldiers killing Vietcong prisoners while on deep jay (jungle) patrol has sharpened public awareness for his book. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  The "Deep Jay" in soldiers' parlancemeans out in the jungle. <i>(Mar 19, 1995,Sunday Star Times,D5</i>)

### deep lead
 
 <i>n.</i> a lead (alluvial deposit of gold along the bed of an ancient river) which can be tapped only by deep-mining operations. [goldmining]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We had a claim ... over the terrace on a deep lead. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,146</i>)

>  ... the alluvial miners thought they were dealing with superficial alluvial deposits; ... Deeper workings showed that the beds dipped steeply, and the gold continued downwards, the conglomerates were then called `deep leads', probably in the belief that they resembled the deep leads of Victoria. <i>(1933,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,14/272</i>) [NOTES: (Macpherson)]

>  <b>EVANS</b> This dredge received its name from the fact that the McGeorge Brothers prospected for and found a new `deep lead' or layer of gold-bearing wash deeper down and separated from thelayer that other dredges in the area had worked upon. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,39</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> The downfolded remnants in the valleys now form the `deep leads', the white `quartz wash' of many areas ..., while the gold from the ... sediments became re-concentrated in the younger terrace gravels and alluvium, or `younger drifts' of the miners. <i>(1970,Gold Trails of Otago,81</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Neither the development of ground-sluicing nor the tunnel claims made Ross and Donoghue's distinctive. The absorbing topic at `the Ballarat of Westland' was the deep leads. These produced the most imposing mining works on the West Coast and gave Ross a permanenceunusual for an alluvial goldfield. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,188,1962 ed.</i>)

### deep sea diver
 
 <i>n.</i> a fiver, a five-pound note. [(prisoners') rhyming slang]

>  I had promised to give her a deep sea diver for the Duke of Kent. deep sea diver ... fiver <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### deep sea fishing
 
 <i>n.</i> whaling from ocean-going ships as distinct from shore whaling [(whalers')]

>  <b>TERRY</b> [Whaling] is carried on in two ways - either by vessels that remain absent from their port for a certain period, which is termed `deep sea fishing', or by establishments in the large bays ...denominated `shore-fishing'. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),241</i>)

### deep sea perch
 
 <i>n.</i> Orange roughy [<i>Hoplostethus atlanticus</i>]

>  [species name] orange roughy .. [scientific name] <i>Hoplostethus atlanticus </i>other name(s)] deepsea perch <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### deep sea spotty
 
 <i>n.</i> scarlet parrotfish

>  <b>FISHER</b> A number of Parrotfish (they do not live in schools) were following me around and picking up the broken off crayfish legs. The Banded Parrotfish is the most common in the shallows - it blends in with the seaweed - but deeper down the Sandaggers Parrotfish and the Scarlet Parrotfish, often called the `deep sea spotty', take over. <i>(1977,Angels Wear Black,37</i>)

### deep sinking
 
 <i>n.</i> the mining of deep ground or deep leads.

>  The deep sinking has not realised the sanguine expectations once entertained regarding it. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,18</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> The greatest drawback with beach diggins is, they are so quickly washed out. Unless they find gold in the Ranges, and have deep sinking, there is very little prospect of the diggers now at the Coast remaining long. <i>(Jan 2, 1866,My Dear Bannie,100,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> DEEP SINKING: putting down a shaft to tap a deep lead. No absolute figure can be given but `deep ground' usually required shafts if over sixty feet. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  The Australian diggers soon found out, that hitherto only the superficial deposits had been worked, and that they had not yet come to the `bottom', where ... the richest treasure was to be expected. They began `deep sinkings', and after having dug to a depth of about 130 feet ... struck a second gold-bed. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),113</i>)

### Deep South
 
 <i>n.</i> usu. Otago and Southland. Contrast Far North.

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Mightn' mean anything but he comes from the deep south. Dunedin. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,150</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> He was a South Islander from the deep South and he rolled his r's. <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,67</i>)

>  Gore was an unexpected eye-opener...; make sure this town is on your agenda when touring the Deep South. <i>(2009,NZ Memories,77/2</i>)

### deep thinker
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of later reinforcements. Contrast debt-dodger. [(WW2)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Puzzled, we hear of `Deep Thinkers'. A member of the 5th Reinforcements, nearing the last of the volunteer New Zealand soldiers because `the rush to the colours' in this war was much more subdued, was heard to observe: `Yes, I must admit I did some deep thinking before joining up.' From then to the end of World War Two, the 5th Reinforcements were known as the `Deep Thinkers'. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,260</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Deep Thinkers Fifth Reinforcements, 2NZEF, WWII, from one saying: `I did some deep thinking before deciding to join up.' [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,35</i>)

### deep-sea end
 
 <i>n.</i> the hold; to work in the deep-sea end, to work in the hold. [(watersiders') ca1949 Robert Lamb, Wellington]

### deep-sea trevally
 
 <i>n.</i> [Otago]

>  <b>WILSON</b> DEEP-SEA TREVALLY A name once used for Bream (<i>Hyperoglyphe porosa</i>) when they were common around Otago. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,60</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE-NOSE WAREHOU Hyperoglyphe antarctica</i> (Blue-nose grouper, griffins silverfish, bonita, deepsea trevalla) ... [302] They are caught regularly on hapuku long lines along with hapuku and bass and are sold as hapuku. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,301</i>)

### Deepfreeze
 
 <i>n.</i> [The operational name for the original 1955-56 summer pre-IGY US Navy Expedition to the Ross Sea and Antarctica, subsequently applied to later expeditions and, with the establishment of a Deepfreeze Base in Christchurch, applied in NZ to any US Navy or US Science Foundation operations or personnel associated with the Antarctic.]

>  There was a woman in Hornby, who only went for Yanks; she had three kids by different American fathers. Every spring Operation Deepfreeze servicemen flew in from Hawaii and Guam, and with each intake there was a new one to be crazy about; the one to take her away from all this, the flat dusty suburbs, the mean benefit and snooping social worker calls. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  The [American] Ambassador said thesurface unit component of Task Force 43,which would carry on "ExpeditionDeepfreeze" would include two Navyicebreakers <i>(Sep 19, 1955,Dominion,8</i>)

>  There were rumours in Christchurch beforethe arrival of "Deepfreeze" that anunderstanding had been reached betweenthe New Zealand and American authorities... All this.. would not have been necessaryhad someone in authority had theforesight to issue a directive to theChristchurch and Lyttelton police aboutprocedure in the event of any of thepersonnel of "Operation Deepfreeze"getting into trouble.. in New Zealand.about maintaining shore discipline. <i>(Dec 14, 1955,NZ Truth,15</i>)

>  Operation Deepfreeze was planned in twostages. Deepfreeze I in the summer of1955-56 was to establish an airfield atMcMurdo Sound capable of taking the heavyaircraft that would ferry supplies to theSouth Pole base which was planned for thefollowing year... Deepfreeze II in 1956-57 was to see a permanent station builtat the South Pole. <i>(1985,Antarctica: great stories,272</i>)

>  Unfortunately the New Zealand Post Officeor Deep Freeze took it into their headsto collect the mail and send it on in afew massive loads instead of the usualdribbles on every plane leaving for theice. <i>(1971,Penguin Summer,115</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Deep-Freeze Moves Afoot...Plans are afoot to move some of theUnited States Deep Freeze Operations fromHarewood base. <i>(Nov 10, 1987,Evening Post,00</i>)

>  [Hdg] Police Not Told, How to Dealwith "Deepfreeze" Offenders. <i>(Dec 14, 1955,NZ Truth,15</i>)

>  <b>MacFARLANE</b> Was this topic traversed when you went tothe Deep Freeze briefing? <i>(1991,Erebus Papers,113</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from 1981]

>  <b>YOUNG</b> We promptly put them to work unloadingall the packs and sledge boxes from thecar and carting them in to the DeepFreeze desk... I had always loathed putting "housewife"; it sounded so middle-aged and staid.Besides, it would look quite daft on aDeep Freeze form. <i>(1971,Penguin Summer,29</i>)

>  Operation Deep Freeze base commander RonChrans said yesterday the pilot had toldAmerican controllers at McMurdo he wasmaintaining contact with Auckland airtraffic control. <i>(Nov 30, 1979,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>HICKSON</b> Mike Hatcher was in a Deep Freezeclubroom catching up on news from the icefrom some fellow Americans who had justreturned.<i> Ibid. </i> The first message from McMurdo to Deep Freeze Headquarters in Christchurch described the crash site as.. on thenorth side of Mount Erebus. <i>(1980,Flight 901 to Erebus,36</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a member of Deepfreeze Expeditions

 <i>n.</i> [[Get cits.]]

### deer farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who breeds and grazes deer for velvet and/or venison.

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high fence of a prescribed height to contain deer, made with deer netting, high posts and netting gates.

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) high fences used to enclose paddocks running commercially farmed deer.

### deer puller
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These crosses [sc. greyhound-collie/terrier, etc], sometimes euphemistically called lurchers, areexcellent deer-pullers. ... Only with great lu ck and fitness... can the hunter be in on the pull with the knife. On the whole it is better not to have dogs so fast as to be able to pull deer unless you are going in for that line exclusively <i>(Dec 1951,Here & Now,15</i>)

### deer shooting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We called it deer shooting, not stalking, and we were right. Stories of the difficulty of getting deer leave me cold. Stalking - trying to get a good head or one particular stag - may be difficult, but shooting deer when they are fairly numerous, as they are on many stations, is child's play. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,62</i>)

### deer track
 
 <i>n.</i> [(deer shooters')]

>  In recent years there seems to have been a tendency to use both track and trail and to differentiate between them. A trail seems to be something narrow and perhaps rather hardto follow - a way marked only by blazes or worn by animals, usually deer. A track seems to be something broader, cut or formed by man. ... A well-known older Club member made this distinction when, on hearing him speak of a deer trail, I asked him what he thought to be the difference between track and trail. I remarked that I thought it a very recent distinction, one not occurring in the Club until about 1957. He disagreed, believing it to be one he had always known. Yet, on looking through The Tramper, I could not find deer trail used until 1944, but deer track occurs from the first number in 1928 onwards, and he himself had used it in 1940. It remains to be seen whether this distinction will become established. In the meantime deer track still seems to be used far more than deer trail. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,25</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### deer-culler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hunter of feral deer.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> This country now had deer-cullers with hot barrels ... <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,23</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> And when he was satisfied he could do no more, ... he went deer-culling for a year. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Paua Gatherers)]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the shooting of feral deer by Government-employed deer cullers.

### deferred payment
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. NZ use] the payment for land in instalments, 19th cent. [.. a legal phrase. `Land on deferred payment'; `Deferred payment settler'; `Pastoral deferred payment.' These expressions in New Zealand have reference to the mode of statutory alienation of crown lands, known in other colonies as conditional sale, etc., i.e. sale on time payment, with conditions binding the settler to erect improvements, ending in his acquiring the free-simple. The system is obsolete, but many titles are still incomplete.]

>  Both the aim and the object of those who frame laws in favor of special settlements, free selectors, deferred payment, and small farm associations are admirable .. the example set by the Southland deferred payment selectors will no doubt open the eyes of our lgislators [sic] to some of the dangers of the present system. <i>(May 1, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  An industrious and steady man of experience could start on a 100 acre deferred payment bush section. <i>(Jul 1887,NZ Farmer,196</i>)

>  .. before the Roads are better; and Road Board income is less than half what it was in the good times of high-priced deferred payment land, of large Government subsidies and before the capitalisation system had reduced income from deferred payment settlers to less than half its previous amount. <i>(May 26, 1888,Egmont Star,2</i>)

>  <b>ROBSON</b> The first block of land thrown open on the deferred payment system was the Toi Tois block, consisting of 5,000 acres. <i>(1967,Bits and Pieces,68</i>)

### delayed telegram
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a cheap-rate telegram, cf. letter-telegram, tannergram (see Morris).

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> ONE-SPOT PULLER <i>Chromis hypsilepis</i> (Single-spot demoiselle, brown puller) ... [238] There is ...a single white spot on each side of the back ...One-spot pullers are common off south-eastern Australia ... and are now regularly seen off the north-east coast of the North Island <i>(1982,Collins Guide,237,1984 ed.</i>)

### demolition party
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf house-trashing.]

>  Recent cases of `demolition parties' in the city have prompted landlords to call for more legal safeguards against malicious damage. <i>(Oct 18, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),A6</i>)

### demon
 
 <i>n.</i> Often in pl. the demons. A policeman, esp. a detective; =dee n2.; dinnies demons

>  [Bloke's patter] ... And a `Demon' is a `Nark.' <i>(Mar 31, 1906,NZ Truth,41/6/11</i>)

>  According to a well-known Christchurch demon, the slang of three months in thieves' argot is `a drag'. `Critic' thought a 'tec was the `drag' when he pinched 'em <i>(Aug 1, 1908,NZ Truth,163/1/5</i>)

>  The brand new rank was to be known as `Detective-Sergeant,' ... The `demon' upon whom this overwhelming honour was bestowed, was to receive the enormous increment of 6d per day house allowance, over and above common of garden `D's,' ... <i>(Jun 17, 1911,NZ Truth,312/5/8</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Demon. 3. Cf. the C. 20 Australian and New Zealand c. or low sense (rarely in singular): a detective. Cf.: <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,214</i>)

>  When they got to the station a big demon walked into Joe's cell with his coat off. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,16</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> (Usually pl.) A policeman: Australian: since ca. 1880: 1889, B and L, `The demons put pincher on me', `I was apprehended'; 1891, F and H; ob. They are `the very devil' - from the criminal's point of view. Hence, a detective: New Zealand: 1932, Nelson Baylis (private letter); by 1935, also Australian; 1941 Kylie Tennant, The Battlers, `Demons' or plain-clothes detectives'; 1942, Baker; extant. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,183,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I told a bloody demon, though he didn't understand, ... <i>(1960,Collected Poems,215,1979 ed.</i>)

>  Talk of `demons' and `jokers' being `hooked' ... proved too much for Mr L.G.M. Sinclair, S.M., in Auckland Court. Te Atatu mussel curer, Benjamin Patrick Nathan, 28, explained. `Demons', he said, were detectives. ... <i>(Jul 30, 1963,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> `I had them demons really worried about you.' <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,96</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> `What sort of questions did the demons ask you?' `The demons?' `The police,' said Mervyn. `The bulls'. <i>(1974,Predicament,36,1981 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [1930s-40]]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Bloody demon all right, plainclothes. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,36</i>)

>  The police, they are just doing their job; ... But it's all cool now. The demons they came in the pub and apologised for coming up to me all the time. <i>(Dec 4, 1981,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> DEMON (n) Detective. In the sense of an ordinary policeman, Australian since about 1880. As a detective, a New Zealand specialist usage since about 1932. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

>  <b>NORGROVE</b> `The police!' `Yeah, he wanted to get the demons in on the act.' <i>(1987,Shoestring Sailors,14</i>)

### demonstrator/demo
 
 <i>n.</i> A vehicle or other object used as a demonstration model. [Peter Whiteford suggests that the usual term for this is "demos" leading to "ex-demos". The word does not appear in dictionaries in this sense until American Heritage 2. A product, such as an automobile, used for demonstration and often later sold at a discount and 3rd Macquarie 3. One of a line of products used to demonstrate the qualities of the line. Oxford and earlier US and other dictionaries tend to use demo for (1) a political demonstration and (2) a demonstration tape or recording.]

 <i>n.</i>

### dempsey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This would read "<b>dempsey</b> <i> n.</i> 1. an outrageous bluff designed to explain the unexplainable. 2. an own goal. 3. a bizarre suicide attempt assisted by legal advice. 4. an egregious joke.<i> v.t.</i> minimise the effects of an appalling error of judgment ((<i>That nuclear bomb we just dropped on Paris, I had no idea these things could do so much damage.</i>) (f. early 21st-century soccer administrator with too many lawyers and an irrational fear of fax messages)"...Charlie would simply stand his ground, and take the custard pies while reducing the nmedia to fits of laughter with a steady stream of prepared dempsies. For example, in explaining the millions of dollars being tossed away through public servants living in Auckland and flying to work in Wellington, the likely dempsey would be :"It prevents their being delayed on the northern motorway." <i>(Jul 15, 2000,Dominion,20</i>)

### den to den
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game [(children's game) 1952 June 7 R. Mason letter (Wellington) Den To Den 1920 Pukekohe School A game played by the girls at Pukekohe School. The seats along the wall of the school and the seats on the opposite side of the narrow playground are dens. The players run across from den to den whenever they see a good chance to avoid being caught by whoever is `it'. A player who is caught then becomes `it' immediately.]

### dental clinic
 
 <i>n.</i> a state centre for the dental treatment of school children; shortened form of school dental clinic, the building (or room) usu. in a primary school grounds set aside for the treatment of first teeth by a dental nurse under the schools dental programme

>  A survey of Hamilton schoolchildrensuggests a visit to the dental clinic isno longer considered a trip to the"murder house". <i>(Nov 6, 1993,Dominion,11</i>)

>  The Education Estimates included GBP1000 with which to make start on a travelling dental clinic for country schools. <i>(Sep 18, 1917,Auckland  Star,8</i>)

>  In numbers of Auckland schools children from Standard 2 upwards will no longer receive dental care at the School Dental Clinics ... <i>(Dec 1952,Here & Now,30</i>)

>  <i>(1979,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Crouched under the lawsonianas by the Dental Clinic, a sense of loss ... seized me. <i>(1988,Rainshadow,61</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> She had an appointment that day at the school's "Murder House" - the dental clinic, with its treadle drill ably wielded by the dental nurse. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,24</i>)

>  The first [cruelty] has been there for truly as long as I can remember: a small, weatherboard shed, which you hope might be used to store a lawnmower, rakes, spades, axes, a wheelbarrow, perhaps some firewood or bags of coal, but in fact is the Omanu Primary School dental clinic... The bell rang at three o'clock - my worst memory of school is being called to the dental clinic one afternoon at five to three. <i>(Nov 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  ...you will see such trouts in boarding schools, dental clinics and Social Welfare offices the length of the country. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  The first dental clinic was established in Greytown on March 1st, 1928, the intention being to establish rooms in the schools at Featherston, Martinborough and Carterton, the nurse travelling to these schools in turn. <i>(1961,Carterton District Centennial Celebration,13</i>)

>  Hawera had been moving in the direction of having a dental clinic established, not necessarily in Hawera, which would serve a number of schools near the town. <i>(Dec 13, 1923,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  For the rest of the day there was a constant stream of children to and from the Dental Clinic. <i>(May 15, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,3</i>)

>  The teacher brings his class to the dental clinic - a suite of specially fitted up rooms in one of the central school [sic] . <i>(Nov 28, 1904,Manawatu Times,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

There is hereby established a department of State to be known as the Department of Conservation, which shall be under the control of the Minister. <i>(1987,NZ Statutes,65/260</i>)

### depasturage licence
 
 <i>n.</i> the right, by payment of a fee, to graze stock on specified Crown land; = depasturing licence

>  When the selection [of the N.Z. Company share of the Otago Block] had been made the Crowngrant was to be issued, and there was an understanding that if Colonel Wakefield desired stillmore land he was to have it. This was the origin of what was afterwards known as thedepasturage licences, under which settlers were authorised to run cattle and sheep in the adjoining districts. <i>Ibid.</i> 138-139 Under the original terms, land purchasers were entitled upon payment of an annual license fee of 10s. 6d. to depasture or run sheep and cattle upon such waste lands within the block as remained unappropriated. ... though Captain Cargill was then gazetted Commissioner of Crown Lands for the Otago block only, he received no definite instructions upon the subject of granting the runs. [139] The result was that he merely filed the applications for depasturage licenses as they were presented to him, whilst the applicants were left to pursue their further course as seemed best to them. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,59</i>)

### depasturage licensing system
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Much of the country [in Otago] had been taken up as runs under the depasturage licensing system, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,149</i>)

### depasture
 
 <i>v.</i> to put sheep or stock to pasture.

>  ... adapted for the depasturing of sheep. <i>(Jul 1844,Nelson Examiner,235</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)1844 Jul D.Monro in Nelson Examiner in Hocken Contributions 235]

>  WANTED TO PURCHASE, 500 SCABBY SHEEP, to depasture at Rhodes Bay. <i>(Jul 19, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  ... certain parties have been Pig-Hunting on my run, between Mount Pleasant and Godley Heads - by which dogs my sheep there depasturing, have been scattered and several killed; ... <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  Farmers depasturing sheep near the confines of the borough are suffering severe losses by the ravages of dogs. <i>(May 7, 1932,Oamaru Mail,6</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> [Reproduction of official license application.] Application for Depasturing License... I hereby apply for a License to Depasture Stock upon the Land hereinafter described, subject to the 'Rules and Regulations for the issue of Licenses for the Occupation of Waste Lands of the Crown'. <i>(1856,Blue Cliffs,4</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [1898 citation is of noun depasturing]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The rock here is a dry crumbling basalt, so that both the herbage and the natureof the ground are admirably suited for the depasturing of sheep. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,235</i>)

>  A month later [April 1855] a Christchurch paper reported the discovery of "a plain of immense extent capable of depasturing sheep" - behind the mountains in which Mackenzie had been captured. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(10); 4</i>)

>  Four horses were depasturing in the immediate vicinity. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  Mr. J.D. Enys.. while depasturing his flocks, gathered wild flowers on the mountains. <i>(Apr 27, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to stock land.

>  Wanted to purchase, 500 scabby sheep, to depasture at Rhodes Bay. <i>(Jul 19, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>C W RICHMOND</b> We have just advised the Governor to assent to the new land regulations of the province under which pasturage leases will be granted for 14 years at a yearly rate of 1d. per head on the sheep depastured. <i>(Jan 29, 1857,Letter,257</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>POTTS</b> Years ago, on the Rakaia, the writer depastured a large herd of breeding cattle. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,47</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> A.T. Clark depastured his sheep nearby. <i>(1944,Kelly of Inverkelly,85</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lease licence for stock to be grazed and occupied on unused land [1800s.]

Dated at Dunedin this 4th day of November, one thousand eight hundred and fifty six. ...' [Also frontispiece to 1984 Kerse (ed) The Knapdale Run] <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,88</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Nov 4 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 88]

>  <b>WHEELER</b> A race for the Lands Office would follow, there to fill in a `Depasturing License'. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,1</i>)

### depatch
 
 <i>v.</i> to expel from a gang by removing the identifying membership patch. - [(gang jargon)]

>  `If we find the ... people responsible, we will `depatch' [expel them from the gang] [sic] them and ensure they give themselves up to the police,' Mr Mamfredos said. <i>(Dec 16, 1986,Dominion,1</i>)

### deposited plan
 
 <i>n.</i> In modern times subdivisions of urban and rural land are defined in terms of a surveyor's plan lodged in the office of the currently appropriate administrative entity (Lands and Survey, DOSLI etc) and known as a <i>deposited plan (DP)</i>. [Jim Cameron writes: Individual properties are <i>lots</i> on such a plan and so described on the title. Eg "[20 perches] more or less being part of section 32 Karori District and being Lot 4 on Deposited Plan 8018..." Note pre-metric NZ use of <i>perch</i> to define area. NSOD describes this use as "more fully, square perch ... now rare exc. hist". Until 1968 perch (never square perch) was standard in legal and popular use to describe the area of sections smaller than a quarter-acre.]

### derision
 
 <i>n.</i> sleety bad weather; drizzle. Compare murk. [(Wellington trampers') ca1940 R. Mason Taraura Tramping Club, Wellington Usually stronger than `muck': hail, heavy cold rain, wind-driven rain generally holds some idea that human beings are at the mercy of the elements.]

### derris dust
 
 <i>n.</i>

### derry
 
 <i>n.</i> to have (occas. get) a derry on, to have a strong dislike for or aversion to.

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> They hadn't such a derry on the bloke upon the track. <i>Ibid.</i> 58 I ain't down on them if they aint got a derry on me. [1945 Glossary Derry - another word for dislike or aversion.] <i>(1897,Station Ballads,30</i>) [NOTES: (The Swagger)]

>  Now, I think if you leave England alone, and just look after your own country, you will find plenty of matter in it to fill your paper, including immorality. You seem to have a derry on the Home people. <i>(Jun 1, 1912,NZ Truth,362/7/4</i>)

>  derry n. sl. prejudice.-to have a derry on to be prejudiced against, have a `down' on (playfulallusion to hey derry down derry,' as refrain of old songs). <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Another familiar variant is `to have a derry on', derry being a substitute for down, apparently from the association of the two words in the refrain `derry down', `to have a derry on blowflies' is to have a strong dislike of them. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> [She] had had a derry on that crowd ever since Hilda took her to an evening service. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,63</i>)

>  derry, to have a v. have an aversion to. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  derry n. NZ & Austral. have a derry on hold in disfavour. [song refrain derry down, cf. DOWN] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,201</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

At time a tuber of thevariety is to be obtained, but it is apoor apology for the mealy, fullflavoured sort that was so abundantduring the year[s] 1864 to 1885. ### Desert Gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### desert sore
 
 <i>n.</i> [(WW2)]

>  <b>LATHAM</b> The juice was necessary for the troops from the desert whose `desert sores' were a form of scurvy caused by lack of fresh fruit and veges. <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (fig.) A medal (ribbon) (?Africa Star)?; ??a wound stripe.

### destock
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  LINZ is failing to protect the public interest, It allows grazing to continue of tussockland and shrublands which should be destocked... <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

### destocking
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED has this but only in the sense of a grocer destocking his shelves. The Australian and S African dictionaries do not have it but Webster's Third has it as African in the senses of removing livestock from an (overgrazed) range or pasture. (second and third citations belong with the verb to destock)]

>  [His] farm is carrying only half its usual number of ewe hoggets... Further destocking will be necessary if there is no respite next month. <i>(Jan 28, 1999,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Farmers have been de-stocking since September, shedding animals they calculated were unsustainable before Christmas. <i>(Jan 31, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  Farmers have been more responsive this time and have been de-stocking, and a lot of cattle have been moved. <i>(Oct 29, 1999,Dominion,11</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the removal or sale of stock from a farm property [often as result of drought or flooding.]

>  .. if it is a dryland property, destocking in the summer months is a typical strategy. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Aug/Sept 43</i>)

 (the right to develop land commercially) <i>n.</i>
