However, Miss Clark said officials' advice was that "a development right is not a customary right". <i>(Dec 12, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### deviation
 
 <i>n.</i> a road (or rail) detour, now commonly used of any (esp. new) formation which deviates from an old route. [ca1952 July 27 R. Mason reports: A speaker at the Tararua Tramping Club meeting said: `That was when the deviation was put in on Middle Ridge.']

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> [County Council minutes] G. Pott was authorised to ... lay off a cutting and procure estimates for a deviation north of the Mimi Bridge. <i>(1989,Clifton,15</i>) [NOTES: 1885 Mar 1 in 1989 Wright Clifton 15]

>  <b>DOUGLAS</b> ... but the new deviation will be beyond the funds of the county council, as I expect ... <i>(Jan 30, 1894,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Jan 30 Douglas Letter to Roberts in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 209]

>  Dryden was acting under the instructions of Mr Furkert, the engineer in charge of a gang of men engaged on `The Deviation' on the Waiouru section of the North Island Main Trunk line. <i>(Oct 7, 1911,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  Connection of the Wilson deviation to the main railway line ... <i>(Jul 3, 1952,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  CONSTRUCTION of the South Rangitikei railway bridge, north of Palmerston North, is getting close to completion. The 300-metre bridge is the longest of three on the nine kilometre Mangaweka deviation, ... The deviation is being constructed to eliminate a steep winding section of the North Island Main Trunk. When the project is completed late next year the new deviationwill replace the old Mangaweka Viaduct, ... <i>(Dec 29, 1980,Evening Post,28/1-2</i>)

### devil
 
 <i>n.</i> often pl., a European or settler not considered favourable to missionary interests [(hist)]

>  ...the natives were quite surprised to see so many white men going to Mass when none go to Turner's preaching - men to whom the missionaries gave the name of devils." (Turner was a Protestant missionary.) <i>(Jun 2, 2002,NZ Catholic,5</i>) [NOTES: Written by Thomas Poynton about 1838]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Of great numbers of our countrymen I truly say that they are the vilest of the vile; and such Europeans are distinguished by the name of `devils' by those natives who know them.' <i>(Apr 8, 1839,Matthews of Kaitaia,121</i>)

>  (Letter from E.J. Wakefield) ... the rangatira missionary will come out along with the devils, as the missionaries have kindly christened every European not of their own cloth. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/202</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Turoa made another speech at me; saying that he wanted the devils, and not the missionaries, who had told him lies, on his land ... [ibid Mar31] ... what was the meaning of the word Devilwhich [the missionaries] had chosen to apply to all pakehas but themselves. <i>(Mar 29, 1840,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 29 (1839) E.J. Wakefield MS Diary]

>  To those whom Mr.Coates terms `unprincipled colonists', the natives are taught to give thename of `devils'. <i>(May 25, 1840,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1840 May 25 (Port Nicholson) Joseph Somes to R.Vernon Smith in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 143]



 
 <i>n.</i> a small cake

>  devil n. a small cake of flour and water baked on hot ashes; ... <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a Maori unconverted to Christianity.

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> A Dunedinite, Michael Abbott, is pitting himself against the worst that the wilds can throw at him ... It sounds rather curious to hear a native, in answer to a question as to whether he is a missionary or not, reply quite coolly, `No, I'm a devil!' <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,44,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JAMIESON</b> The unconverted tribes of the interior [of the North Island] receive the appellation of devils. <i>(1841,New Zealand, S. Aust. and NSW,262</i>)

>  All the Mauries about there were Missionaries ... my party ... were all `Devils' (a term to be explained thereafter) 1841 May 30 <i>Ibid </i>319 In fact Hospitality is the universal attribute of all white men `Devils' (a term used to distinguish them from the members of the Mission) residing in New Zealand, ? <i>(Apr 3, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,284,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> An old native, from whose ear a large shark's tooth was hanging as an ornament, on being questioned by me as to the danger to which they were exposed while in the water from those animals, replied, that `the missionaries had told him that the converts need be under no apprehension of danger from them, but that the `dewaras' (devils) or unbelievers would certainly be devoured.' ... On being answered in the negative, he appeared satisfied; at the same timecomplaining, that the missionaries did not treat him with due respect, but called him a `devil,' because he would not be converted. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),53</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Those natives who are still heathen are generally styled `devils' by the Christianized people; and they themselves adhere to the [153] appellation. Hongi Hongi, ... in answer to my inquiry as to whether he was a `mihorari' or a `pikopo'(Catholic), confessed with evident delight that he was a `devil'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/152-153</i>)

 <i>n.</i> spurrey

### devil's darning needle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SALE</b> I surmise from their size and general colour on the wing that they are probably what somesimply call large dragonflies, or others name more colourfully Devil's Darning Needles, or Horse Stingers (or, in Scotland, Flying Adders). [92] ... My book on native insects says the `darning needle' nickname derives from a tradition that the dragonflies sew up the ears of delinquents. And that a Maori legend records a chief who dispatched an army of them (he would have calledthem kapokapowai) to destroy an enemy and all his habitations. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,91</i>)

### devil's dice
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIN</b> So when I wasn't reading any novels I could lay my hands on I was filling myself up with Gordon's Mining and Engineering and Miner's Guide and back numbers of the Mines Department's annual reports, the New Zealand Mines Record and all the rest of it. Before long I could have written you a dictionary from adit to winze and what I didn't know about dredges, hydraulic sluices, alluvial mines, lodes, faults, throws, heaves, slides, blocking out, stopes, dollies, devil's dice and drills could have gone on the back of a halfpenny stamp. <i>(1959,No Remittance,47</i>)

### devil's highway
 
 <i>n.</i>

### devil's missionary
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical, derogatory) a name applied to a pre-1840 European. Compare devil 1.

They really deserve a name which has been given them, `Devil's Missionaries.' <i>(1840,Information Relative to NZ,108</i>)

>  'Oh yes, I know it,' said Mr Ward, 'it is spurry, or better known perhaps as the Devilweed.' <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Hawera Star,7</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> There must have been about 100 acres of pasture on the steep hillside, with here and there a clump of bush and quite an area of devil-weed. >  <b>SALE</b> .. even the fluffy flowers of the Mexican devilweed have a glow ... <i>(1987,Country Diary,87</i>)

### diamond
 
 <i>n.</i> ellipt. for diamond-back(ed) flounder. (See [flounder](../F#flounder))

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea plebeia</i>; diamond, tinplate, dab, square; patiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,216</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER [<i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i>] ... Other names are dab, diamond, tinplate, and square. The Maori name patiki, originally generic, is now loosely used for most flounders. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,32</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a diamond-shaped stock yard with gates opening to four other yards and often to two races.

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Working in the confined space of the diamond, the young dog inclined to bite will quickly take advantage of the opportunity afforded. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,43</i>)

>  From the diamond, through the crush pens, the drafting race and the full length of the main race, all have been concreted. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> .. it is a good idea afterwards to let fifteen to twenty-five sheep at a time into the pen, preferably the diamond or race-filling pen. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,152</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> Mr Williams was most fastidious when culling his two-tooth ewes. We would have to put seven in the diamond at a time, while he stood in the yard with a long thin manuka stick on which was tied a small bag of crushed raddle. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,36</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The race is empty, then so is the crush and if you haven't patched the gate, so is the diamond ... [diamond pen] <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,40</i>)

 <i>n.</i> turutu, inkberry

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> DEPTFORD PINK (<i>Dianthus armeria</i>) European biennial. ... Deptford pink grows in scattered places on stony, sandy or pumice soil, ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,49</i>)

### diarrhoea bags
 
 <i>n.</i> knickerbockers

>  <b>McGILL</b> diarrhoea bags knickerbockers; recalled in NZ Oral History Unit Martinborough study. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,35</i>)

### diaspora
 
 <i>n.</i> the overseas dispersion of emigrant New Zealanders

>  This is where the diaspora comes in. It is our insurance policy against isolation and Erewhonitis - and why it's possible to stop spooking ourselves about people leaving. <i>(Jan 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  <b>BRIAN EASTON</b> My guess the use may have come out of the Knowledge Wave Conference of 2001, precipitated by the parallel with the Irish Diaspora, which is thought to have contributed to the revival of the Irish economy. (The numbers however are very different400,000 NZers against 70m of Irish descent.) The Irish use may be recent too, perhaps as a result of Mary O'Regan(?) president of Ireland who made a major speech (about 1990 I think) in which she said that Ireland should reach out to its emigrants. Coming out of the KWC, there has been formed KEA (Kiwi Expatriates Association) to harness our diaspora for New Zealand's benefit. <i>(Dec 6, 2002,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: e-mail]

>  With 800,000 Kiwis already living outside New Zealand, the country has one of the largest diasporas of any country in the world for its size. <i>(Dec 14, 2002,Dominion Post,F1</i>)

### dibbly-dobbler
 
 <i>n.</i> a medium pace bowler in cricket [Also dibbly-dobblies below (bowlers or balls?)]

>  There was a need now to concentrate more on faster bowlers than the "dibbly-dobbly" medium-pacers that have been such a part of the Black Caps' approach. "Perhaps the days of the dibbly-dobbler are gone." <i>(Jan 31, 2001,Dominion,60</i>)

>  They were, to use the parlance of the game, "dibbly-dobblers", seemingly inoffensive New Zealand medium-pacers of an earlier age whose control and subtle variations rendered them ideal for the limited-overs game, especially in home conditions. <i>(Feb 24, 2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.stabroeknews.com/index.pl/article_sports?id=45116525Accessed 28 Feb 2006. Stabroek is a West Indian news paper found online.]

>  [heading] Dibbly-dobblies stymie S Africa <i>(Oct 23, 2005,Sunday Star Times,B8</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> (coarse slang) dick in child, a child molestation charge [ORIGIN: acronym]

They had to get him out in the fastest possible time.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],249,(1962-63 ed.</i>)

### dick
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Not keen on a coalition with either party, "Labour dicked them on the foreshore". <i>(Sep 26, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  "The next time they're shocking and getting dicked," says another...Harbour, despite Buck's exhortations, was, alas, "dicked" by Taranaki. <i>(Nov 8, 1998,Sunday Star Times,F11</i>)

>  We've been dicked again. <i>(Nov 12, 1998,Radio NZ / National Radio,Kim Hill Show</i>)

### dicken
 
 <i>exclam.</i> constr. (aw) dicken! or dicken on that!; an exclamation or interjection of disbelief, disgust, etc., never commonly used in NZ and now (1984) obs in many localities even in children's usage.

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Aw dikkon, Mr A!' said Wade. `What did you say?' `Haven't you heard that one, sir? I suppose it's New Zealand digger slang. `Dikkon'. It's the same as if you'd say `Come off it'. Used to hear it on the Peninsula. `Aw dikkon, dig'' [check cit and edn] <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,238</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> In constant use by our youngsters ... dickin! <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Ah, dicken! I know what makes things tick between you and Bert. He's a decent joker.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,58</i>)

>  dicken interj. exclamation expressing incredulity, dissent, prohibition, or rejection. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  dicken int. NZ & Austral. sl. (also dickin, dickon) come off it! cut it out! [foll.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,207</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dicken disgust or disbelief or surprised belief, in form of an interjection, largely replaced by `really!' or `really?'; an antipodean contraction of `the dickens' or `what the dickens!', being a euphemism for the devil. - dicken on that! go easy, will you!; eg `You want me to help you lift that dirty big post? Dicken on that, mate.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,36</i>)

>  '... It has not been restrained by any fear of the penalties that are provided for the offence known as 'contempt', but by a desire to assist in preserving the dignity of the court and in upholding its authority.' Dicken! <i>(Apr 1, 1911,NZ Truth,310/4/3</i>)

>  [Caption] "Say Kiwi, some coot told meyou were a wingless bird." "Dicken Yank,yer gotter have arms when there's a waron." <i>(Apr 10, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/41 : 101</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Dicken on that for a joke. <i>(1949,Roads from Home,237</i>)

>  The accused [, Harman,] said: `Murder ... dicken on that, me murder anyone.' <i>(Nov 6, 1950,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `What in hell are ya doin in Christchurch anyway? Bludgin on the [check] social security, I spose?' `No. Dicken on that.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,44</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> - dicken on that! go easy, will you!; eg `You want me to help you lift that dirty big post? Dicken on that, mate.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,36</i>)

### dickey
 
 <i>adj.</i> stupid.

>  <b>McGILL</b> dickey stupid; eg 'Well, I don't think Billy T's dickey at all, I think he's neat.' [Alison Gray] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,36</i>)

### didn't come down in the last shower
 
 <i>phr.</i> not be easily fooled

>  <b>McGILL</b> I didn't come down in the last shower I am not a fool; to which the reply is: `You mightn't have come down in the last shower, but you're pretty wet all the same.' NZA variant of Royal Navy's `I didn't come down in the last bucket.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,60</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>shower </i>, phr. <i>I didn't come down in the last shower </i> claim to be not gullible, but shrewder than given credit for. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,102</i>)

### didymo
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Didymosphenia geminata</i> [Internet search Feb 2006: also used N Amer (Missouri), Austral] (See [rock snot](../R#rock-snot))

>  In 2004 didymo was discovered in New Zealand, the first time it was found in the southern hemisphere. To restrict its spread, the whole of the South Island of New Zealand was declared a controlled area in December 2005. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didymosphenia_geminataAccessed 10/02/06]

>  [advert] We need you to knock the snot out of Didymo. Didymo is an invasive Northern Hemisphere algae that threatens many of New Zealand's rivers. <i>(Dec 14, 2005,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  The biosecurity crises of didymo and seasquirt are an abrupt reminder that all our activities are conducted within the context of a natural world that has no innate repect for our interests. <i>(Oct 27, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Biosecurity's allocation has fallen by $21 million to $151 million, due to spending in the year 2005-06 year being lifted by "one-off' funding for all responses to disease - namely fall webworm, painted apple moth, didymo, sea squuirt and the southern saltmarsh mosquito. <i>(May 31, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

### die
 
 <i>v.</i> Usu. die well, die badly etc., of stock killed for meat, to provide a high (etc.) quality carcase. Compare kill.

>  <b>PERRY</b> They [sc. Southdown sheep] die well, i.e., have a small percentage of offal, and the bonesbeing small there is little waste. <i>Ibid.</i> The Ryeland kills well, and produces meat of a fairly good quality, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,56</i>)

### die-off
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a winter death of possums.

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> I'm certain that big die-offs occur in harsh winters. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,13</i>)

### died in the hole
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shearing] having not quite reached one's tally.

>  <b>PETER</b> Died in the hole: shearing term: not quite reaching your expected tally.i.e. 99, 199, 299. One short. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,6</i>)

### Dieffenbach's rail
 
 <i>n.</i> [(?obs)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> `Dieffenbach's Rail. . . . This beautiful Rail was brought from the Chatham Islands by Dr. Dieffenbach in 1842, and named by Mr. Gray in compliment to this enterprising naturalist. The adult specimen in the British Museum, from which my description was taken, is unique, and seems likely to remain so.' <i>(1988,Birds of New Zealand,121</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The forest is enlivened by numerous tuis or mocking-birds; a little green parroquet flocks in hundreds to the potato-fields ... The mako-mako, the finest songster in New Zealand, is also found here, being another species of the honey-eater. The large New Zealand pigeon, called `kukupa', finds plentiful food here ... A new kind of rail was formerly very common; ... <i>(1840,Journal of Royal Geog. Society,11/195-215</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Dieffenbach An Account of the Chatham Islands Journal of the Royal Geographical Society 11 pp195-215 in 1984 Holmes Chatham Islands 95]

>  <b>BULLER</b> RALLUS DIEFFENBACHII. (DIEFFENBACH'S RAIL.) ... <i>Native name</i>. Moeriki. ... [180] This beautiful Rail was brought from the Chatham Islands by Dr. Dieffenbach in 1842 ... [1888 edn, 2/12, places this specimen in Hutton's <i>Cabalus modestus</i> as Hutton's Rail.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,179,1888 2nd edn 2/123 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> <i>Note</i>: Dieffenbach's Rail (<i>Rallus dieffenbachi</i>), once abundant in the Chathams Islands, was similar to the Banded Rail in so many respects that that it may be regarded as a strong sub-species of <i>philippensis</i>. The last specimen was obtained in 1840. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,105</i>)

>  131.b. <i>Rallus philippensis dieffenbachii</i> Gray, 1843 <i>Dieffenbach's Rail</i> Chatham Island, extinct (one specimen, 1840). <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,41,1980 ed.</i>)

>  The second wave [of extinction] occurred gradually over centuries as the native crow, owlet nightjar, giant coot and Dieffenbach's rail were lost. <i>(May 2003,Forest & Bird,No. 308/2</i>)

### Diehards
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There used to be much more of the push element around Christchurch than there is now, and it frequently occurred that pushes [i.e. youths belonging to these groups] like the `Tigers,' the`Diehards,' the `Scalpers,' and the `Skull-Draggers,' faced the music before the beak. <i>(Feb 3, 1912,NZ Truth,345/7/3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAITE</b> By 4.30 a.m. on December 19 [1915], the last beetle cleared from the shore leaving the `Diehards' of the Division, only 3,000 strong, to hold the line against a mighty army. ... [286] The `last ditchers' were to be sacrificed for the army. There was no lack of volunteers. ... men of Anzac and Suvla alike - vied with each other in an endeavour to become included in the `Diehards'. [288] Previous parties had taken the `Diehards' kits and impedimenta. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,285</i>)

### dier
 
 <i>n.</i> an old sheep on the point of death [(farmers')]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This number pretty well accounted for all the old "diers" - sheep that would not have seen another winter out. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal in poor condition.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Dog tucker and mutton on a place that size will add up to 300 or more sheep and this number pretty well accounted for all the old 'diers' - sheep that would not have seen another winter out. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,48</i>)

>  a sick sheep: a dier. a sick cow: a dier. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)
